L4: ## CLINICAL REVIEW
L5: Application Type	NDA
Application Number(s)	212839
Priority or Standard	Standard
Submit Date(s)	11/21/2018
Received Date(s)	11/21/18
PDUFA Goal Date	11/21/19
Division/Office	DN2/ Office of Neuroscience
Reviewer Name(s)	Steven Dinsmore, DO
Review Completion Date	10/15/19
Established/Proper Name	YKP3089
(Proposed) Trade Name	XCO PRI
Applicant	SK Life Science, Inc.
Dosage Form(s)	Tablet 12.5mg, 25mg, 50mg, 100mg, 150mg, 200mg
Applicant Proposed Dosing Regimen(s)	initial dose of XCO PRI is 12.5 mg once daily for two weeks; followed by 25 mg once daily for two weeks; followed by 50 mg once daily for two weeks. Increase the dose in bi-weekly increments by no more than 50 mg once daily to a recommended maintenance dose of (b) (4) mg once daily. Maximum daily dose is 400 mg
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	indicated for the treatment of partial-onset seizures in adult patients.
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	XCO PRI is indicated for the treatment of partial-onset seizures in adult patients.

L24: ## 1. Executive Summary
L25: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L26: Cenobamate is a novel tetrazole derived compound with one chiral center.
Chemical Name: [(1R)-1-(2-Chlorophenyl)-2-(tetrazol-2-yl)ethyl] carbamate]
Molecular Formula: C_{10}H_{10}ClN_5O_2
Molecular weight is 267.67 g/mol.
L27: Figure 1 Cenobamate structure1
L28: Cenobamate drug substance was originally discovered by SK Biopharmaceuticals Co., Ltd. and was given a laboratory code, YKP3089. The name cenobamate was adopted as an INN and both terms are used interchangeably throughout the New Drug Application (NDA). Cenobamate has been shown to reduce repetitive neuronal firing by enhancing the fast and slow inactivation of sodium channels and by inhibiting the persistent component of the sodium current. It is also a positive allosteric modulator of 6 subtypes of the \gamma-aminobutyric acid ion channel.
L29: Proposed Indication: Cenobamate is indicated for the treatment of partial-onset seizures in adult patients.
L30: Proposed Dosing: The recommended initial dose of XCO PRI is 12.5 mg once daily for two weeks; followed by 25 mg once daily for two weeks; followed by 50 mg once daily for two weeks. Increase the dose in bi-weekly increments by no more than 50 mg once daily to a recommended maintenance dose of [REDACTED] (b)(4) mg once daily. Maximum daily dose is 400 mg.
L31: 1 eCTD Module 2.2 Introduction
L32: **Recommended Indication:** treatment of partial-onset seizures in adult patients
L33: **Recommended Dosing Information:** The recommended initial dosage of XCO PRI is 12.5 mg once daily for two weeks; followed by 25 mg once daily for two weeks; followed by 50 mg once daily for two weeks. Increase the dose in bi-weekly increments by no more than 50 mg once daily to a recommended maintenance dose of 200mg once daily. Based on individual patient response and tolerability escalation to a maximum daily dosage of 400 mg may provide additional benefit.
L34: A table of the dose regimen will also be included in the label to assist interpretation of the narrative titration schedule.
L35: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L36: The studies included in the NDA submission for cenobamate contain two adequate and well controlled clinical trials that meet the evidentiary standard for effectiveness. Both studies were double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group multicenter, multinational studies. These were studies C013 and C017.
L37: Study C013 was a two-arm study with 109 patients treated with placebo and 113 patients in the 200mg cenobamate treatment arm. There was significant separation between placebo and treatment with a 28-day median percent seizure reduction from baseline to treatment of 56% with an effect size of 34%. The p-value for comparing the medians of the two treatment groups was nominally smaller than 0.05.
L38: Study C017 was a 4-arm, multi-center, Phase 2 clinical study to determine the effective dose range of cenobamate as adjunctive therapy for the treatment of partial seizures. The study also aimed to evaluate the safety and tolerability of cenobamate in the partial epilepsy population. Approximately 400 subjects aged 18- 70 years (inclusive) were planned to be enrolled and randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio to receive cenobamate 400 mg once daily, cenobamate 200 mg once daily, cenobamate 100 mg once daily, or placebo. There were 106, 108, 109, and 111 patients in the placebo, 100mg, 200mg, and 400mg arms respectively. The observed medians of percent change from baseline in seizure frequency per 28 days were -55.3%, -55.2%, -36.3%, and -24.3% for the cenobamate 400 mg/day group, cenobamate 200 mg/day group, cenobamate 100 mg/day group, and placebo group, respectively. All three cenobamate dose groups were statistically significantly different from the placebo based on the rank ANCOVA model.
L39: The reviewer from Division of Biometrics 1 concludes that collective evidence from Study C013 and Study C017 indicates that YKP3089 is effective for the treatment of partial onset seizures in adult patients.
L40: ## 1.3. **Benefit-Risk Assessment**
L41: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L42: ## Benefit-Risk Integrated Assessment
L43: Epilepsy is a common condition that affects 1% of people worldwide where partial onset seizures (focal) are the most common subset. These seizures are a threat to the patient on any occurrence where loss of consciousness occurs. In some circumstances such an event may be fatal. High frequencies of seizure are also associated with an increased risk of sudden unexpected death in epilepsy (SUDEP). Frequent partial seizures may promote a propensity for progression of the seizure disorder through kindling. When an outcome is not a threat to life, all events are disruptive to quality of life. The proposed indication for cenobamate, partial onset seizures (focal), is directed at a population of patients where a medical need remains.
L44: There have been multiple antiepilepsy drugs added to the armamentarium for epilepsy treatment in the past 25 years. Despite these additions, approximately 30% of patients remain refractory to treatment. Although the number of available antiepileptic drug (AED) therapies has expanded, in clinical practice, patients who fail one AED are given a trial of an alternate drug to assess benefit. This is an ongoing process and it is unpredictable, on an individual basis who will benefit from a particular agent. There is a continuing need for more alternative AEDs.
L45: In this submission, two pivotal studies identified a reproducible robust separation of treatment from placebo. The endpoint of median percent change from baseline to treatment per 28 days revealed a -55.6 percent reduction from baseline at the 200mg dose in study C013. The percent change from baseline in study C017 treatment arms were -24.3, -33.6, -55.2 and -55.3 in the placebo, 100mg, 200mg and 400mg arms respectively. The dose response appears flat after 200mg but the 50% responder rate shows additional benefit of the 400mg treatment arm over the 200mg treatment arm. Neurologic adverse events were dose proportional. A dose of 200mg will be the recommended maintenance dose with escalation to a maximum dose of 400mg based on individual patient response and tolerability.
L46: The primary risk identified in cenobamate treatment was Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS). See Section 8.6.1., **DRESS Risk**. A DRESS case occurred in the TQT (thorough QT) healthy volunteer study that had a fatal outcome. Two additional cases of DRESS occurred, one in a healthy volunteer participating in a PK multiple ascending dose study and one in a patient participating in the open label extension of study C017. These latter two patients recovered but did require hospitalization and corticosteroid treatment. DRESS risk may be associated with the rate of dose escalation. A long-term open label safety study in epilepsy patients, study C021, initiated treatment with a reduced rate of dose escalation. No cases of DRESS were identified among 1339 subjects treated with YKP3089, including 1110 subjects treated with YKP3089 for at least 6 months where a lower starting dose (12.5 mg/day) and a slower titration rate (every 2 weeks) was used. This study sample size was chosen, such that with no new cases of DRESS, it would establish a 0.3% upper limit of the 95% confidence interval for the rate of DRESS with the modified titration rate. This conclusion has a favorable bias because the reduced titration rate was not tested in a
L47: randomized controlled trial and there are underlying differences between the population in study C021 and the populations from which the DRESS cases were identified. However, the study offers assurance that DRESS risk may be managed in the intended treatment population, with a reduced titration rate and vigilance for features of a hypersensitivity reaction.
L48: Additional risks identified were QTc shortening and neurologic adverse events as well as possible associations with hyperkalemia and appendicitis. This represents an uncertain threat. This threatening cardiac dysrhythmia was first identified in individuals with familial short QTc syndrome. Cenobamate will be contraindicated in these patients and drugs that are known to reduce QTc interval should be used with caution. This will be delineated in the label. Neurologic adverse events had a high frequency with cenobamate treatment. These adverse events are frequent in AED therapy. These may be monitored, will resolve with discontinuation and generally diminish with accommodation to the drug treatment. A trend of hyperkalemia was identified and will be included in labeling to alert prescribers, but no critical values were identified that were associated with adverse cardiac adverse events. The events of potassium elevated out of reference range to high value tended to be sporadic. This sporadic occurrence leads to uncertainty about causal association. Clinical chemistry monitoring will not be indicated. An adverse event of appendicitis, occurring at a rate higher than the background rate was identified in long term use of cenobamate. The causal relationship is unclear. This event will be added to the label to inform prescribers of the observation.
L49: The high-risk adverse event of DRESS may be managed by a slow dose escalation and informing patients of hypersensitivity features that require professional assessment. Although this is a threatening adverse event, it is also seen is a broad array of approved drug treatments across a variety of disease states, see Section 8.6.1 DRESS risk. The potential benefit of a new AED provides practitioners in the epilepsy community an additional option for treatment of this serious medical illness. The risk of DRESS, QTc shortening and neurologic adverse events are not disproportionate to the potential benefit in the patient population with this treatment indication.

L53: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
including somnolence and dizziness. These CNS adverse effects tended to abate as treatment was maintained. These adverse reactions may be monitored, and, if necessary, cenobamate may be discontinued
QTc Shortening presents an uncertain risk in the general population. Labeling will contraindicate patients with known QTc shortening syndrome and also caution against use with other medications that shorten the QT interval.
Appendicitis was observed at a higher rate than typical background frequency in the open label long term data. This will be presented in the labeling, although causal relationship to cenobamate is uncertain.
A shift to increased post baseline potassium values was observed. There were no critical outlier values in controlled study data. Labeling will contain an entry in "laboratory abnormalities" to alert practitioners to this observation.
L54: ## 2. Therapeutic Context
L55: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L56: Epilepsy is a life shortening disease of the brain. It may episodically be life threatening. This disorder affects approximately 1% of the worldwide population. Although repeated epileptic seizures are the clinical hallmark of epilepsy, the disease process (epileptogenesis) begins before the first seizure and may also lead to the progression of epilepsy after the onset of seizures. Epilepsy is diverse, with over 15 different seizure types and over 30 epilepsy syndromes, and it is associated with substantial comorbidity, including depression, anxiety and increased mortality. Approximately 70 to 80% of patients with new onset epilepsy may enter remission with current AEDs, However, 20 to 30% of patients remain refractory to available therapy.

L78: ## 4. Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L79: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)4
L80: Clinical site inspections are addressed in the Data Quality and Integrity sections of the Individual trials sections 6. No protocol deviations were of sufficient magnitude to impact the clinical study outcome.
L81: There was a procedural data capture issue that caused seizure diary data discrepancies. Data discrepancies were due to the inability of the eCRF system to accept daily seizure counts &gt;99. Based on this finding, an information request was sent to the sponsor asking whether any additional subjects had daily seizure counts &gt;99 during this study. The sponsor identified three subjects participating in Protocol YKP3089C017 with daily seizure counts &gt;99. The Biometrics reviewer integrated the corrected seizure counts into the statistical review, and the previous conclusion of efficacy was not altered.
L82: There was an issue of database lock and unlock in protocol C017. The database had been unlocked on four separate occasions after the database hard lock date. The sponsor and CRO inspections were conducted to evaluate the processes in place for database locking and unlocking and to investigate any changes made to the database after the hard lock date. As part of this process, audit trails were requested from the sponsor. Review of the audit trails focused on changes to the seizure diary data (the primary efficacy endpoint) and adverse events. Other than the removal of the duplicate seizure count (see below) in one subject, no other significant changes were noted.
L83: (b) (4) was responsible for the database hard lock and subsequent unlocks; both of which required sponsor approval. The sponsor had disclosed four dates of approvals provided for
L84: 4 From "Overall Assessment of Findings and Recommendations, Clinical Inspection Summary, Division of Clinical Compliance Evaluation, Office of Scientific Investigations.
L85: database unlocks involving seven subjects. One of these database changes involved the deletion of a seizure entered in both the double-blind and open-label phases of the study.
L86: The end of study visit for the double-blind phase (Visit 9) was also the same visit as the first visit for the open-label phase. The sponsor confirmed that seizures occurring on the last day of the double-blind period were not counted in the double-blind phase but rather were included in the open label phase. A review of all seizures occurring on the last day of the double-blind phase was performed in order to evaluate whether not counting seizures occurring on the last day of the double-blind phase in that phase of the study introduced any potential bias. This review confirmed some treatment arm imbalances for seizures occurring on the last day of the double-blind phase, but not in favor of the study drug.

From a quality perspective, the application provides adequate information to ensure that the applicant can consistently manufacture a product that is suitable for use by the intended patients.

L93: ## 4.5. Clinical Pharmacology
L94: ## 4.5.1. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics (from OCP Review)5
L95: **Mechanism of Action (MOA):** Cenobamate reduces repetitive neuronal firing by enhancing the fast and slow inactivation of sodium channels and by inhibiting the persistent component of the sodium current. It is also a positive allosteric modulator of six subtypes of the \gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA_{A}) ion channel. However, the exact mechanisms by which cenobamate exerts its anticonvulsant effect in humans is unknown.
L96: 5 OCP Integrated Review, Section 3.2, Page 8
L97: **Dose Proportionality:** The pharmacokinetic parameters of cenobamate increased in a greater than dose-proportional manner following single oral doses of escalating strength, ranging from 5 mg to 750 mg. However, the Cmax of cenobamate increased in a dose proportional manner. The PK of cenobamate was essentially dose proportional following multiple doses ranging from 50 mg/day to 500 mg/day.
L98: **Variability:** Inter-subject variability of cenobamate in humans was up to 25% for Cmax and up to 35% for AUC0-t. The intrasubject variability of cenobamate was estimated to be 14% for Cmax and up to 5% for AUC0-t.
L99: **Absorption:** Cenobamate was well absorbed, based on mass-balance study demonstrating that at least 88% was recovered in urine following oral administration. Co-administration with a high-fat meal showed no significant effect on the rate and the extent of absorption.
L100: **Tmax:** The median cenobamate Tmax 1 to 4 hours after single- or multiple-dose administration under fasted condition over the range of 10 to 400 mg.
L101: **Distribution:** Plasma protein binding of cenobamate is moderate (60%) and independent of concentration. Cenobamate mainly binds with human albumin protein and not with \alpha1-acid glycoprotein. Cenobamate is mainly confined to plasma. The whole blood to plasma ratio of cenobamate was 0.6. The estimated volume of distribution was 40 L to 50 L.
L102: **Metabolism:** Cenobamate is extensively metabolized in liver, primarily by glucuronidation via UGT2B7 and to a lesser extent by UGT2B4, and by oxidation via CYP2E1, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, and to a lesser extent by CYP2C19 and CYP3A4/5.
L103: There were no major circulating metabolites (i.e., &gt;10% of total drug related material) identified in human plasma. In a mass-balance study, administration of radiolabeled cenobamate, unchanged cenobamate accounted for &gt;98% of the total AUC of radioactivity in plasma. M1 (N-glucuronide of cenobamate) was the only metabolite found in human plasma, and the exposure (AUC) of M1 was found to be 1.2% of the parent drug. In urine and feces, the N-glucuronide metabolite (39.4% of the dose), oxidative O-phenyl glucuronide (20.4% of the dose), dihydrodiol and its glucuronide (17.1% of the dose), and side chain O-glucuronide (3.9% of the dose) appeared to be the major metabolite of cenobamate in vivo. Unchanged cenobamate accounted for 6.8% of the dose which was mainly excreted in the urine (6.4%).
L104: **Excretion:** Following administration of radiolabeled cenobamate, a mean of 93.0% of the total radioactive dose was recovered in urine (87.8%) and feces (5.2%). The mean terminal half-life for cenobamate ranged from 50 to 60 hours following single oral doses of 100 to 400 mg.
L105: ## 4.5.2. Dose Response
L106: The OCP reviewer created plots of change from baseline seizure frequency by study epochs in titration and maintenance dose periods and dose arms to examine the effect of treatment over time. This analysis reveals a trend of increased effectiveness as the treatment interval progresses through titration into maintenance periods in study C013 (Figure 2) and C017 (Figure 3).
L107: **Figure 2 Percent Change from Baseline Monthly Seizure Frequency by Arm and By Time Period in Phase 2 Study C013**
L108: **Figure 3 Percent Change from Baseline Monthly Seizure Frequency by Arm and By Time Period in Phase 3 Study C017**
L109: ## Titration Rate vs Clinical Efficacy
L110: The pivotal study titration rates were more rapid than in study C021 from which the rate in proposed labeling is derived. This slower rate of dose escalation will result in a shift of the desired therapeutic effect to later in the titration schedule. The OCP reviewer conducted an independent analysis to compare the pivotal study titration scheme to the modified schedule of reduced escalation rate in proposed labeling. The analysis examined concentration time profile, Figure 4, and the seizure frequency compared to titration regimen, Figure 5. This analysis is valuable to determine how the reduced dose escalation rate and the prior titration schedules map to each other over the titration timeline. The OCP reviewer concludes that “The slower titration scheme proposed for the label can be expected to produce approximately half the maximum effect by 5 weeks and maximum effect at 14 to 18 weeks.” In addition, on visual inspection of the graphic analysis of Figure 5, a therapeutic effect in the range of the 100mg treatment arm of study C017, see Table 11, is expected to be present at approximately 7 weeks of titration.

L112: **Figure 5 Predicted Change from Baseline 28-day Seizure Rate for Both Pivotal Study and Study C021 (proposed labeling) Titration Regimens**

L123: ## 5.2. Review Strategy
L124: Examination of irregularities including protocol violations were performed to guide OSI site investigation. Also considered in site investigation were high volume study site recruitment and disproportionate contribution to the efficacy outcome. Distribution of study population demographics across placebo and treatment arms was examined. The trial design was assessed and is similar to the design of approved drugs for partial onset seizure treatment. The statistical reviewer was able to perform an independent review using the Applicant's submitted datasets and confirmed the Applicant's analysis results.
L125: ## 6. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L126: ## 6.1. C013
L127: ## 6.1.1. Study Design
L128: ## Overview and Objective
L129: The primary objective of this study is to evaluate the efficacy of YKP3089 at dosages up to 200 mg/day in reducing seizure frequency when compared to baseline in subjects with partial onset seizures not fully controlled despite their treatment with 1 to 3 concomitant AEDs.
L130: ## Trial Design
L131: This is a multicenter, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled study, with an 8-week baseline, 6-week titration and 6-week maintenance phase. This is followed by a 1-week taper period for subjects who are taking 100, 150 or 200mg of YKP3089.
L132: Baseline seizure frequency is captured by an 8-week seizure diary documentation of seizure frequency. If the subject maintains an excellent prospectively created seizure diary on either a written or electronic calendar that can be presented to the study staff and approved by the sponsor or their designee, then this can be used for a 4-week retrospective baseline, which will be combined with a 4-week prospective baseline. If no such documentation is available, an 8-week prospective baseline is required.
L133: Study drug (cenobamate) 50mg will be taken once daily in the morning (with or without breakfast) for two weeks. If there is good tolerability for 14 days dose will be advanced to 100mg (or placebo) daily beginning day 15. If the 100mg dose is well tolerated for 14 days, then cenobamate will be increased to 150mg on day 29. If the 150mg dose has good tolerability for 14 days, then the dose will increase to 200mg a day. Patients who report side effects may remain on the same dose (50mg, 100mg, 150mg, or placebo) and delay titration until the next visit.
L134: After day 43 (day of achieving 200mg dose) the patient will be called one week later to assess tolerability. In the event of poor tolerability, the dose may be reduced by 50mg during the telephone contact. This should be followed by an unscheduled visit. Following day 43 patients will continue treatment an additional 6 weeks, until day 85. Patients have a follow up visit at day 106.
L135: ## Dose Reduction
L136: A dose reduction can occur at a visit during titration, maintenance, open-label extension or during the phone contact. Subjects who have had their dose increased and subsequently fail to meet good tolerability criteria (as described in the section below) will have their dose decreased. Dose adjustments require an unscheduled visit. If the patient is unable to tolerate 50mg, he/she will be discontinued at the discretion of the investigator. Subjects whose dose was decreased may not have their dose increased again during the double-blind period.
L137: ## Study Endpoints
L138: ## Primary Endpoint
L139: Percent change in seizure frequency per 28 days in the Treatment Period compared to the Baseline in the ITT Population. Last-observation-carried-forward (LOCF) method will be used to impute for missing data for subjects who withdraw from the study early. Seizure frequency will be based on number of seizures per 28 days, calculated as the number of seizures over the entire time interval divided by the number of days in the interval and multiplied by 28. Analysis will be conducted using rank ANCOVA with treatment, study region (such as site or country) as factors, and the baseline seizure frequency as a covariate.
L140: ## Secondary Endpoint
L141: Responder rate: An analysis of subjects who experience a 50% or greater reduction in seizure frequency in the Treatment Period of the Double-Blind Phase.
L142: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L143: *Statistical Analysis Plan Amendment 3 dated 26 July 2013*
L144: ## Primary Efficacy Variable
L145: ## Seizure Counts
L146: The primary efficacy variable is the partial seizure frequency per 28 days.
L147: Partial seizure frequencies (average monthly [28-day] partial seizure rates) for each study
L148: period (baseline, double-blind period) will be calculated by summing the number of seizures in each period and dividing by the total duration for that period (i.e., number of days in that period) excluding days with “no data available” as per seizure diary, and multiplying by 28 in order to normalize to a 28-day rate.
L149: Any seizure cluster where individual seizures cannot be counted will have a seizure count of “1” assigned.
L150: Baseline seizure frequency will be calculated using the 56 days prior to Study Day 1 (randomization date) excluding days with “no data available” in the seizure diary.
L151: To calculate the on-treatment seizure frequency, the days utilized will be Study Day 1 (randomization date) through the last day of the double-blind treatment excluding all dose tapering days (or a subset depending on the specified analysis) and days with “no data available” in the seizure diary.
L152: Partial seizures are defined as the total of simple partial with motor component (type B), complex partial seizures (type C) (-unique seizure types to be combined) and secondarily generalized tonic clonic-seizures (type C).
L153: Partial seizures without a motor component (Type A) will not be analyzed.
L154: ## Primary Efficacy Outcome
L155: The primary efficacy outcome is defined as percent change (reduction) from baseline in seizure frequency during the double-blind period. Percent change (reduction) from baseline during the double-blind period will be calculated as follows:
L156: Percent change (reduction) = (baseline seizure frequency rate per 28 days – double-blind period seizure frequency rate per 28 days) / baseline seizure frequency rate per 28 days x 100.
L157: ## Sample Size Determination
L158: A sample size of 100 subjects per treatment group will be able to detect a difference of 16% in median percent reduction in seizure frequency between an active dosage group and placebo group, at a two-sided significance level of 0.05, assuming a standard deviation of 40%. This sample size was estimated assuming 80% power with a two-sample Wilcoxon rank sum test used and assumed as the primary analysis.
L159: ## Protocol Amendments
L160: There were 6 protocol amendments following the initial protocol through the duration of the
L161: active trial. Only protocol amendment 1 was substantive in potential influence on study outcome. This amendment allowed an additional cohort of epilepsy patients into the study that had previously been excluded due to treatment with concomitant carbamazepine. The reason for protocol amendment 1 was to allow patients on concomitant carbamazepine into the study. A DDI study had been completed that showed YKP, when added to carbamazepine therapy reduced the total carbamazepine &amp; carbamazepine epoxide concentration by 20%. This level of carbamazepine reduction was considered low enough to allow these patients into the study.
L162: Reviewer Comment: This modification could potentially increase seizure frequency if YKP reduced the concentration of the concomitant AED meaningfully. However, randomization should distribute this effect across treatment arms.

L173: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L174: Protocol violations are examined. Eight are identified in an area that may influence study outcome. Four are in the category IP compliance. Two patients on cenobamate did not have their dose advanced beyond 50mg apparently due to clerical error. These were at site 110. In the next instance in the IP compliance category one patient in the YKP arm lost eight capsules of study drug and another patient lost 4 capsules. There were two instances involving seizure diary. In the first a patient in the placebo arm had no seizure diary at visit 6; the remediation for seizure record keeping is not provided. In the second, a patient in the cenobamate arm had no diary entry for a single day. There were two concomitant medication items. In the first a patient in the placebo arm was on concomitant diazepam for 6 weeks, in the second a patient in the placebo arm had levetiracetam added at visit 12 (last treatment visit).
L175: None of the protocol violations involving study drug or concomitant medications favor the cenobamate treatment arm. The two seizure diary events also do not bias in favor of the cenobamate treatment arm.
L176: ## Table of Demographic Characteristics
L177: Examination of baseline features reveals an equal distribution of females and males in the study and across treatment arms. The mean and median patient age in the cenobamate treatment arm are 36.2kg and 36kg respectively while the mean and median ages in the placebo arm are 37.5kg and 38kg respectively. The racial distribution reveals a majority of “white” patients where the second most common racial group is “Asian”. The distribution of these two groups across the placebo and treatment arm is balanced. There is a small proportion of “black or African American” with a total of 2.25% of the total patients. These patients are adequately balance across treatment arms given the small number. There are 4 countries in study C013. The United States had the majority contribution in the study with 38.7% of patients while India, South Korea, and Poland contributed 23%, 18.5% and 19.8% respectively. The distribution of patients by national origin is balanced across the treatment arms, see Table 4.

L184: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L185: The primary efficacy analysis demonstrated a statistically significant effect of cenobamate (p &lt; 0.0001) compared to placebo on the primary endpoint of median percent reduction in the 28-day partial-seizure frequency in the ITT Population (55.6% reduction in the cenobamate group versus 21.5% reduction in the placebo group; Table 5).

L188: ## Data Quality and Integrity
L189: Based on risk level assessment by OSI and concurrence by DNP two sites for clinical inspection were determined for study C013. Site 209 located in Bangalore, Karnataka India. There were 15 patients enrolled, 7 in the placebo arm and 8 in the cenobamate treatment arm, no screen failures are identified. There was also an FDA Establishment Inspection of the sponsor for study C013.
L190: The CIS overall findings (for both studies C013 &amp; C017) noted inspectional observations at one of the clinical investigator sites (C017), as well as at the sponsor and CRO. These findings are unlikely to have a significant impact on overall study results. OSI concluded that the study data generated are considered acceptable and may be used in support of this NDA.

L203: ## 6.2. C017
L204: ## 6.2.1. Study Design
L205: ## Overview and Objective
L206: The primary objective of this study is to evaluate the effective dose range of YKP3089 at dosages from 100mg up to 400 mg/day in reducing seizure frequency when compared to baseline in subjects with partial onset seizures not fully controlled despite their treatment with 1 to 3 concomitant AEDs.
L207: ## Trial Design
L208: This is a multicenter, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled study with an 8-week prospective baseline and an 18-week double-blind treatment period (including a 6-week titration phase and 12-week maintenance phase), followed by a 3-week blinded study drug taper period (for subjects leaving the study) or a blinded 2-week conversion period (for subjects participating in the open-label extension), with a final follow-up visit 2 weeks after the last dose of study drug.
L209: Subjects who meet all inclusion criteria and none of the exclusion criteria will first undergo an 8-week baseline to assess seizure frequency. Subjects who have experienced at least 8 seizures during the baseline period without a seizure-free interval of greater than 25 days any time during those 8 weeks will be randomly assigned in a 1:1:1:1 ratio to receive placebo or YKP3089 100, 200, or 400 mg given once per day in the morning. Subjects must have at least 3 partial seizures during each of the two consecutive 4-week periods of the baseline. See timeline of assessments in Table 7.
L210: Subjects will first enter a 6-week titration phase, during which the initial dose will be 50mg/day. The planned increase in the daily dose will be 50mg/day/week increments until 200mg is reached and after which the dose will be increased by 100mg/day/week in subjects randomized to 400mg/day. See titration schedule in Figure 8. Subjects will then enter a 12-week double-blind maintenance phase.

L213: ## Study Endpoints
L214: The primary efficacy endpoint to be evaluated for registration in the United States and the Rest of the World is the percentage change from the pretreatment baseline phase in seizure frequency (average monthly seizure rate per 28 days) of all simple partial motor, complex partial, or secondarily generalized seizures compared with the seizure frequency in double-blind treatment period.
L215: The baseline seizure rate (B) is calculated by counting the number of seizures over the baseline period and dividing by the number of days in the interval with non-missing seizure data and then multiplying by 28. The double-blind period seizure rate (D) is calculated in a similar manner. The percentage change is equal to 100*(D-B)/B. Simple partial seizures without a motor/visual component will not be counted in seizure frequency rates.
L216: Percentage change in seizure frequency will be undefined if B=0. For the primary efficacy analysis, there will not be any baseline values with zero seizures due to inclusion criteria requirements. However, for the analysis of seizure sub-types, there is a possibility that B may be zero.

L218: *Statistical Analysis Plan Amendment 2 dated 20 March 2015*
L219: ## Protocol Amendments
L220: There were 2 protocol amendments following the original protocol on 6/12/13. Amendment 1 of 1/7/14 provided results from a multiple ascending dose study up to 600mg a day (proposed C017 dose did not change). The results of study C013 were also included in the updated protocol. Initial starting dose was reduced to 50mg to improve tolerability. There was a revision of data monitoring committee review. Amendment 2 of 3/20/15 removed the interim analysis, added details of the statistical procedures and added lacosamide as a concomitant medication to have PK analyses.

L226: ## Patient Disposition
L227: There were 533 patients screened that resulted in 437 patients randomized into study C017. There were 360 patients that completed the study. The most common reason for study withdrawal was an adverse event in 55 patients. The next most frequent reason for not completing the study was withdrawal by the patient. Additional attrition was due to, "lost to follow up", "protocol violation", "other" and "pregnancy". Withdrawal due to adverse events had a dose related trend while there was no differentiation between treatment arms for other categories of withdrawal, Table 8.

L230: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L231: The ADDV (protocol deviation analysis dataset) was examined. There were 16 entries from 15 patients in the "major" violation category. There were none in the placebo arm, with 5 (4.6%), 3 (2.7%), and 7 (6.3%) in the 100mg, 200mg and 300mg treatment arms respectively. The protocol violation codes were "concomitant medication", "exclusion criteria", "inclusion criteria", and "study procedures/assessments" with 8, 5, 2, and 1 instances respectively.
L232: The protocol violation codes with the greatest potential impact on study outcome were "Missing Endpoint Assessments", "Study Treatment Administration/Dispense" and "Study Treatment Compliance" categories. The highest frequency in any code was "Study Treatment Admin/Dispense" in the 400mg treatment arm that was seen in 19 (17%) of patients. The greatest differential from placebo was seen in the "Study Treatment Compliance" code, in the 100mg treatment arm where there was a frequency of 15% compared to 8% in the placebo arm,

L236: Reviewer Comment: Examination of each instance of the key protocol violation codes reveal a low likelihood of effect on study outcome because the distribution is balanced between treatment arms as in the "Missing Endpoint Assessments" code or the violations is not likely to have a consistent effect of seizure frequency, as in the "Study Treatment Admin/Dispense" code. The effect of the "Study Treatment Compliance" code is more likely to favor an increase in seizure frequency, therefore the distribution seen in this code would not bias the cenobamate treatment arms toward fewer seizures.

L247: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L248: The C017 concomitant medication dataset does not have a dedicated variable for “reason”. Any instance of midazolam, diazepam or lorazepam is selected as a possible rescue medication. There were 12 instances identified containing one of these benzodiazepines. Four entries are eliminated because the concomitant medication end-of-treatment date was prior to the study treatment-start date. From the remaining 9 entries there were 9 patients where 2, 1, 2, and 2 patients had an entry for one of these benzodiazepines. The frequency of these exposures is balanced across the treatment arms and will not influence the study outcome.
L249: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L250: Table 11 presents the primary analysis of the percent change from baseline in seizure frequency per 28 days. All three YKP3089 dose groups were statistically significantly different from the placebo based on the rank ANCOVA model. The observed medians of percent change from baseline in seizure frequency per 28 days were -55.3%, -55.2%, -36.3%, and -24.3% for the YKP3089 400 mg/day group, YKP3089 200 mg/day group, YKP3089 100 mg/day group, and placebo group, respectively.

L253: ## Data Quality and Integrity

Site 1010 was located in Philadelphia, PA where 9 patients were enrolled. Enrollment was distributed across the treatment arms as follows; there were 2, 1, 4, and 2 patients enrolled in the placebo, 100mg, 200mg, and 400mg arms respectively. There was 1 patient not assigned to treatment and 2 screen failures at the site. There was also an FDA Establishment Inspection of the sponsor for study C017.

OSI concluded that the study data generated are considered acceptable and may be used in support of this NDA.

L277: ## 7.1.4. Dose and Dose-Response
L278: In study C013 the sponsor's strategy for assessing dose response was examination of seizure frequency by dose during titration phase of the study. A moving average of seizure frequency by study visits, that represent dose epochs, was created. This analysis reveals declining seizure frequency as treatment progresses from titration through the 200mg treatment target. The sponsor does not provide corresponding cenobamate concentrations to provide assessment of concentration response. This analysis is shown in Table 13.

L282: In the study C017 Clinical Study Report, the sponsor's strategy for assessing dose response was examination of responder rate by dose group, Figure 6. This analysis reveals a maximum difference between treatment arms at the 50% responder rate. At this responder rate the approximate proportion of patients that achieve this rate are 25%, 40%, 55%, and 65% in the placebo, 100mg, 200mg, and 400mg treatment arms respectively.
L283: **Figure 6 Cumulative Distribution of Responder Rate by Treatment Group (MITT-M Population)**
(from C017 CSR, Figure 6-3, page 128)
L284: To see the FDA assessment of dose response, refer to the OCP dose response presentations in Section 4.5.2, Dose Response.
L285: Dose response is of interest in this application because the titration rate in study C013 and C017 had a more rapid escalation of cenobamate dose than in proposed labeling, Figure 8. This issue emerged due to the DRESS risk seen in phase 1 and pivotal studies. Hypersensitivity reactions were seen to be fewer when dose escalation was slower, see Figure 7. Based on these observations and the potential for immune tolerance discussed in Section 8.6.1, DRESS Risk. The
L286: resulting strategy to reduce DRESS risk was a reduction of the rate of dose escalation in study C021. There were no further DRESS case identified in this study (n=1344). This dosing strategy is carried into proposed labeling.
L287: Figure 7 Hypersensitivity Case Frequency Trend Related to Titration Rate
L288: The cenobamate titration schedule in study C021, slowed in escalation compared to the pivotal studies, is the benchmark that has been established for DRESS reduction. The benefit of accelerating dose escalation to achieve more rapid seizure reduction (increased rate of rise to therapeutic concentration) need to be balanced against the potential of an increase in DRESS risk. The increase in DRESS risk between the pivotal study titration rates and the rate in C021 has not been established and cannot be predicted. The OCP estimate (Section 4.5.2, Dose Response) reveals that half the maximal benefit is reached at week 5 and the benefit of 100mg is achieved at approximately 7 weeks (based on median % change from baseline in study C017). This duration of titration to therapeutic effect is acceptable and does not support alteration of the proposed schedule.
L289: **Figure 8** Cenobamate Dose-Titration Schedule by Study Compared to Proposed Labeling, C013, C017 and C021
L290: ## 7.1.5. Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects
L291: The OCP reviewer examined the percent change from baseline of monthly seizure frequency in study C013 and C017, see Figure 2 and Figure 3. In study C013 the last titration epoch and one maintenance epoch are compared, the first-time interval was day 29 to 57 (titration) while the second was day 57 to 85 (maintenance). There was no noticeable change in frequency across these two epochs. The span of epochs from C013 was short. A longer span is available in study C017 were three maintenance epochs are available for inspection. The three maintenance epochs span weeks 6-10, 10 to 14 and 14 to 18 respectively. There is a small loss of seizure reduction between the first and second maintenance epoch but no change between maintenance epoch 2 and 3. That second interval covers weeks 10 to 18 without notable decline in efficacy.
L292: Study C012 did not have quantitative seizure diary data for examination. The frequency of epilepsy related adverse events was examined to assess seizure worsening, see Seizure Worsening Analysis. This may act as a proxy for durability of efficacy. If there were more frequent seizure breakthrough as the 1-year study timeline progressed, that would be an indicator of loss of efficacy effect. The examination showed a rate of epilepsy / seizure adverse events consistent with background sporadic breakthrough but no upward trend that would be a signal for loss of effect.
L293: Reviewer Comment: the available analyses support an acceptable durability of cenobamate treatment effect.

L304: ## 8. Review of Safety
L305: ## 8.1. Safety Review Approach
L306: ## 8.1.1. Overview
L307: Although there are three open US INDs for YKP3089, epilepsy (IND 76,809) is currently the only indication being studied (DSUR- 10/2018). The first study of YKP3089 was initiated as IND (b) (4) on 8/12/05.
L308: At the time of the submission of IND 76809 approximately 100 patients had been exposed to YKP3089 in two studies: one completed single dose sequential administration study that examined doses of 5 to 750 mg, and a second 14-day multiple dosing (QD) study which provided preliminary data on patients receiving 50 and 150 mg QD. No serious adverse events were reported in these studies. One patient was withdrawn from the multiple dosing study for reasons of blurred vision associated with headache and dizziness; this was later thought to be consistent with migraine. In summary, there were no notable worrisome signals from the clinical trials that preceded the opening of IND 76809 (indication epilepsy).
L309: Reexamination, by this division, of preclinical data at the time of review at the opening of IND 76809 revealed an underlying event in monkeys that is worrisome in the population of patients with epilepsy. Two studies demonstrated seizures in monkeys associated with the administration of YKP3089. In one study a seizure occurred following the second YKP3089 dose of 60mg/kg. In another study consisting of 2 monkeys receiving 60mg/kg/day both had seizures on the second day of administration. After lowering the dose to 40mg/kg/day, no seizures were observed.
L310: The Sponsor failed to adequately explain the findings of seizures in monkeys. The Sponsor did revise the protocol in a fashion, as recommended, by the division to allow individual patients to be tested with lower doses before exposure to higher doses. They also altered the study so that the initial lower dose of exposure and the maximal dose is reduced. The lower proposed dose appeared to have an adequate safety margin while the proposed upper dose remained more than the required safety threshold. The division had a teleconference with the Sponsor on 4/25/07 at which time they were told that they may proceed with the protocol provided the single dose did not exceed 250 mg. At the most conservative, considering underestimation of the margin based upon single dose PK in monkeys, this would result in a 7-fold safety margin for Cmax and a 3-fold safety margin for AUC. Because of the rather long half-life this compound the Sponsor was also: 1) asked to expand the short visits from 24 to 48 hours and 2) allow at least 2-week washout after these short clinical visits.7
L311: On December 17, 2010 there was a meeting Type B where the sponsor presented questions on the design of study C013 with a maximum dose was 200mg. Enrollment in study C017 was started on 7/31/13 with a placebo, 100mg, 200mg and 400mg dose arm.
L312: An initial safety report on subject (b)(6) was submitted by the sponsor on June 26, 2015. The subject was a 38 yo female, healthy volunteer, in study YKP3089C020 a TQT study. The patient received 32 days of treatment with YKP3089. Study drug was discontinued but signs and symptoms of DRESS syndrome progressed. The subject subsequently expired 54 days after study drug was discontinued. Cause of death determined at autopsy was “determined to be cardiac dysrhythmia due to eosinophilic myocarditis due to adverse reaction to experimental drug”. Two additional reports of DRESS syndrome were also reported in the initial submission on subject (b)(6). These subjects recovered without sequelae. Following this event, a partial clinical hold was imposed in 11/9/2015 for patients treated with YKP3089 for less than 4 months and any healthy volunteers in clinical studies and new patient enrollment into ongoing studies. Patients who were on YKP3089 treatment for 4 months or longer were permitted to continue treatment. Complete discussion of the DRESS safety issue and sequence of clinical hold and the approach to resolution of safety concerns is presented in Section 8.6.1, DRESS Risk.
L313: Review of safety is directed at the indication of partial onset seizures in adults. There is a focus on the potential for DRESS a hypersensitivity reactions with a dedicated safety review of study
L314: 7 Norman Hershkowitz MD, PhD Medical Review, Complete Response to Hold, 6/15/07.
L315: C021 to assess the DRESS issue. Following the emergence of this safety signal in the phase 1 and 2 stages of development a larger, open label, safety study, C021 was performed to investigate the frequency of DRESS with a reduced titration schedule. Two, pivotal studies, C013 and C017 provide placebo-controlled safety data. In addition, due to the nonclinical signal of seizure occurrence in monkeys, an examination for the detection of seizure worsening is performed, see Section 8.4.5, Seizure Worsening Analysis.

L346: ## Reviewer Comment:
L347: Overall exposure was adequate. The sum of exposure in the pivotal studies with open label extensions and study C021 met the ICH E1A guidelines. There were 865 patients with exposure to cenobamate for at least 6 months and 710 patient for at least one year, see Table 18. Examination of the study C013-C017 pool reveals that approximately half of patients maintained cenobamate treatment for 1000 days. A high proportion of patients achieved a mean dose in the recommended dose range. Examination of study C021 reveals approximately half of patients maintained cenobamate treatment for 400 days. A high proportion of patient achieved a mean dose near the recommended dose range while the majority of patients had a modal dose at or above the recommended dose of 200mg. This comprehensive evaluation reveals sufficient exposure to adequately support proposed dosing and is also sufficient to support the safety review.

L365: ## 8.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L366: ## 8.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L367: The narrative clinical study reports for both phase 1, study C013 and C017 were complete. The xpt datasets, that were the primary support for assessment and analysis in this review were complete.
L368: ## 8.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L369: There were no systematic lumping or splitting of adverse events detected. The adverse events datasets were Re-formatted for use with the ODE I Query Coding.

L371: Clinical chemistry, hematologic and urinalysis panels obtained during the phase 1, pivotal studies and study C021 were adequately complete.
L372: ## 8.4. Safety Results
L373: ## 8.4.1. Deaths
L374: There were 16 deaths in the Cenobamate development program, Table 21. A visual summary of the dose, timeline, category and cause of deaths in the development program is shown in Figure 16.
L375: One death occurred in the baseline period leaving 15 deaths that occurred during Cenobamate treatment. There were no deaths during the double-blind treatment period. There were 10 deaths during the open label extension period of studies C013 and C017 and 4 deaths during open label study C021. One death due to DRESS occurred during a phase 1 thorough QT study.
L376: The death with the most serious implications during the development program occurred in a healthy volunteer patient in the TQT study. The patient developed DRESS syndrome after 32 days of treatment with YKP3089 at a dose of 200mg a day during days 22 to 32 of treatment. The disease process progressed after cessation of study drug although there was partial improvement with prednisone treatment. Prednisone was discontinued on day 64 followed by
L377: an increase in eosinophils and death 51 days after cessation of study drug on day 84. The issue of DRESS is discussed more fully in section 8.5.1, DRESS Risk.
L378: There were three cases of cardiogenic death. The first (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C013 OLE), due to unexplained cardiac arrest where a 58-year-old patient had right sided chest pain followed by diaphoresis and collapse. The subject subsequently went into irreversible cardiac arrest. In the second case a 60 yo female (patient [REDACTED](b)(6), C017 OLE) with a prior history of a “cardiovascular accident” developed “stomach pain” and within minutes collapsed and became unresponsive. In the third case a 39 yo male (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C017 OLE) developed unexplained cardiogenic shock. This patient had a prior adverse event of “dyslipidemia” although no history of coronary artery disease is reported. In cases 1 and 3 the causal relationship to cenobamate is not supported by the long latency to the event. In both of these cases the cardiogenic event occurred after approximately 2 years of treatment. In the second case (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) C017 OLE) the myocardial infarction occurred after 300 days of cenobamate treatment. In this case there was evidence of pre-existing cardiovascular disease, listed as “cardiovascular accident”.
L379: There were 3 cases of SUDEP, 1 each after 282, 200 and 129 days of treatment. Based on a conservative estimate of overall exposure to Cenobamate the SUDEP rate in the development program is 3.1/1000 patient years. This is comparable to a rate of 4.2/1000 patient in refractory epilepsy patients reported in 2016.8
L380: There was one case, in a 60 yo male (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C021) of death due to traumatic intracranial hemorrhage acquired during a seizure. The head injury due to seizure was the second experienced by this patient during cenobamate treatment. This may be a case of drug ineffective. Potential for seizure worsening will be further explored in Section 8.4.5 Seizure Worsening Analysis
L381: There were two cases of completed suicide, the 1st (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C013 OLE) after 132 days of cenobamate treatment and the 2nd (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C017 OLE) after 3.8 years of treatment. Causality is not well supported in the second case due to the absence of temporal relationship to treatment initiation. In the 1st case the 4-month latency allows for causality. Suicide risk is a known class effect of Antiepilepsy medications, the single case with possible temporal relationship does not define a change in this safety risk.
L382: There were 2 deaths related to physical trauma in pedestrians in traffic collisions. There was one case in a 57 yo male (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C017 OLE) reported to have sepsis from a complication of pneumonia. There is no report of seizure with a related aspiration. A severely medically compromised 21 yo female (patient [REDACTED](b)(6) study C021) with Angelman syndrome developed urosepsis with a prolonged hospital course and respiratory failure. A decision was made to withdrawal life support. In the final fatal case, a 41 yo female (patient [REDACTED](b)(6), study
L383: 8 Devinsky O, et. al, Sudden unexpected death in epilepsy: epidemiology, mechanisms, and prevention. Lancet Neurol 2016; 15: 1075–88. PMID: 27571159 DOI: 10.1016/S1474-4422(16)30158-2
L384: C017 OLE) had a seizure with aspiration and hypoxia. The patient called EMT but was subsequently found unresponsive at home. There was a hospital course consistent with multiorgan failure and hypoxic encephalopathy from the initial hypoxic event.

L392: ## Death, Reviewer Summary:
L393: There were 16 deaths during the development program, 15 were on treatment and 1 during baseline (not on study drug treatment). This count includes the 120-day safety update, representing 0.8% of patients exposed to Cenobamate. Eleven of the events occurred within 349 days of Cenobamate treatment initiation while the remaining 4 events occurred after 1.9 years of treatment. No deaths occurred in the treatment or placebo arms of the double-blind study intervals (12 weeks in study C013 and 18 weeks in study C017). There were 2 suicides, one that occurred after 132 days of Cenobamate treatment, 46 days after entering the open label phase, the second occurred after 3.7 years of Cenobamate treatment. There were two traumatic deaths, both instances of pedestrian patient struck by an automobile. In one case, the report states the patient was killed on the "highway" with no other information provided. If this event occurred on a limited access type of transit, it may represent a suicide due to the unusual nature of a pedestrian entering such an environment. However, this is speculative. There were 3 SUDEP events, this frequency does not exceed the frequency reported overall in patients with refractory epilepsy. There were 3 cardiogenic events, none before 1.9 years of treatment, one in a 39-year-old male after 2.3 years of treatment with little explanation but no compelling temporal relationship to Cenobamate treatment. There were 3 deaths related to infection, two subsequent to pneumonia. From among the 2 due to pneumonia one was due to an aspiration during a seizure. The 3rd infection related death followed a urinary tract infection in a medically fragile patient. There was one death due to a seizure with associated head injury and a death due to DRESS at day 84 in the thorough QT study.
L394: **Reviewer Comment:** there is temporal dispersion of death events with variation in the category of underlying medical characteristics of the events. There is absence of clear causal relationship in all but the subject who developed DRESS. Analysis of the deaths in development reveal a death risk related to DRESS.
L395: ## 8.4.2. Serious Adverse Events
L396: ## C013 &amp; C017 Double Blind Pool
L397: ## SAEs by SOC with Greater Than a Single Event
L398: Serious adverse events were examined in the pivotal study double blind pool of studies C013 and C017. This pool contains a total of 659 patients randomized into studies C013 and C017. One placebo patient was not treated. In the pooled group there were 108, 223, 111, and 216 patients in the 100mg, 200mg, 400mg and Placebo treatment arms respectively, see Table 22, Figure 17 and Figure 18.
L399: ## Nervous System Disorders
L400: The highest frequency of SAEs was occurred in the “Nervous System Disorders” SOC with a total of 18 SAE entries, where the most frequent preferred term was “seizure”. Under the preferred term seizure there were 2 events in the 100mg treatment arm, 1 in the 200mg treatment arm and 1 in the 400mg treatment arm. The highest frequency in the lowest dose arm may be related to the low end of the dose response curve. One SAE of status epilepticus occurred in the 200mg treatment arm. The remaining SAE events in Cenobamate treatment arms occurred under the preferred terms Ataxia, Dizziness, lethargy, hemiparesis, nystagmus, and somnolence. There was a trend of dose response for non-seizure related preferred terms where SAEs occurred in 1.9% of patients in the placebo arm, 3.7% of patients in the 100mg treatment arm, 0.9% of patients in the 200mg treatment arm and 7.2% of patients in the 400mg treatment arm.
L401: Reviewer Comment: Serious adverse events in the “Nervous System Disorders” SOC were the most frequent. Epilepsy related events of “Seizure”, “Status Epilepticus”, and “Convulsion” were half of events where the other half were primarily related to CNS dysfunction. The only SAEs in the placebo arm were epilepsy related where 4 events occurred, including one convulsion and 3 status epilepticus. The distribution of the central nervous system adverse effects was greatest in the 400mg arm. This is an expected distribution.
L402: ## Injury, Poisoning and Procedural Complications
L403: There were 10 SAEs (9 unique patients) in the category of “INJURY, POISONING AND PROCEDURAL COMPLICATIONS”, 2 in the placebo and 8 with Cenobamate treatment. Two of these events were seizures accompanied by a fall, both in the placebo arm.
L404: From among the events in the cenobamate treatment arms there were 8 events that occurred in 7 unique patients. There were three events of fall that, one of these events coded under the preferred term of “facial bones fracture” where the patient fell and struck his face on the
L405: bumper of an automobile. The facial bone fracture occurred on study day 44 at a Cenobamate dose of 400mg while another of the “fall” events occurred on study day 42 on a Cenobamate dose of 400mg. The third and final fall entry in the “INJURY, POISONING AND PROCEDURAL COMPLICATIONS” SOC appears to be secondary to an event of agitation when the patient threw herself on the floor. There is no background on the SAE of “Femur fracture”, the sponsor reports due diligence in multiple attempts to obtain additional background information. The entry in this SOC of “thermal burn”, also included in the “PSYCHIATRIC DISORDERS” SOC, occurred during a suicide attempt. Two events that included “limb injury” and “hand fracture” are identified from a single patient where the narrative indicates “performing manual labor”. The final event in this category was a “laceration” that occurred when a patient slipped while using scissors.
L406: Reviewer Assessment: The events of fall and injury are likely due to the central nervous system effects of cenobamate. The event of “thermal burn” was related to a suicide attempt.
L407: ## *Psychiatric Disorders*
L408: There were 3 events from three unique patients treated with Cenobamate in the SOC “psychiatric disorders”. Three were 2 events of suicidal ideation and one event of suicide attempt all in the 100mg treatment arm.
L409: Reviewer Comment: Suicide and suicidality risk are a class effect of Antiepilepsy drugs. In the DB dataset there were three suicidality events (no completed suicide) in cenobamate treatment and none in placebo. This supports continued class effect signal for this AED.
L410: ## Investigations
L411: Among patients in the cenobamate treatment arm there were 3 events from a single unique patient. These events were aspartate aminotransferase increased, alanine aminotransferase increased, and immunoglobulins decreased. Treatment was discontinued. When rechecked 5 days later rapid improvement in the ALT and AST values were noted. When normalized to the ULN ALT values declined from 7.6 to 4.2 while AST values declined from 3.8 to 1.4. During this interval there was a mild increase in alkaline phosphatase level to 1.4 x ULN. The decline in immunoglobulin values was observed in samples obtained 13 days after cenobamate discontinuation where IgA and IgM values were found to be 0.6 and 0.7 x LLN respectively. These measurements were repeated at 28 days post cenobamate treatment with little change in value. No baseline IgA and IgM values are available.
L412: ## Infections and Infestations
L413: There were 3 events from 3 patients. Two of three events occurred in the placebo treatment arm. The preferred terms of these events were “infectious colitis” and “abscess jaw”. In the 200mg cenobamate arm there was an entry with the preferred term “urinary tract infection”.
L414: This event started on study day 35 and resolved on study day 37 and is reported as resolved and of moderate severity by the investigator
L415: Reviewer comment: During the double-blind interval there were SAEs in the SOC of infection events in 0.9% of the placebo group and 0.4% of the cenobamate 200mg treatment arm. In this interval there is no signal for infection vulnerability associated with cenobamate treatment.
L416: ## SOCs with a Single SAE
L417: The remaining 6 SAE entries occurred in six unique patients in the categories shown in Figure 18. From among these there were two due to drug hypersensitivity. One event, drug hypersensitivity was entered in the “Immune system disorders” SOC while the second event DRESS was entered in “skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders” SOC. The patient (YKP3089C013-(b)(6)) with an entry of “drug hypersensitivity” developed flushed erythema of palms and soles with mild itching of ears after the first dose of cenobamate and was treated in the emergency department with oral methylprednisolone and antihistamines. Five days later the patient was examined and had continuation of these findings. The reaction is reported resolved 21 days after the single dose of cenobamate. The second patient (YKP3089C017-(b)(6)) had a starting dose of 100mg cenobamate during week 1 of treatment, scheduled to advance to 200mg during week 2. On study day 24 where according to schedule the patient was taking 200mg cenobamate for 16 days, the patient developed diffuse macular erythematous and pruritic rash covering the majority of body surface area including the face, trunk, back, arms, and legs; headache; arthralgia; myalgia; and memory problems. Bilateral pleural effusions were also present. Cenobamate was discontinued on study day 24. The patient was treated with antihistamines and methylprednisolone 60mg IV every 8 hours. The patient was discharged after a 7-day inpatient admission. Subsequent emergency room evaluations are documented on study day 35 and 39 for rash. The patient was then lost to follow up. Outcome of the DRESS event remains unknown, see section 8.5.1.
L418: Reviewer Comment: The occurrence of a notable and persistent erythema and pruritis after a single dose of cenobamate is consistent with a high potential to generate a hypersensitivity response. The second case in this discussion is more severe, with multisystem involvement and serositis. In this second case, the adverse event occurred in the scheme of rapid cenobamate escalation (reduced in later protocol iterations). Again, this emergence of DRESS is consistent with the high hypersensitivity potential of cenobamate. These two cases are causally related to cenobamate therapy.
L419: Among the remaining 4 events, there was one event each in the SOCs of RESPIRATORY, THORACIC AND MEDIASTINAL DISORDERS, GASTROINTESTINAL DISORDERS, MUSCULOSKELETAL AND CONNECTIVE TISSUE DISORDERS and EAR AND LABYRINTH DISORDERS. The event in the “gastrointestinal disorders” SOC was an adverse event preferred term “gastroesophageal reflux”. occurred in the placebo arm. The remaining three events were “pulmonary embolism”,
L420: “musculoskeletal chest pain” and “vertigo”. Because of the possible relationship of a pro-thrombotic state to a pro-inflammatory state, the event of “pulmonary embolism” will be explored further.
L421: YKP3089C017-[REDACTED] (b) (6) (PT pulmonary embolism): a 41-year-old Caucasian male who was admitted to the hospital on study day 24 for suspected stroke. The patient had worsening of a pre-existing right hemiparesis and a new infarct was suspected. During the evaluation asymptomatic pulmonary emboli were found incidentally, in one of the innominate arteries in the chest. The outcome is identified as recovered, the patient was begun on warfarin. Cenobamate was discontinued on study day 24.
L422: Reviewer Comment: The patient apparently had sedative adverse effects during the introduction to cenobamate. The narrative indicates that activity was reduced. The worsening of right hemiparesis was likely due to functional worsening the pre-existing left fronto-temporal encephalomalacia due to cenobamate associated CNS depression. Causal relationship is association only.

L428: **Reviewer Comment:** Analysis of serious adverse events in the double-blind period reveals a higher frequency overall in the cenobamate treatment arms but without a clear dose response relationship. The most frequent adverse events in the placebo arm are events related to epilepsy, where seizure terms almost equally divided between placebo and treatment arms and there is a minimum in the 400mg arm. "Nervous System Disorder" events that predominate in the cenobamate treatment arm are related to central nervous system dysfunction, suicidality, and drug hypersensitivity. The safety events to consider for labeling are central nervous system adverse events, drug hypersensitivity and suicidality. These are present in proposed labeling. The event of ALT elevation entered as an SAE during the double-blind treatment period reached a maximum of 7.6 x ULN while on 400mg cenobamate. The ALT value improved to 2 x ULN 12 days after study drug discontinuation. A single event of "pulmonary embolism" occurred in the
L429: 100mg cenobamate treatment arm associated with a cerebral infarction. This event may have been related to change in physical activity. An overall pro-thrombotic state could unify the cerebral infarction and pulmonary embolism. This single event without clear causality does not provide support for a mechanistic relation to cenobamate.
L430: ## C013-C017 OLE
L431: A C013-C017 Study pool is derived from the 120-day ISS. Studies C013 and C017 are selected. From this subset all entries that have a “Y” entry for C013 and C017 open label safety flag are selected (SF13OLFL &amp; SF17OLFL). Treatment day is calculated by subtracting the cenobamate treatment start date (YKPSDT) from the adverse event start date (AESDT). Finally, all treatment start dates with a value of \ge 1 are selected to yield an adverse event dataset with 4479 entries (rows) from 464 patients. The “Y” serious adverse event flag is selected from this set to yield 173 SAEs from 114 patients.
L432: Fatal events and cenobamate discontinuations are excluded from this dataset and are covered in sections 8.4.1 and 8.4.3 respectively.
L433: There were 24 preferred terms with a frequency in &gt;1 patient, see Table 23 SAE, Studies C013 &amp; C017 Pooled Open Label Extension, Preferred Terms with Frequency &gt;1 Patient (0.4%).
