L437: ## Discussion of key selected events m (occurrence may be any &gt;0):
L438: Epilepsy Events, SAE entries: There were 13 patients with an entry for “seizure”, two patients with an entry for “epilepsy”, three patients with an entry for “seizure cluster” and two patients with an entry for “generalized tonic clonic seizure”. This are a total of 20 patients with epilepsy events. *Causal Assessment: These events are distributed across a long timeline of cenobamate exposure with no clear coupling between the seizure event and duration of exposure, Figure 19. This property reduces a causal pro-convulsant relationship between cenobamate and seizure occurrence. This uncoupled pattern also reduces the likely of loss of effectiveness as the underlying cause of seizures.*
L439: **Figure 19** Studies C013-C017 Open-Label Extension, Plot of Time of Epilepsy Event vs Time of Total Exposure Duration by Individual Patients.
L440: Infection Entries: The identification of 4 patients with a preferred term entry “pneumonia” where 2 of these patients have and entry for “sepsis”. Two additional patients also have an
L441: entry for “sepsis”. The laboratory dataset for these 6 patients is examined for abnormalities in hematologic parameters as well as the patient with the single entry for “appendicitis” and the patient with an entry for “Appendicitis perforated” (0.4% each). This assessment reveals that patients YKP3089C017-(b)(6) and YKP3089C013-(b)(6), preferred terms of “appendicitis” and “pneumonia &amp; Sepsis” respectively, had a mild reduction from baseline in values for neutrophils. Patient YKP3089C013-(b)(6), with a preferred term entry for “sepsis” and “urosepsis” had an elevation of leukocytes, lymphocytes and neutrophils noted between 30 and 66 months. Two patients with the preferred term “urinary tract infection” are also included in the analysis. One of these patients (YKP3089C013-(b)(6)) had two (episodic) entries for leukocytes out of reference range (OORR) low and the second patient (YKP3089C017-(b)(6)) had a sporadic entry of lymphocytes OORR low. **Causal assessment: Overall, these infections do not appear related to systematic or sustained myelosuppression.**
L442: **Myocardial Infarction:** There were two patients with a preferred term entry for “myocardial infarction”. Patient “YKP3089C017-(b)(6)” had a fatal event that is also presented in section 8.4.1. Patient “YKP3089C017-(b)(6)” was a 60 yo female with a prior history of cardiovascular disease who had a fatal myocardial infarction on study day 1142. Patient “YKP3089C017-(b)(6)”, a 47 yo Caucasian male with a 7-year history of hypertension who also has bronchial asthma, developed intermittent shortness of breath and numbness in hands. Upon hospitalization on study day 1122 there was a diagnosis of myocardial infarction, semi-posterior. The patient had stent placed in the obtuse marginal branch. The exposure dataset reveals that cenobamate was continued until treatment day 1366. A third patient in the OLE dataset (YKP3089C013-(b)(6)) had a preferred term entry of “cardiac arrest”. This 58 yo Caucasian male developed right side chest pain radiating to his back on 200mg cenobamate treatment day 695. First responders witness a cardiac arrest upon arrival, but the patient did not survive. This patient does not have cardiovascular risk in the medical history, however the sudden cardiac arrest in the context of thoracic pain are likely due to a cardiovascular cause.

**Causal Assessment:** *These serious cardiovascular events occurred on average 2.7 years after beginning of cenobamate treatment. In one patient there was prior cardiovascular disease. In the second, younger patient, there is a history of hypertension. The third patient, entered as “cardiac arrest”, was a 58 yo Caucasian male with no documented cardiovascular risk history. The long latency to the myocardial infarction in all reports. In 2 of the 3 cases there is a plausible cardiovascular risk history. The long latency to the cardiac event in addition to 2 patient histories confounded by cardiovascular risk in the context of an adverse event with a high background rate “myocardial infarction”9, do not support conclusion of a causal relationship to cenobamate.*
L443: **Cardiac Arrest:** There were two patients with an entry for “cardiac arrest”. The first, fatal case, patient YKP3089C013-(b)(6), is discussed in sec 8.4.1 and is also entered above in the discussion of reports of the preferred term “myocardial infarction”. In the second case, patient
L444: 9 Mozaffarian D et al. Heart disease and stroke statistics--2015 update: a report from the American Heart Association. 2015 Jan 27;131(4):e29-322. doi: 10.1161/CIR.0000000000000152. Epub 2014 Dec 17.
L445: YKP3089C017-(b)(6), a 33 yo Caucasian male experienced a severe SAE of ictal asystole on study day 1141 and was hospitalized. During routine electroencephalogram (EEG) monitoring as part of an epileptic pre-surgical study, an ictal asystole was recorded during a complex partial seizure. The asystole lasted for 60 seconds. The subject fully recovered after the seizure and it was noted that the seizure characteristics were similar to the subject's usual recorded seizures with the exception of an ictal asystole seizure characteristics were similar to the subject's usual recorded seizures with the exception of an ictal asystole which had never been recorded. The patient subsequently had a pacemaker implantation. *Causal assessment: The linkage to seizure support an ictal neurocardiac or autonomic basis for the event. The long latency from start of cenobamate also reduce support for a causal relationship to cenobamate treatment.*
L446: ## *PT Pulmonary Embolism:*
L447: There are 2 patients with the adverse event term “pulmonary embolism” identified in the OLE dataset. One report, patient YKP3089C017-(b)(6), event on treatment day 183. This same patient was found to have an underlying pro-coagulant condition including lupus anticoagulant and heterogeneous status for a G20210A (Factor II) mutation which increases clotting risk. The second patient YKP3089C013-(b)(6), a 48 yo African-American male experienced the moderate SAE of pulmonary embolism on cenobamate treatment day 1897 at a dose of 350mg. The subject presented to the Emergency Department (ED) after 3 days of pleuritic chest pain. The pain was described as constant with fluctuating intensity, starting after an insect bite. An electrocardiogram was normal, chest x-ray showed clear lungs, no pneumothorax or pleural effusion; however, the thorax was positive for acute left lower lobe segmental pulmonary embolism. The subject was stable with unremarkable vital signs, was not hypoxic and was considered stable for outpatient treatment with anticoagulants for a period of 4 to 6 months. Cenobamate treatment was continued. *Assessment: The 1st case of pulmonary embolism was associated with underlying pro-coagulant state. In the second case there was a long, 5.2 year, time to onset from start of cenobamate treatment although there was no reported medical history captured, that provides an explanation for the event. Causal Assessment: One case occurred without underlying, pre-existing cause. This report has a very long latency to the adverse event. This long latency allows for a long background of non-captured, confounding etiologies, that could be drivers to the event. A causal relationship to cenobamate treatment is not supported.*
L448: ## *PT “road traffic accident” YKP3089C017-(b)(6):*
L449: Day 295), the subject experienced a severe SAE of car accident. According to the CIOMS report, the subject was hospitalized after a single vehicle crash where the subject's car collided with a tree on the same day. The accident was suspected to be secondary to having a seizure.
L450: ## *PT Cerebrovascular accident YKP3089C017-(b)(6):*
L451: (Day 50), a 70 yo African American male experienced a severe SAE of stroke. According to the CIOMS report, the subject had a history of 2 prior strokes, which presented with a history of 1.5
L452: weeks of left upper extremity weakness. The subject experienced the current stroke event manifesting as left-sided weakness as well as headache and clumsiness for 3 days and was seen at the principal investigator's clinic on [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 52). An MRI scan was obtained on an unknown date, and magnetic resonance angiography showed a right internal carotid occlusion and string of pearls sign at the borderline region of diffusion on the right side between the middle cerebral artery and anterior cerebral artery, associated with flair hyperintensities. The mechanism for the infarct appeared to be acute and chronic atherothrombotic/embolic disease resulting in right frontal lobe stroke and watershed infarcts. The patient was treated with cilostazol and atorvastatin. Cenobamate treatment was continued. **Causal Assessment: This event occurs on a background of pre-existing cerebrovascular risk. There is a temporal relationship to start of cenobamate treatment which offers some support for causality.**
L453: **PT Vision blurred YKP3089C017-[REDACTED](b) (6):**
L454: 51-year-old white female with hypoplasia of the left PCA. On study day 16 the patient experienced a left PCA territory infarction, 'cerebral infarction'. On study day 29 the patient experienced "vertigo" and "dysarthria" and was hospitalized for reassessment of cerebrovascular status. No acute cerebral ischemia was identified. There was new V1 to v3 T wave inversion. A patent foramen ovale was also identified. The patient was taking cenobamate 300mg at the time and dose was not changed. On study day 64 the patient experienced "dysarthria" and in the emergency room also reported a headache. An episode of "blurred vision" was reported the following day (study day 65). The left occipital infarct previously identified (Left PCA territory) was unchanged. **Causal Assessment: The most threatening event of occipital infarct occurred on study day 16. Causal relationship to cenobamate is confounded by a prior history of prior PCA stroke and patent foramen ovale. Patent foramen ovale has an independent thromboembolic risk. It is uncertain if all the following events across day 29 to 65 were related to recurrent ischemia or these were central nervous system dysfunction related to study drug.**

L461: PT "vertigo", YKP3089C017- [REDACTED] (b) (6):
L462: 28-year-old white female (Day 377), the subject experienced a mild AE of vertigo. On [REDACTED] (b) (6) (Day 537), the AE worsened to a severe SAE of vertigo and the subject was hospitalized. According to the CIOMS report, the subject was admitted due to dizziness, imbalance, and nausea that had been persisting and worsening over the last 3 days. A neurological examination on admission found horizontal nystagmus, an unsteady Romberg's test, and a tendency for plantar reflex on the left. (Day 537), an ECG showed regular sinus rhythm 60 bpm and flattened T waves in most leads; a head CT scan revealed asymmetric vestibulocochlear nerve canals; and a head MRI scan revealed a minor angiogenic lesion in the left frontal lobe. Administration of investigational product was maintained. During hospitalization, dizziness and nystagmus were observed to have worsened which correlated with the time of investigational product administration. (Day 540), the dose of study drug was decreased to 150 mg QD due to the event. On (Day 541), the subject was discharged from the hospital and the outcome of the event of vertigo was recovered or resolved with sequelae of moderate nonserious vertigo.
L463: **Causal Assessment:** *This report reveals a coupling between the central nervous system adverse events of vertigo, dizziness, and imbalance and the dose of cenobamate event after a long period of exposure. Causal relationship is confirmed by the dechallenge response to cenobamate dose reduction.*

L476: ## *PT "pericarditis"*
L477: YKP3089C017-(b)(6): 67-year-old Hispanic female. On study day 562, the patient experienced a severe SAE of complete left bundle branch block. Prior to the event, the investigator reported that the subject had 1 episode of pre-syncope [Day 190], the subject had felt dizzy during a left foot x-ray with lateral movements of the neck, but without impairment of level of consciousness; the subject was admitted to the emergency room for analysis including a chest x-ray and an EKG), and 1 episode of syncope [Day 326], the subject was hospitalized for dysarthria; a neurologic and physical examination showed mild alteration in recent memory and pain in the right hip; 4 days prior to the event the subject had micturition syncope while urinating and had been hospitalized). (Day 562), the subject reported syncope in the morning and presented to the emergency room. She was walking in the street on her spouse's arm and suddenly lost consciousness and fell to the ground, partially supported by her husband. An ECG showed complete left bundle branch block with changes secondary to repolarization. An ECG from the past year showed a progressing anterosuperior partial left branch block and subsequent conduction disorder. After evaluation in a clinical meeting, it was considered probable that the syncopal episode could have been related to a complete paroxysmal atrioventricular block, and a tributary pacemaker implantation was recommended (class IIa indicated). (Day 566), the tributary pacemaker was implanted. (Day 568), the subject felt intrascapular pain for 4 hours without other symptoms, and on (b)(6) (Day 569), the subject experienced dizziness and dysarthria. The subject presented to the emergency room with symptoms of dizziness when standing, not associated with other movements, and was readmitted. An ECG showed regular sinus rhythm at 72 beats per minute with ventricular response from the pacemaker; left axis, narrow QRS complex, left anterior hemiblock; and no changes in repolarization. Day 580), the subject experienced a severe SAE of pericarditis. She returned to the hospital with fever up to 39°C and pericarditis was reported. The subject felt mild epigastric pain varying with postural changes and mild somnolence. The cardiologist evaluated the subject in the emergency room and after a thoracic x-ray, CT scan, and echocardiogram, the subject was diagnosed with several pericardial effusions with incipient tamponade signed. A chest x-ray showed cardiomegaly (not previously present) which was attributed to irritative pericarditis probably related to perforation by the articular cable of the pace maker. The investigator considered the severe SAE of complete left bundle branch block possibly related to the study drug, and the severe SAE of pericarditis remotely related to the study drug. Day 605), the subject was discharged from the hospital. A transthoracic echocardiogram at discharge showed removal of pericardial layers and normal biventricular function. The suspected diagnosis was perforation by the atrial lead and secondary irritative pericarditis. A repeat echocardiogram showed no signs of pleural effusion relapse, so the pericardial drain was removed. The subject was diagnosed with cardiac tamponade, probable cardiac perforation from the atrial electrode. *Causal Assessment: This patient had an intraventricular conduction defect identified on baseline ECG. The 1st episode of syncope may have been the earliest manifestation of an emerging conduction disorder. Overall, causal relation to cenobamate is uncertain. It is possible that the baseline intraventricular conduction*
L478: *delay is a selective vulnerability for drug related adverse pharmacodynamic effect. The subsequent SAE of "pericarditis" is a procedural related adverse event.*
L479: PT "Road Traffic accident" YKP3089C017-(b)(6): (Day 317), the subject experienced a severe SAE of automobile-pedestrian accident which was fatal. The subject was hit by a car on the highway at approximately 20:30 and sustained multiple injuries that proved fatal, and he died at the scene of the accident. 400mg Cenobamate. *Causal assessment: There is insufficient information to judge causal relationship, either due to suicidal intent or other manifestation of central nervous system dysfunction such as somnolence or confusion.*
L480: ## *PT "confusional state"*
L481: YKP3089C013-(b)(6): (also psychosis): 40-year-old Caucasian male. Approximately study day 365 while on cenobamate 200mg, the subject developed a group of symptoms and signs including blurry vision, memory loss, confusion and word finding difficulties. He was suspected of having increased seizures at first. The subject was diagnosed with Confusional state. According to the investigator, carbamazepine was tapered off, locasamide was decreased and topiramate was increased, and YPK3089 200 mg/day was continued. An EEG on (b)(6) revealed background slowing and right frontal spike activity. The subject was admitted to the hospital on (b)(6) for EEG monitoring. No significant increased seizure activity was demonstrated. In the hospital, his condition worsened with him developing more irritability, boisterousness, and confusion as well as new perseveration. At times he was aggressive and needed aripiprazole and lorazepam for sedation. On (b)(6) the subject experienced a psychotic episode with delusions, visual and auditory hallucinations and became aggressive and disruptive. He was given a single dose of Lorazepam 2mg IV and then PRN. A psychiatric consult was requested. He was started on Zyprexa 10mg qd. An EEG of (b)(6) showed mild-to-moderate generalized background slowing suggestive of bihemispheric dysfunction, may be seen in toxic or metabolic encephalopathies or primary neurological disorders. An MRI of (b)(6) showed diffuse cerebellar atrophy which could be due to chronic seizure disorder, no evidence of infarct, intracranial hemorrhage and no mass effect or midline shift. On (b)(6) the subject continued to be confused but marginally improved. On (b)(6) the delusions and auditory hallucinations resolved. On (b)(6) the subject was clinically stable. His mental status improved. He continued to have intermittent agitation, but it was less often and less intense. He was close to his baseline and was discharged. Both informed the investigator that the subject is doing very well without any residual effects of the SAE. Study Drug was discontinued on study day 382. *Causal Assessment: No clear underlying vulnerability to psychoactive drugs is identified in the medical history. A causal association is supported by a positive dechallenge response (potentially confounded by antipsychotic drug treatment).*
L482: ## Study C021
L483: Serious adverse events in study C021 were examined using the 120 day SDTM update. The 3
L484: sponsor assigned treatment arms that included Cenobamate and Phenytoin group, the Cenobamate and Phenobarbital group, the Cenobamate and Other Concomitant AEDs group were pooled to create a single arm of any patient treated with cenobamate. The JMP Clinical application was used for analysis. There were 1344 evaluated patients in this analysis pool. At the time of the 120-day safety update there were 1339 patients contained in the SDTM exposure dataset (EX.xpt). The mean and median daily dose for these patients was 177mg and 179mg respectively while the mean and median treatment durations were 352 and 355 days with a 1st quartile treatment duration of 271 days. In the C021 120-day safety update cohort there were 201 serious adverse events from 150 unique patients.
L485: In the analysis of serious adverse events in study C021, the SOC's that have a frequency of 9 or more events will be examined by each SOC term in the subsequent paragraphs.
L486: ## SOC
L487: ## *Nervous System Disorders*
L488: The highest frequency of SAEs occurred in the SOC “Nervous system disorders” with 64 events from 55 patients. The most frequent preferred terms within this group of events was “seizure” where there were 22 events from 22 patients (1.6%). There were no cenobamate discontinuations among these 22 events. Seizure SAEs occurred from study day 10 to 747 with a mean and median of day 205 and day 324 respectively. There is no clear temporal relationship to these seizure events, and these do not appear to indicate seizure worsening. This patient had additional preferred term entries for “jaw fracture”, “concussion”, “face injury”, and “tooth fracture”. Non-seizure related events were also prominent as seen in the evaluation of the pivotal studies in double blind phase and open label extensions. These events include “ataxia”, “Dizziness”, “somnolence”, “dysarthria”, “balance disorder”. The majority of these events occurred in the 1st 8 months of cenobamate treatment.
L489: ## *Injury, Poisoning and Procedural Complications*
L490: The SOC with the second highest frequency of events (after “Nervous system disorders”) was “Injury, poisoning and procedural complications”. There were 29 events that occurred in 25 patients in this SOC. The most frequent preferred terms identified from this SOC were fractures. There were 17 preferred terms containing fracture from 15 patients. Overall treatment emergent (TEAE) adverse events from these 15 patients were examined for the co-occurrence of seizure, as a possible basis for the orthopedic injury. Among these 15 patients there were 119 TEAE entries. There was one patient with a preferred term entry for seizure. These events are identified as recovered with no change in cenobamate dose. The most common TEAE preferred term entries with a frequency greater than 2 were 7, 5, 4, 4, 3, and 3 instances of “somnolence”, “dizziness”, “headache”, “fall”, “upper respiratory tract infection”, and “fatigue” respectively. The high co-association of central nervous system TEAE’s “somnolence”,
L491: “dizziness” and “fatigue” are an indication that a portion of these injuries may be due to central nervous system dysfunction.
L492: ## *Infections and infestations*
L493: There were 23 entries from 20 patients in this SOC. The most frequent preferred term in this SOC is “appendicitis”. On inspection this event appears to be more frequent than commonly observed in a cohort of this size and duration of treatment in an antiepileptic drug trial. This adverse event will be examined further in the discussion of the preferred term frequencies and also in Significant Adverse Events, 8.4.5 Appendicitis. There were 5 instances of pneumonia entered from 5 unique patients. The time to onset ranged from 4 days to 424 days where the median time to event was 161 days. All are entered as “recovered” while none of these “pneumonia” events resulted in treatment discontinuation.
L494: From the remaining 12 adverse event preferred terms in this SOC from 10 patients there were 2 instances of urosepsis, 2 instances of urinary tract infection, one instance of sepsis, one instance of herpes zoster, two instances of gastroenteritis one instance of encephalitis, one abdominal wall abscess (this occurred in a patient with appendicitis), and one instance of cellulitis. From among these 12 adverse events, two patients had study drug discontinued, two had study treatment interrupted and in 6 patients cenobamate treatment was continued unchanged. Two of the adverse events were identified as “recovered/resolved with sequelae”. These events with sequelae were “encephalitis” and “herpes zoster”.
L495: There was a preferred term “encephalitis” entered on study day 119 (patient [REDACTED](b) (6)). A lumbar puncture was performed. Serum glucose was depressed at 10mg/dl (corresponding serum glucose not provided) with a highly elevated protein of 600mg/dl. There is no report of microbiology from the 1st spinal fluid sample obtained 2 days after hospital admission. At the second lumbar puncture on the 6th day of hospital admission the narrative indicates “Chinese ink staining gene expert and bacteriologic culture were negative”. The patient’s clinical status was poor on discharge after a 17-day hospitalization. The patient was noted to be in a minimally conscious state, see narrative. All laboratory values are examined during the study timeline. Absolute neutrophil count at baseline was 2.3 \times 10^9/L with a nadir of 1.1 \times 10^9/L on study day 60 that returned to baseline level at study day 88 but had some decline to 1.7 \times 10^9/L on study day 116. There were no other notable post baseline laboratory abnormalities.
L496: The preferred term “herpes zoster” was entered on study day 296 (patient [REDACTED](b) (6)), see narrative. This patient experienced a herpes zoster infection (shingles). The patient had a left facial shingles rash. The patient was subsequently hospitalized on study day 299 with a diagnosis of varicella zoster virus encephalitis and deranged liver function studies. The patient was treated with acyclovir 800mg three times a day, oxycodone 2.5mg twice a day as needed and pregabalin 25mg three times a day. The study drug was not changed due to the event. The patient was discharged home to continue intravenous acyclovir via PICC line. All laboratory values are
L497: examined from the course of the 371-day study timeline. There was an episodic decline in absolute neutrophils from 2.7 \times 10^9/L at study day 115, however, the value at day 269, most proximate to the event of herpes zoster, the neutrophil value was 2.2 \times 10^9/L. There was no consistent change from baseline to abnormal values of any laboratory metrics during the study timeline.

L500: Reviewer Comment: There is an increase in appendicitis over expected background rate prompting evaluation of overall infection rate as well as the lymphocyte and neutrophil counts of patient YKP3089C021-(b)(6). The profile of events in the SOC “infections and infestations” does not appear to be high in relation to the size and medical status of the study population. The event of “encephalitis” is of uncertain etiology. There was a value of neutropenia, not critically low, that was not in temporal association with the diagnostic entry. It is not conclusive from the narrative that this was an infectious event. The profile of overall infectious event does not rise to a safety signal.
L501: ## *Psychiatric disorders*
L502: There were 17 entries from 16 patients in this SOC. The most frequent events were “suicide attempt” and “suicidal ideation”. These accounted for 6 events from 5 patients. The onset of these events ranged from study day 144 to 441 with a median of 189 days. One patient, YKP3089C021-(b)(6), is identified as having “recovered/resolved with sequelae”. The narrative for this patient’s event is examined and reveals an interval of psychosis preceded (*not entered as AE*) the suicide attempt. This patient was noted to have paranoid ideation from approximately study day 102 until the suicide attempt on study day 190. This event occurred 6 days after the last cenobamate dose. On study day 190 the patient stabbed himself multiple times. “The physical examination revealed approximately 10 abdominal stab wounds, 3 stab wounds on the right side of the neck, a 20cm long slash wound on the left forearm and a 5cm long slash wound on the right forearm. The CT procedure revealed liver injury as well as injury
L503: of an abdominal vessel.” See YKP3089C021-(b)(6) narrative.
L504: The next most frequent preferred term entry in this SOC is “mental status changes”. The earliest onset of a “mental status change” was on study day 79 with the latest on study day 330. The first patient (b)(6) discussed has an entry in the outcome variable (AEOUT) of “recovered/resolved with sequelae”. The narrative report is examined. This report reveals that the patient’s spouse found the patient on the floor and noted her to be very unsteady. Gait continued to decline, and the patient was taken to the emergency room where recent memory was faulty. A CT angiogram was performed and did not reveal evidence of stroke. Right temporal encephalomalacia was identified. The narrative indicates the patient recovered from the altered mental status with sequelae 2 days after onset and from ataxia 9 days after onset. The reviewer conclusion is that this may have been a post-ictal event. The second report of mental status change was due to an acute alcohol intoxication. The third report of mental status change was ameliorated by a dose reduction from 200mg daily to 150mg daily.
L505: The remaining 8 events in the “psychiatric disorders” SOC occur in 8 individual preferred terms. These preferred terms are “aggression”, “anxiety”, “depression”, “postictal psychosis”, “major depression”, “affective disorder”, “anxiety disorder”, and “psychotic disorder”. All are identified in the outcome variable (AEOUT) as “RECOVERED/RESOLVED”. In three of the 8 events the study drug was discontinued. Two of these events occurred in the same patient.
L506: Reviewer Comment: There is a signal for neuropsychiatric adverse effects based primarily on the frequency of suicidality. In the other terms from the “psychiatric disorders” SOC there were eight events. Three of these eight events may have a causal relationship based on temporal relationship. Two of these events were paranoid delusions where there was underlying psychiatric history in one case and in the second of the paranoid delusion cases an epileptogenic basis was possible. The third event was a “major depression” that was temporally related to cenobamate initiation but was managed while therapy continued for an additional 520 days.
L507: ## *Gastrointestinal disorders*
L508: There were 10 entries from 10 patients in this SOC. There were 3 entries of the preferred term vomiting and 2 with the preferred term abdominal pain. The remaining 5 terms were individual entries for constipation, enteritis, gastritis, lower gastrointestinal haemorrhage and pancreatitis chronic.
L509: Reviewer Comment: From among the gastrointestinal events obstipation may have a causal relationship supported by the high frequency of “constipation” adverse events in the clinical trials. Among the remaining serious adverse events a relationship to cenobamate is not adequately supported.
L510: ## *Skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders*
L511: There were 9 entries from 9 patients in this SOC. The most frequent event preferred term was “rash Papular” with 3 entries. The remaining six entries was from six distinct preferred terms. All nine events were reported resolved in the AEOUT variable while cenobamate was discontinued in 8 of the 9 patients.
L512: Reviewer Comment: All of the SAEs in this category were based in a hypersensitivity responses. Examination of these SAEs reveals that development of these hypersensitivity reactions usually occurs within 60 days of the start of cenobamate. There were two cases of “rash papular” without a temporal relationship. There was one case with a rash onset on day 316 and a second with rash onset at day 138. These SAEs are consistent with the strong hypersensitivity signal that has been observed in cenobamate treatment.
L513: ## Terms of Interest from SOC's with Less than 9 Entries
L514: There were 16 SOC's that had less than 9 entries. Among these there were 49 entries containing 44 preferred terms from 39 patients. Individual reports from entries will be examined where the event is identified as “resolved with sequelae” in the AEOUT variable or where cenobamate was withdrawn identified in the AEACN or the event is identified as severe in the AESEV variable. Nineteen entries from 16 patients fulfil these criteria.
L515: There remain 22 entries from 20 patients that do not fulfill criteria for narrative inclusion based on the specific variable (AEACN, AEREL, AEOUT) criteria described above. From this group, selected terms of interest that may be related to hypersensitivity, are unexpected, and potentially highly threatening are chosen for examination. These events will include: “visual acuity reduced”, “Acute myocardial infarction”, “pyrexia”, Fibrin D dimer increased”, “acute kidney injury”, “acute respiratory failure”, “breast cancer” and “deep vein thrombosis”.
L516: ## *Less than 9 entries with Outcome “Resolved with sequelae”*
L517: Reviewer Comment: among these reports there was a report of a pulmonary embolism and a report of deep venous thrombosis without alternative cause. There was however, no temporal relationship where both occurred after more than six months of exposure. The remaining events in this group had no causal relationship or were in a category were causality could not be excluded but were isolated events without a comparator placebo arm. Condensed summaries of the case narratives are presented in Appendices 13, 13.2.1 Summaries From the Category of “Less than 9 entries with Outcome ‘Resolved with sequelae’”
L518: ## Reports not Captured by Variable “AEACN, “AEREL” and “AEOUT” Criteria.
L519: There were 30 entries from 25 patient containing 28 preferred terms that did not fulfill capture
L520: criteria based on the “AEACN,” “AEREL” and “AEOUT” variables described in the section above: Terms of Interest from SOC’s with Less than 9 Entries. From this remaining group of serious adverse events there are eight preferred terms of interest selected. These terms are selected to have a narrative summary entry due to the potential they represent for a high physiologic threat or potential relationship to DRESS. These eight preferred terms include “Acute kidney injury”, “Acute myocardial infarction”, “Acute respiratory failure”, “Breast cancer”, “Fibrin D dimer increased”, “Pyrexia”, “Tetany”, and “Visual acuity reduced” captured from 6 patients.
L521: **Reviewer Comment:** from among this category of reports there were clear causally related events. One event of concern is a myocardial infarction that occurred in a 38-year-old female. The earliest symptoms were at treatment day 80 although the infarct occurred at study day 170. This event is confounded by a history of dyslipidemia and a light smoking history as well as a family history of cardiovascular disease. The patient’s mother had a myocardial infarction at age 50. The attribution of this event is not adequate to influence approvability or add to labeling. A condensed version of the narrative summaries may be seen in Appendices 13 Reports (of interest) not Captured by Variable “AEACN,” “AEREL” and “AEOUT” Criteria.
L522: ## Exploration of Selected Preferred Terms
L523: The term “appendicitis” was identified in 6 entries from six patients (0.5%) in study C021. Cenobamate exposure in study C021 at the time if these entries is calculated as approximately 1394 patient years. The occurrence of 6 cases of appendicitis yields an incidence of 4.3/1000 patient years of exposure. This is greater than the reported background incidence of 1.1/1000 population per year.10 This adverse event will be explored further in Section 8.4.5, Appendicitis.
L524: ## 8.4.3. SAE, Summary and Conclusions
L525: **Reviewer Comment:** Analysis of serious adverse events in the double-blind period reveals a higher frequency in the cenobamate treatment arms but without a clear dose response relationship. The most frequent adverse events in the placebo arm are events related to epilepsy with seizure terms almost equally divided between placebo and treatment arms. There was a minimum of epilepsy terms in the 400mg arm. “Nervous System Disorder” events that predominate in the cenobamate treatment arm are related to central nervous system dysfunction, suicidality, and drug hypersensitivity. The safety events to consider for labeling are central nervous system adverse events, drug hypersensitivity and suicidality. These are present in proposed labeling. The single event of ALT elevation, entered as an SAE, reached a maximum of 7.6 x ULN while on 400mg cenobamate. The ALT value improved to 2 x ULN 12 days after study drug discontinuation. A single event of “pulmonary embolism” occurred in the 100mg
L526: 10 1. Addiss DG, Shaffer N, Fowler BS, Tauxe RV. THE EPIDEMIOLOGY OF APPENDICITIS AND APPENDECTOMY IN THE UNITED-STATES. American Journal of Epidemiology 1990;132(5):910-25 doi: 10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a115734[published Online First: Epub Date]].
L527: cenobamate treatment arm associated with a cerebral infarction. This event may have been related to change in physical activity.
L528: In the open label extension of the pivotal trials C013 and C017 20 events of epilepsy were distributed across a long exposure timeline without clustering early or late in treatment. This points away from both pro-convulsant and loss of efficacy distribution patterns. There were 3 patients with a myocardial infarction where the events occurred on average 2.7 years after starting cenobamate. Two patients had plausible cardiovascular risk factors. Given the high background rate of myocardial infarction a causal relationship is not supported for these events. Two patients had cerebrovascular events (stroke) with a temporal relationship to initiation of cenobamate treatment, although both patients had a history of cerebrovascular disease. The temporal relationship does support potential causality in a circumstance of selective vulnerability. In this OLE safety dataset there were two cases of interest where elements of central nervous system dysfunction, usually associated with the earlier treatment period, occurred as “balance disorder” and “vertigo” on days 1192 and 377 respectively. Symptoms were ameliorated by dose reduction, demonstrating a dechallenge response. Two severe psychiatric events of psychosis were identified, in one case there was causal support based on a dechallenge response while in the second there is no causal support and the event resolved spontaneously without change in cenobamate dose. These events raise a concern for an adverse psychoactive property of cenobamate but do not provide conclusive support.
L529: Examination of serious adverse events in the open label study C021 reveals the highest frequency of events in the “nervous system disorders” SOC. Seizure related events were the most frequent followed by central nervous system dysfunction. The severity of these later events was judged as “moderate” or “severe” in the majority of cases but there were no cenobamate discontinuations. There was a higher frequency of seizure related events in the 1st 60 days but thereafter a more event distribution to 420 days. This distribution, as in the discussion of epilepsy related events in the pivotal open label studies does not raise alarm for seizure worsening or lack of efficacy but is consistent with an ongoing background rate of seizures in a refractory population. The “injury, poisoning and procedures” SOC contained 3 “fall” events. Two of three falls occurred in the first 2 months and the third at near 6 months. The “infections and infestations” SOC contained an unexpected high frequency of the preferred term “appendicitis”. Exploration of the development program identified 9 cases of appendicitis that yields an estimate for a frequency that is 2.6-fold greater than the expected background rate, see Significant Adverse Events 8.4.5, Appendicitis. The “psychiatric disorders” SOC revealed 5 patients had an entry of suicide attempt or suicidal ideation. This yields an occurrence of 3.7 events per 1000 patients that is similar to the signal of 3.4/1000 patients for suicidality in current class labeling for Antiepilepsy drugs. There were two events of psychosis. In one case the psychosis preceded a suicide attempt. This indicates a possible signal for an adverse psychoactive effect of cenobamate. Examination of serious adverse events in the “gastrointestinal disorders” SOC did not reveal notable safety signal. Examination of the “skin and subcutaneous disorders” SOC revealed 9 serious adverse events from 9 patients. Of note, 7 of the 9 events occurred in the 1st 90 days. There were 2 SAEs that developed rapidly after
L530: initiation of cenobamate. In the 1st case the SAE entry entered as “swelling face” (ID YKP3089C021-(b)(6)) developed on day 2 of treatment with cenobamate 12.5mg. In this second rapid onset case a patient developed an urticarial body rash after the 2nd dose of 12.5mg cenobamate (YKP3089C021-(b)(6)). These reports demonstrate the high potential for a hypersensitivity response with cenobamate treatment. Seven of the 9 events led to cenobamate discontinuation. The high rate of discontinuation is in alignment with heightened pharmacovigilance in the study for hypersensitivity reactions. No new cases of DRESS occurred in study C021. There was an event of myocardial infarction in a 38 year old female. This event is confounded by an early family history of cardiovascular disease and a comorbidity of a hyperlipidemia syndrome. However, the young age of the patient involved is a potential cardiovascular safety signal, possibly for patients with an underlying vulnerability such as strong family history.
L531: **Conclusion:** Examination of serious adverse events in the phase 2/3 safety datasets reveal the core safety issues are central nervous system dysfunction that may be severe but generally occur early after initiation of cenobamate. The reduced titration rate may mitigate this effect, although there were reports of these SAE events after long periods of exposure. The next major core safety issue are psychiatric disorders where suicidality frequency is in alignment with the current AED class labeling. There were four reports of psychotic disorder that indicate a signal for an adverse psychoactive effect of cenobamate. There were reports of appendicitis with a frequency higher than expected background rate. Hypersensitivity reactions were evident and could emerge very early after the start of cenobamate exposure. There was a case of DRESS in study C017 but no new DRESS cases were identified in study C021 at the reduced cenobamate titration rate, see DRESS Risk, 8.6.1.

L540: ## DRESS
L541: ## YKP3089C017- (b) (6) (PT DRESS):
L542: 36-year-old white female. On study day 24 the subject presented to an emergency room with a several day history of fever and chills; a 2-day history of diffuse macular erythematous and pruritic rash covering the majority of body surface area including the face, trunk, back, arms, and legs; headache; arthralgia; myalgia; and memory problems. The patient was hospitalized, upon admission, the subject had a fever with temperature of 101.3°F and a morbilliform rash on the abdomen and legs. The last entered site visit is study day 11 while the final study laboratory day -2. Laboratory studies related to the DRESS event are obtained from the hospitalization and reveal elevation of ALT that reached a maximum of 117 (approximately 3.5 x ULN of the study upper range), eosinophil percent increased to 15.3%, and platelet count reached a nadir or 142,000, within normal study reference range. Note, hospital reference ranges are not provided.
L543: During hospitalization a CT scan of the chest and pelvis was performed on study day 29 to evaluate for lymphoma. Findings included bilateral pleural effusion, right side was greater than the left side, with right lower lobe consolidation. A moderate amount of free fluid in the abdomen and pelvis tracking along the right pericolic gutter was observed, with either a thickened gallbladder wall versus fluid in the gallbladder fossa. Mediastinal lymph nodes were enlarged bilaterally but not consistent with lymphoma along with bilateral axillary and inguinal lymph nodes. The subject was treated with methylprednisolone (60 mg, IV, every 8 hours) before being transitioned to prednisone but duration of this treatment is not reported. On study day 31 the patient was discharged from the hospital but returned to an emergency room on study day 35 and 38 due to rash. Following this, the subject was lost to follow up despite multiple attempts to have her return with a conclusion of DRESS outcome unknown.
L544: Cenobamate dose: the last recorded dose in the exposure dataset is entered on study day 7. The study narrative indicates no study drug was taken from study day 8 to 12. The patient was scheduled to escalate dose from 100mg to 200mg on study day 8 and the investigator indicates the last dose of study drug was 200mg on study day 16 (although not verified in exposure dataset).

L548: **Causal Assessment:** The patient presents to an emergency room on study day 24 with fever and rash. Last onset contact was study day (-2) and prior phone contact was study day 18 where “tiredness” is reported but no other abnormal finding. The temporal profile of cenobamate intake is incomplete with the last confirmed dose of 100mg on study day 7. It is unclear if any further cenobamate treatment followed. The event therefor may have occurred 17 days after last cenobamate although this is uncertain. The temporal relationship to cenobamate treatment and absence of reported introduction of confounding medications in the same timeframe essentially confirm causal relation to cenobamate. The event had serious multiorgan effects including mediastinal adenopathy, serositis, fever and increased eosinophils. Rash also persisted after hospital discharge and resolution is not confirmed due to “lost to follow up”.
L549: **Figure 23 Study C017 Adverse Events Leading to Discontinuation by Treatment Arm by Percent of Dose Group**

A composite group of All Rash that included “Rash, Rash Macular and Rash Pruritic” was also in this frequency category. These discontinuations are seen in Table 26. The “All Rash” summation category did not have a dose response.

L553: ## Ataxia
L554: The narratives of 3 patients with the preferred term “Ataxia” are examined to assess the severity of the adverse events, concomitant medications and titration rate.
L555: YKP3089C017-(b)(6) (PT Ataxia): 43-year-old white male experienced severe SAEs of nystagmus, ataxia, and dizziness on study day 42. The subject arrived at the medical center with suspected drug intoxication and was admitted to the hospital. The subject complained of dizziness, nystagmus observed when looking sideways, and truncal ataxia. abnormal general movement, gait, biceps, finger-nose, heel-shin (hemiataxia), knee, rapid alternating movements, and Romberg's tests secondary to left hemiparesis (present in medical history) and nystagmus with gaze evoked bilateral and truncal ataxia. Cenobamate was discontinued. The patient had been escalated to a cenobamate dose of 300mg on study day 35 and titration was started at 100mg on day 0. There is an interval between study days 10 and 28 with no entries in the exposure dataset, Figure 24. It is unclear if medication was not received. Concomitant medication included clonazepam 15mg BID and lamotrigine 350mg BID. **Causal assessment:** *This event was in temporal proximity to a 100mg dose increment precede the event by 4 days. The close relationship to dose escalation support a causal relationship. In addition, the event appears to be a notable CNS intoxication characteristics. There was underlying CNS vulnerability based on the medical history of a left hemiparesis and mild cognitive impairment. The medical history is brief so a detailed assessment of the degree of compromise is uncertain.*
L556: Figure 24 Patient YKP3089C017- (b) (6) Dose by Date
L557: YKP3089C017- (b) (6) (PT Ataxia): 55-year-old white female Study Day 17, the subject experienced mild AEs of ataxia and nystagmus. The study drug was withdrawn due to the events and the last dose was taken on study day 17, see Figure 25 (ADEX dataset identifies last dose on day 33). A neurological examination on the same day showed the following significant abnormal findings: gait with mild ataxia and Romberg's test showing instability; the clinically significant abnormal finding included horizontal nystagmus on the left eye. At the time of the events of ataxia and nystagmus, the subject also experienced nonserious AEs of vision blurred, vomiting, and nausea. Concomitant Antiepilepsy drug was carbamazepine. **Causal Assessment:** The events of ataxia, nystagmus, vomiting, and nausea occurred on the 4 day following dose escalation from 200mg to 300mg. This temporal relationship supports causality. The OCP review indicates that carbamazepine increases the oral clearance of cenobamate by 15% in population PK modeling. This event is not due to interaction with carbamazepine based on modeling of clearance.
L558: Figure 25 Patient YKP3089C017- (b) (6) Dose by Study Day (Red Line = Discontinuation)
L559: YKP3089C017- (b) (6) (PT Ataxia): 44-year-old white male On Study Day 22), Figure 26, the subject experienced a mild AE of ataxia. On study day 29, a neurological examination showed the following clinically significant abnormal findings: gait with mild sway and unsteady but can walk in tandem and nystagmus, which was unspecified in examination per source. The study drug was withdrawn due to the event and the last dose was taken on study day 39. Concomitant AED medications included clobazam (20 mg, oral, QD), topiramate (50 mg, oral, QD; 100 mg, oral, QD), and lacosamide (200 mg, oral, BID). A population PK modeling study. Causal Assessment: This adverse event occurred 15 days after dose escalation from 100mg to 200mg cenobamate, during the phase of escalating concentration and is consistent with the adverse CNS effect profile of cenobamate, Figure 4. Causality is likely. Concomitant clobazam decreases oral clearance of cenobamate by 19% in a Population PK study noted in the OCP review. This decrease may have contributed to the adverse effect.
L560: Figure 26 Patient YKP3089C017- (b) (6) (PT Ataxia) Dose by Study Day (Red Line = Discontinuation)
L561: Reviewer Comment: the adverse event of “ataxia” leading to discontinuation is plausibly related to cenobamate in the 3 discontinuations that were examined. In all three reports that event occurred either proximal to a dose escalation or during the phase of rapid escalation of plasma cenobamate level. Discontinuations due to ataxia were more frequent in cenobamate treatment than in the placebo with 88% in the cenobamate treatment arm. There were 17 “ataxia” adverse events from 16 patients in studies C013 and C017 on cenobamate treatment where it is observed that 82% of these events occurred in the 1st 6 weeks of treatment.

The patient is in the titration phase where there is a steep increase in plasma cenobamate concentration, (see Clinical Pharmacology, Figure 4 and Figure 27).*

L575: ## Hypersensitivity
L576: YKP3089C017- (b) (6) (PT Hypersensitivity): This report is explored because it was the single discontinuation due to hypersensitivity in the DB pivotal study pool. The patient was randomized to a daily dose of cenobamate 100mg/day and began titration phase at a dose of 50mg daily x 1 week (reduced titration rate of protocol amendment 1/7/2014). On Study Day 57, the patient experienced a moderate AE of hypersensitivity (verbatim term: allergic reaction with rash and swelling of the face). The patient was treated with hydroxyzine dihydrochloride (10 mg, oral, every 12 hours) and levocetirizine (5 mg, oral, every 24 hours). The study drug was withdrawn due to the event and the last dose was taken on study day 70. On study day 88, the outcome of the event of hypersensitivity was recovered/resolved. The CRF and patient profile are examined but no additional clinical detail concerning the clinical course of pruritus, rash or swelling are provided). The profile of cenobamate exposure is shown in Figure 32. *Causal Assessment: The event is in alignment with the hypersensitivity potential of cenobamate. This event was delayed to day 57. There was apparently successful treatment with antihistamines only and a report of resolution 18 days after study drug discontinuation.*

L592: ## Preferred terms
L593: ## Frequent Events
L594: The most frequent adverse event preferred term leading to discontinuation was “dizziness” with 14 events from among 14 patients representing 1% of the C021 study population. Twelve of these events occurred in the first 100 days of treatment. Seizure was next in frequency with 9 events from 9 patients. These events occurred between study days 1 and 201 and 66% occurred within the 1st 50 days. Rash followed at the third most frequent event in 9 patients leading to discontinuation. The severity is entered as mild in 6 and moderate in 3 while the 8 had onset before day 47 with one outlier occurrence at day 254. Somnolence followed as the 4th most frequent adverse event leading to discontinuation. There were 8 events from 8 patients. Fatigue followed somnolence as the 5th leading adverse event leading to discontinuation with 8 events from 8 patients. Headache was the 6th leading cause of discontinuation from adverse event with 6 event from 6 patients. All remaining preferred term entries had 5 or less events leading to discontinuation.
L595: **Reviewer Comment:** From among the most frequent adverse event entries those with preferred terms “dizziness”, “somnolence”, and “fatigue” are seen commonly with antiepileptic drugs, possibly due to CNS depression or neural network dysfunction associated with modification of action potential generation. These events are not unexpected for this drug group.
L596: **Seizure:** The adverse events due to the preferred term “seizure” are of concern because of the possibility of a proconvulsant effect on some individuals or drug ineffective. Differentiating these possibilities from the occurrence of background seizures in a refractory epilepsy population is difficult. From among these 9 events in 9 patients they were judged as “moderate” in 7 patients, “mild” in one patient and “severe” in one patient. In the case judged as severe, the investigator judged the event as “unrelated” to study drug. All recovered without sequelae. There was no systematic temporal relationship to seizure occurrence with a range from study day 1 to study day 201 with a somewhat even dispersion through this interval. The absence of a temporal relationship weakens the likelihood of a causal relationship.
L597: Rash: Rash was among the most frequent AE preferred terms leading to discontinuation. This was in part, driven by the heightened pharmacovigilance for hypersensitivity reactions included in the design of this study. There were 9 events from 9 patients. Three events were judged by the investigator as moderate in severity and 6 were judged as mild. All are entered as “recovered/resolved”.
L598: Headache: There were 6 AE's with the preferred term headache leading to discontinuation. Three events are judged as “moderate” by the investigator, 2 are judged as “mild” and one was judged as “severe” The narrative for the patient (YKP3089C021-[REDACTED](b) (6)) with entry as “severe” was examined. The narrative reveals the patient has a background history of headache. The event began on day 1 of 25mg cenobamate, study day 14. The headache was associated with nausea and vomiting. Cenobamate was discontinued on study day 18 and the patient recovered from headache 14 days after discontinuation. *Causality is probable in this “severe” case due to the temporal relationship to escalation of cenobamate dose.*
L599: ## Preferred Terms of Interest
L600: The remaining preferred terms are examined for entries of interest due to potential serious, previously unexplored disorders, or due to potential relationship indication of DRESS. These include one presentation each for “dyspnea”, “cardiac failure congestive”, “pyrexia”, “tachyphrenia”, “thrombocytosis”, “vomiting”, “intentional overdose” and “abdominal pain”.
L601: YKP3089C021-[REDACTED](b) (6) (PT dyspnea): 53-year-old Hispanic male, on study day 7 the subject experienced the mild AE of anxiety, on study day 8 the subject experienced the mild AEs of heartburn and shortness of breath, and on study day 13 the subject experienced the mild AE of blurred vision. On study day 16, while taking 25 mg of study drug, the subject experienced the mild AEs of palpitations of chest, headache, and numbness of face. On study day 56, while taking 50 mg of study drug, the subject experienced the mild AE of nasal pressure. On study day 56 study drug dose was increased to 100 mg, further increased to 200 mg on study day 69. On study day 82, study drug dose was decreased to 150 mg. On study day 94 study drug dose was increased to 200 mg. The subject self-reduced dose to 150 mg on study day 108, and on study day 120 the subject self- reduced dose to 100 mg. The study drug was withdrawn, and the last dose was taken on study day 130. On study day 134, the subject recovered from the events of anxiety, heartburn, shortness of breath, blurred vision, palpitations of chest, headache, numbness of face, and nasal pressure, see Figure 35. *Causal Assessment: This sequence of adverse events may be related to anxiety or uncertain mechanisms of intolerance. The self-reduction of dose beginning at day 108 supports persistent intolerance. The narrative indicates recovery on study day 134 which supports a dechallenge response. There does not appear to be a serious underlying threatening pathophysiology driving this array of symptoms.*

L603: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT Cardiac failure congestive): 65-year-old Caucasian male study day 17 the subject experienced the moderate AE of worsening of congestive heart failure, and on study day 23 the subject experienced the mild AE of first-degree AV block. No treatment was reported. The study drug was withdrawn due to the events and the last dose was taken on study day 22. L604: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT Pyrexia): 44-year-old Hispanic male, (study day 1), the subject experienced the mild AE of fever. Specific temperature was not reported. Treatment for the event was not reported. On study day 3, the subject recovered from the event. The study drug was withdrawn due to the event and the last dose was taken on study day 14. The narrative indicates that the patient was started on 12.5mg cenobamate on day 1. The narrative indicates the patient continued cenobamate until day 14, however, the exposure dataset for study C021 does not indicate any drug treatment. *Causal assessment: The relationship of cenobamate to fever is uncertain, in part, because of the divergence between dosing identified in the narrative compared to the exposure dataset. It is possible the patient developed a fever following one dose but there is no associated report of physical or laboratory examination abnormality and no*
L605: *sequela reported.*
L606: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT Tachyphrenia): a 50-year-old, Caucasian female. On study day 20, 6 days after transition from 12.5mg to 25mg cenobamate, the subject experienced the mild AE of mind racing, and the moderate AE of anxiety. Treatment for the events was not reported. On the same day the subject recovered from the event of mind racing. On study day 28 while still on 25mg, the study drug was withdrawn. *Causal Association: The patient has a history of "psychosis" related to the introduction of lamotrigine treatment approximately 1 year earlier. The clinical course is uncertain, but the patient was on concomitant lamotrigine 150mg a day at the time of the AE of tachyphrenia. Causality is uncertain, if related, this patient may have a selective vulnerability due to past history of psychosis during lamotrigine treatment.*
L607: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT Abdominal pain): On study day 1 this 27-year-old, Caucasian male experienced the mild AEs of abdominal pain and general malaise. Treatment for the events was not reported. On study day 16 the study drug dose was increased to 25 mg. The study drug was withdrawn due to the events and the last dose was taken on study day 31. *Causal assessment: This event was examined in greater detail due to the identification of 6 entries in study C021 with appendicitis. This event had no further progression beyond abdominal pain. A causal relation to cenobamate is uncertain and this is not a report of appendicitis.*
L608: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT thrombocytosis): 33-year-old Caucasian female study day 273) the subject experienced the severe AE of reactive thrombocytosis. The study drug was withdrawn due to the event and the last dose was taken on study day 272. On study day 308 platelet count was within normal range. Causal Assessment: The patient achieved a dose of 200mg cenobamate on study day 71. Platelet counts averaged over the interval following day 71 was only 12 \times 10^9/L greater than baseline if the outlier value of 643 \times 10^9/L is removed. This post day 71 average includes the value from termination visit that was 2 days after cenobamate discontinuation. This termination visit value was 238 \times 10^9/L lower than the maximum value on study day 266. If cenobamate was the driver of the high platelet count than a decline seen only two days after discontinuation would not be expected due to the long half-life (60hrs) of cenobamate. Overall there appears to be an increase in platelet count related to cenobamate treatment in this patient, but cenobamate is not conclusively related to the high outlier value on study day 266 (see Figure 36).
L609: Figure 36 YKP3089C021-(b)(6) Platelet Count by Study Day (slope= 0.79, R2=0.56)
L610: Two patients (YKP3089C021-(b)(6), YKP3089C021-(b)(6)) with AE preferred terms of “vomiting” leading to discontinuation were examined. These events were judged mild and moderate by the investigator. Examination of the narrative report does not reveal a threatening level of severity.
L611: Two patients (YKP3089C021-(b)(6), YKP3089C021-(b)(6)), with a preferred term entry of “intentional overdose” are examined further to determine if an AE of “suicide attempt” was also present in the adverse event dataset. In both cases the “intentional overdose” was also captured as a suicide attempt.
L612: **Reviewer Comment:** Discontinuations in study C021 provide insight into potentially significant adverse event among a large sample of cenobamate treated patients with the reduced dose escalation schedule. The most frequent adverse events are common for Antiepilepsy drugs. These include those associated with altered neurotransmission such as “somnolence”, “dizziness”, and “ataxia”. There is no unexpected safety signal in the frequency of these events, and they are considered in labeling. The frequency of “seizure” and “epilepsy” do not give rise to a signal for ineffective drug or a pro-convulsant effect. The frequency of “headache” and “nausea” indicate potential for difficulty with tolerability in a proportion of patients but do not rise to the level of a serious physiologic threat or broad difficulty with tolerance. Discontinuations in the “Psychiatric disorders” SOC including preferred terms “irritability”, “anxiety”, “suicidal ideation” and “suicide attempt” reveal that cenobamate has some adverse
L613: psychoactive quality. The frequency of discontinuation due to these “psychiatric disorder” SOC adverse events is 1%. The subset of suicide related terms is 0.4%. The frequency of discontinuations due to suicidality terms does not appear out of alignment with current Antiepilepsy drug class labeling for “Suicidal Behavior and Ideation” in Section 5.
L614: Seven selected preferred terms were examined to assess the level of threat represented by the adverse event. Three of the select preferred term events, “dyspnea”, “abdominal pain” and “vomiting” are present in the package insert, adverse reactions table. The remaining 4 terms “cardiac failure”, “tachyphrenia”, “thrombocytosis”, and “intention overdose” do not reach a level of evidence to support a new cenobamate specific safety signal.
L615: ## 8.4.5. Significant Adverse Events
L616: ## Appendicitis
L617: The average exposure per patient in the ISS 120-day safety exposure dataset is 1.578 years per patient. This was the average exposure for 1945 patients who received cenobamate in the pivotal studies and their open label extensions and study C021. This estimate yields 3070 patient years of total exposure. There were 9 cases of appendicitis identified in the same safety dataset, see Table 28. This yields a frequency of 2.9 cases of appendicitis / 1000 patient years of exposure. This is greater than the reported background incidence of 1.1/1000 population per year based on the literature-based appendicitis risk assessment below.
L618: The mean and median age of patients that experienced appendicitis was 35.5 and 36 years respectively with a range of 26 to 44 years. Sixty six percent of patients were male while 33 percent were female, all but one was Caucasian. This age distribution is similar to the distribution of the cenobamate treatment population as a whole where the mean and median ages were 39.5 and 38 respectively. The racial distribution is also in alignment with the cenobamate treatment population where 78.5 percent were Caucasian. There is a predominance of males in the appendicitis group compared to the distribution of the cenobamate treatment population where 49.7 percent of patients were female.

## 7 Patients with Appendicitis
L624: ## LITERATURE RISK ASSESSMENT
L625: Lifetime risk 6-9%11 overall incidence of appendicitis in the United States, 1.1 per 1,000 population per year12
L626: **Reviewer Comment:** The analysis of appendicitis frequency reveals a signal for an increase over the background population rate. A concurrent analysis of serum globulin (Figure 37) reveals a trend of decline in globulin five of seven patients in study C021 that developed appendicitis. There is no clearly vulnerable subpopulation. Identification of this signal in labeling is recommended, see Section 10.1, Prescription Drug Labeling.
L627: ## *Hyponatremia*
L628: Controlled study data reveals a dose related frequency of sodium values in the laboratory dataset with separation from placebo. All instances are confounded by concomitant oxcarbazepine or carbamazepine. However, there is separation from placebo, yet the background confounding medications are constant across placebo and treatment. The observed separation from placebo is supportive of an independent effect of cenobamate on sodium values but is not sufficiently clear to enter into labeling.
L629: ## *Hyperkalemia*
L630: There was a signal for post baseline elevation of potassium identified in both studies C013 and C017. There was notable excess of high post baseline potassium values with cenobamate treatment. In study C017 there was an excess of high outlier potassium values in the cenobamate treatment arm over the placebo arm. There was also a higher frequency of post baseline shift from normal to OORR high in the cenobamate arm. In study C017 there was an excess of high baseline potassium values in study C017, however, the post baseline distribution of OORR high values was dose related with an increasing frequency of high values as cenobamate dose increased. The laboratory abnormalities subsection of section 6 should reflect that OORR high potassium values were in excess of placebo in a dose related distribution, occurring in 5.6%, 8.3%, 9.1% and 10.8% of the placebo, 100mg, 200mg, and 400mg treatment arms respectively, with a maximum value of 5.9meq/L.
L631: 11 2. Flum DR, Koepsell TD. Evaluating diagnostic accuracy in appendicitis using administrative data. Journal of Surgical Research 2005;**123**(2):257-61 doi: 10.1016/j.jss.2004.08.020[published Online First: Epub Date].
L632: 12 1. Addiss DG, Shaffer N, Fowler BS, Tauxe RV. THE EPIDEMIOLOGY OF APPENDICITIS AND APPENDECTOMY IN THE UNITED STATES. American Journal of Epidemiology 1990;**132**(5):910-25 doi: 10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a115734[published Online First: Epub Date].
L633: ## *Headache*
L634: The ISS 120-day ADAE dataset where all AESDY (*start date relative to 1st dose date*) entries are positive, was examined. This revealed that 297 patients had 509 entries for the preferred term headache.
L635: One headache adverse event entry (YKP3089C021-(b)(6)), on treatment day 72 was an SAE. The narrative report reveals the patient had a past history of headache pre-dating cenobamate treatment by 5 years. The headache event on treatment day 72 was associated with nausea, vomiting, and chest pain. The narrative indicates “cardiac work-up including echocardiogram, all negative”. Cenobamate was discontinued on study day 71. The headache was treated with Fiorinal one tablet PO four times a day for 4 days. On study day 78 the patient experienced a moderate SAE of multiple seizures and was hospitalized. This may represent withdrawal seizure (although the long half-life of cenobamate should allow a physiologic taper).
L636: From among the remaining 508 headache events there were 356 (70%) judged by the investigator as “mild” severity, 142 (28%) were judged as “moderate” severity and 10 were judged as “severe”. The mean and median time to headache AE from start of cenobamate were 330 day and 140 days respectively. Approximately 65% of headache AE occurred by treatment day 270, see Figure 38.

L640: Reviewer Comment: The adverse event of headache is frequent with an occurrence in 15% of cenobamate treated patients. Sixty-five percent (65%) of occurrences were in the first 9 months of cenobamate treatment. Seventy percent are judged as “mild”
L641: severity by investigators and 2% judged as “severe”. There were 9 (0.46%) withdrawals due to headache or associated with headache. In two reports there was a prior history of headache. There is a signal for frequent headache, however the events appear to abate with adaptation to cenobamate treatment and most are self-limited.
L642: ## *Constipation*
L643: There were 116 non-serious TEAE events of “constipation” from 102 (5.2%) patients where 89 (76% of all constipation) events from 82 patient occurred within the first 250 days of cenobamate treatment and 39 (35% of all constipation) events occurred within the first 60 days of treatment, see Figure 39. Ninety events (78% of constipation) were judged by the investigators as mild, 24 were judged by investigators as moderate and 2 were judged as severe.
L644: Figure 39 Studies C013, C017, and C021 DB &amp; OL, Constipation Events by Study Day
L645: A notable event of obstipation possibly resulting from constipation occurred in study C021. There was on SAE of constipation on study day 18 in study C021 (YKP3089C021-(b)(6) A 44-year-old Caucasian male patient experienced the moderate SAE of obstipation and was hospitalized. The patient presented to the emergency medical service due to abdominal pain in epigastrium with propagation into the lumbar region on the left. The patient
L646: had no vomiting, no dysuria, no fever, and had a bowel movement that day. An ultrasound revealed an obstructed pancreas. During hospitalization the patient received analgesic and spasmolytic therapy, diet, and enema. During the enema he evacuated a large amount of stool. The study drug was not changed due to the event. On study day 20, the patient recovered from the event of obstipation and was discharged from the hospital in good condition. Concomitant medications included: Vigantol 2 gtt oral one a day for prevention of osteoporosis, ongoing since [REDACTED](b) (6) and acidum folicum 10 mg oral once a week for mild thrombocytopenia, ongoing since [REDACTED](b) (6). Concomitant AEDs included: clobazam 25 mg oral daily, from [REDACTED](b) (6) to [REDACTED](b) (6), valproate chrono 1000 mg oral twice a day, from [REDACTED](b) (6) to [REDACTED](b) (6), Lamictal 1000 mg oral twice a day, ongoing since [REDACTED](b) (6).
L647: ## Reviewer Comment:
L648: Gastrointestinal disorders were evaluated as to determine if there were events related to constipation. The occurrence of abdominal pain was of potential concern. The co-investigation of “Gastrointestinal disorders” with “constipation” did not reveal any unifying process or family of related adverse events. Constipation was common and there was a single SAE of “obstipation” which was likely related to constipation because it was relieved by an enema. Proposed labeling covers this event in section 6, adverse reaction table.

(b) (6)) has a plausible causal relationship to cenobamate treatment. A causal relationship is remote due to the prolonged interval between the adverse event and start of cenobamate. This long interval allows the potential for multiple intervening variables to influence the psychiatric status of the patient. There was also a suicide attempt on day 183. This event has a plausible temporal relationship to cenobamate treatment.

L659: The overall frequency in the ISS safety dataset (n=1945) for the SOC “psychiatric disorders” is examined. There were 358 events in this SOC from 272 (14%) patients. A causal relationship to cenobamate treatment is supported by a close temporal relationship. The decay curve of total events over time is shown in Figure 40. This analysis reveals that approximately 60 percent of the “Psychiatric disorders” adverse events occurred in the 1st six months. These are more likely to be causally related, leaving the remaining 40% as less likely related.
L660: **Figure 40 ISS, SOC “psychiatric disorders” Kaplan–Meier Plot of the Proportion of Adverse Event Occurrence by Study Day (day 0 – 1700)**
L661: Events that capture “Suicidal Behavior and Ideation” are examined in the ISS dataset. The preferred terms “completed suicide”, “intentional self-injury”, “suicidal ideation”, and “suicide attempt” capture 39 events from 36 (1.9%) patients. Approximately 50% of these events occur
L662: in the first 6 months and 80% in the first year after beginning cenobamate treatment. The longer the latency to an event the less likely the causal association.
L663: Suicidality terms are examined in the double-blind studies. In study C017 there were 2 suicidality terms, “suicidal ideation” and “suicide attempt”. Three terms from 3 patients (1.8%) appeared in the 100mg treatment arm and 1 term (0.9%) in the 200mg treatment arm with none in placebo. In study C013 the same terms are examined. there was one entry each for “suicidal ideation” in the placebo and cenobamate (200mg) treatment arm yielding a frequency of 0.92% and 0.9% respectively.
L664: The more threatening adverse events that are captured by the preferred terms “aggression”, “agitation”, “anger” and “hostility” are examined for frequency and temporal relationship to the start of cenobamate treatment. There were 36 entries for these terms from 35 (1.8%) patients. Approximately 70% of the events occurred in the 1st six months. These events are more likely to be causally related to treatment. There was one entry for the preferred term “homicidal ideation”. This event occurred at day 1020 (2.8 years). This long latency reduces the likely causal relationship.
L665: The adverse events related to psychosis are captured by the preferred terms “delusion”, “hallucinations”, “hallucination, auditory”, “hallucination, visual”, “Paranoia”, “persecutory delusion”, “Psychotic disorder”, and “thinking abnormal”. There were 28 entries from this preferred term grouping from 18 (0.9%) patients. Approximately 45% of these events occurred in the first six months of cenobamate treatment leaving 65% with a latency greater than 6 months. This latter group has a less likely causal relationship to treatment.
L666: ## Reviewer Comment, Psychiatric Disorders
L667: Examination of the adverse event preferred terms, frequency and temporal distribution across the ISS dataset reveals a suicidality is plausibly related to study drug but does not appear to be out of alignment with the risk identified in current AED class labeling in section 5 for “*Suicidal Behavior and Ideation*”. There is a possible signal for an association between cenobamate treatment and the psychiatric disorder terms aggression, agitation, anger, and hostility. This signal is confounded by the severity of underlying illness as well as potential confounding medications. When a temporal relationship of 6 months is considered supportive the frequency of these events is 1.3%. This frequency does not reach a substantive threshold for additional labeling. The terms for psychosis are less substantive in causal relationship due to low frequency, underlying illness and a temporal relationship that is not tightly coupled in 55% of events.

L698: ## Reviewer Comment, Cardiac Disorders
L699: The fatal outcome events of “cardiac arrest”, “cardiogenic shock” and “myocardial infarction” did not have a temporal relationship to initiation of cenobamate that supports causality. One patient had a background cardiac risk history, a second may have been SUDEP. The third case with the shortest latency from cenobamate start of 1.9 years had no clear cause. Examination of syncope revealed sporadic events was performed across an exposure period from day 9 to 542 involving 0.6% of patients. None of the syncope were entered as an SAE and none had cenobamate discontinued. The remaining analysis of events did not identify a trend of threatening cardiac events with compelling temporal relationship or frequency that identifies a specific cardiac safety signal.
L700: ## *Hepatic Transaminases, Hy's Law*
L701: Examination of study C013 revealed no post baseline ALT or AST values &gt;3 \times ULN. In patients with normal baseline the distribution of OORR high values for ALT and AST both reveal that 7 (6.4%) patients in the placebo arm and 9 (8%) patients in the cenobamate (200mg) arm had at least one post baseline OORR high value. In study C017, from among patients with a normal baseline value, there were 1 (0.9%), 1 (0.9%), 4 (3.6%), and 8 (7.1%) patients with an elevation of post baseline ALT to a value OORR high. There was 1 (0.9%) patient in the 100mg treatment arm, 2 (1.8%) patients in the 200mg treatment arm, and 3 (2.7%) patients in the 400mg treatment arm and none in placebo, with a post baseline elevation to &gt; 3 \times ULN. There was a maximum elevation in patient YKP3089C017-(b)(6) to 7.6 \times ULN at visit 6 (day 43), there was no concurrent elevation in bilirubin. study drug was discontinued on study day 48 with a decline to 4.2 \times ULN at study day 127 and 2.1 \times ULN at study day 162. There were no Hy's law cases.
L702: There were 308 (23.4%) patients with a post baseline maximum value for ALT during the course of the study that were OORR high and were an increase from the baseline value. These 308 values occurred from among 12036 post baseline measurements across study visits 3 to 14 and represent 2.6% of measurements. There were 42 instances from 32 (1.6%) patients with ALT &gt; 3 \times ULN. The extreme outliers are examined. A trend of sustained and sequential elevation of ALT values is generally not present. There were no Hy's law cases in the C021 laboratory dataset and no signal for sustained and sequential escalation of ALT.
