L1594: ## 8.9.3. Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1595: N/A Pediatric populations will be addressed in post marketing by PREA requirements
L1596: ## 8.9.4. Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L1597: There were no intentional overdoses of cenobamate.
L1598: Potential withdrawal effects were explored in the phase 1 studies, unconfounded by concomitant antiepilepsy drugs, see 8.5 Phase 1 Safety. The temporal relationship and the implied medial physiology of several preferred terms identified during the end of treatment interval are consistent with a cenobamate withdrawal effect. The terms most likely to have a causal relationship are “insomnia”, “anxiety”, “depressed mood”, “nightmare”, “decreased appetite”, “tremor”, “body temperature increased”, “chills”, “heart rate increased”, “nervousness”, “nightmare”, and “tachycardia”.
L1599: ## 8.10. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1600: ## 8.10.1. Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L1601: No Postmarketing experience is available
L1602: ## 8.10.2. Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1603: ## 8.10.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L1604: ## 8.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1605: **Exposure**; see Section 8.2.1, Overall Exposure
L1606: Examination of safety dataset demographics reveals the set is well balance by sex. Race and ethnicity representation is in alignment with the US population for “WHITE” and “HISPANIC OR LATINO” but the “BLACK OR AFRICAN AMERICAN” subset is under represented.
L1607: The clinical studies were adequate in frequency of safety assessments and capture of safety metrics including physical examination, vital signs, ECG, laboratory investigations including hematology and clinical chemistry. Potential confounding variables of past medical history and concomitant medications were also recorded. Required clinical case narratives were provided as well as case report forms.
L1608: A primary safety concern present at the outset of this NDA review was DRESS risk. DRESS emerged during development just after the end of phase 2 meeting (EOP2). Within a month a fatality of a healthy volunteer was identified in the thorough QT study, due to eosinophilic myocarditis that developed during DRSS syndrome. A partial clinical hold was placed on 11/25/15 for new subject enrolment and subjects that had been treated for less than 4 months. On 2/26/16 partial hold was continued with the condition that new subject enrollment could resume but maximum cenobamate dose could not exceed 200mg. On 6/18/16 partial clinical hold was continued with allowance that a maximum dose of 400mg was allowed. During this interval study C021 that had been initiated on 11/9/15 was modified under discussion with the Division, to assess strategies to reduce DRESS risk and assure an acceptable risk boundary. An agreement was reached that a sample size of 1200 patient with exposure for at least 6 months
L1609: would be adequate to cap the DRESS risk at 0.3% if the upper bound 95% confidence interval of DRESS frequency during the study was demonstrated to be 0.3%. This condition could only be met if no DRESS cases emerged in study C021 (point estimate 0, with upper bound 95% CI = 0.3%).
L1610: A core strategy to reduce DRESS risk in study C021 was a reduced titration schedule compared to the phase 1 and pivotal studies. The initial dose begins at 12.5mg and does not escalate for 2 weeks. After 2 weeks the dose is escalated to 25mg for an additional 2 weeks. To view the complete, modified, reduced schedule see Section 8.6.1 DRESS Risk. In addition, patients were informed of the risk and given a laminated card that was to be carried. This card contained information on potential DRESS features as well as instructions when to go to an emergency room. Subsequently, in study C021 there were 1339 patients enrolled where 804 were treated for 6 months or longer and 306 were treated for 1 year or longer. There were no further DRESS cases identified. Due to the increased pharmacovigilance patients that developed rash or other features of suspicion were withdrawn from the study resulting in 66 withdrawals.
L1611: Examination of the safety dataset did reveal hypersensitivity events could occur early and with substantial severity. This was seen where 4 patients / subjects developed substantive hypersensitive features on day 1 or day 2 of cenobamate dosing, see Rapid Onset Hypersensitivity Events.
L1612: Examination of the distribution of adverse event SOCs by demographic subsets revealed a higher frequency of events in the “neurologic disorders” in females compared to males. There was also an excess of events in the SOC “General disorders and administration site conditions” and “Gastrointestinal disorders” in the “BLACK OR AFRICAN AMERICAN” subgroup compared to “WHITES” and “ASIANS”. However, the “BLACK OR AFRICAN AMERICAN” population is small and may not provide an accurate representation of adverse events compared to larger populations.
L1613: QT shortening was identified in the safety evaluation of cenobamate. Formal cardiac ECG studies demonstrated shortening of the QT interval (mean = 16 msec, for doses &gt;400 mg daily) with cenobamate. In a placebo-controlled study of the QT interval (study number YKP3089C020), a higher percentage of cenobamate-treated subjects (31% at 200 mg and 66% at 500 mg) had a QT shortening of greater than 20 ms compared to placebo (6-17%). Reductions of the QTc interval below 300 ms were not observed. The magnitude of the QTc shortening is similar to that associated with rufinamide. Similar language for labeling will be used.
L1614: Adverse events in the SOC “nervous system disorders” were the most frequent in the safety dataset and are dose-related and very notably separate from placebo at the 400mg dose, Figure 42. Examination of the ISS reveals the most common preferred terms within this SOC are “dizziness”, “somnolence”, and “balance disorder” (headache in 3rd place is discussed separately). In the overall phase 2-3 dataset 1.9% of patient had discontinuation of cenobamate
L1615: for those three preferred term adverse events. There were 12 patients with these same adverse events, “dizziness”, “somnolence”, and “balance disorder”, that were entered as an SAE, although none of these was withdrawn. Expressed as a proportion of patients that experience the top three “nervous system disorder” events, those who were discontinued represent 4.8% and those with SAEs represent 1.5%. This analysis reveals that a low frequency, &lt;5%, of these common adverse events interfered with the course of cenobamate treatment.
L1616: Additional issues that emerged on broader review of the cenobamate safety data are QT shortening, appendicitis, hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, headache, constipation, psychiatric events, cardiac adverse events, hepatic transaminase adverse events, and hypophosphatemia. As review progressed and potential issues were identified, they were added to Section 8.4.5, Significant Adverse Events for final adjudication.
L1617: As the totality of the NDA safety information was integrated appendicitis does have a frequency that is higher than the background rate. There was no correlation identified with other potentially coexisting or synergistic physiology such as frequency of overall infections, leukocytes or neutrophils. There was an association with a decline in globulin in a few cases, but no clear informative correlations emerged.
L1618: There was a clear post-baseline high frequency of hyponatremia seen in clinical studies. However, further exploration revealed that all instances were confounded by concomitant treatment with oxcarbazepine or carbamazepine. There was separation between placebo and cenobamate treatment arms in the controlled study data. However, the confounding by medications with a high frequency of hyponatremia causes difficulty in determine the magnitude of separation from placebo that is sufficient to allow a conclusion that an independent effect of cenobamate is present.
L1619: There were few adverse event entries for the term “hyperkalemia” but as assessment of the clinical laboratory data proceeded, an excess of post baseline OORR high potassium values was identified. The frequency of these events is sufficient to add an entry for “hyperkalemia” or increased potassium to section 6 of labeling.
L1620: Headache is a notable adverse effect of cenobamate due to the frequency of this event rather than the severity or medical threat. There were 460 instances from 280 (14.4%) patients where only one was entered as an SAE. From among the 460 instances 72% were identified as “mild” by the investigator, 26% as “moderate and 2.4% as severe. The frequency of headache is of similar magnitude to central nervous system adverse effects seen in cenobamate treatment.
L1621: Constipation emerged in review as an event of interest due to the high frequency. The co-investigation of “Gastrointestinal disorders” with “constipation” did not reveal any unifying process or family of related adverse events. Constipation was common and there was a single SAE of “obstipation” which was likely related to constipation because it was relieved by an enema. Proposed labeling covers this event in section 6, adverse reaction table.
L1622: Psychiatric disorders reached a threshold of increased concern in review due to the identification of several adverse events of “psychotic disorder” identified in the review of adverse events. These events are high impact, disruptive events for patient, family and / or caregiver. There were also two completed suicides and one particularly violent suicide attempt through self-inflicted stab wounds. There is a potential signal for an association of cenobamate with “psychiatric disorder” preferred terms, discussed in Section 8.4.5 Psychiatric. The totality of the safety review reveals that due to the severity of background illness (refractory epilepsy, post ictal phenomena) and potentially confounding concomitant medications, the frequency of the psychiatric adverse events do not reach a substantive threshold for additional labeling.
L1623: Cardiac adverse events emerged as an area of focus due to the identification in “deaths” of fatal outcome events of “cardiac arrest”, “cardiogenic shock” and “myocardial infarction”. One patient had a background cardiac risk history, a second may have been SUDEP. The third case with the shortest latency from cenobamate start of 1.9 years had no clear cause. In addition, a bifascicular block and a complete AV block were seen in the dataset. There were also 10 patients with a syncopal event. The patient with bifascicular block was found to be on placebo. The patient with complete heart block did not have a strong temporal relation to cenobamate initiation, it began on day 185 and it was confounded by concomitant treatment with lacosamide. Examination of syncope revealed sporadic events across an exposure period from day 9 to 542 involving 0.6% of patients. None of the syncope were entered as an SAE and none had cenobamate discontinued. Overall, the analysis of events did not identify a trend of threatening cardiac events with compelling temporal relationship or a sufficiently high frequency to conclude there is a cenobamate specific cardiac safety signal.
L1624: Hepatic transaminases: Review of the adverse events and laboratory dataset did not reveal a high frequency or severity of ALT elevation. There was a dose related increase of OORR high values in study C017, several of these events were &gt; 3 x ULN, also with a dose related frequency. There were 32 (1.7%) patients with 42 instances of ALT &gt; 3 x ULN in the safety dataset but there was a single outlier with an elevation to 7.6 x ULN with no increase in bilirubin. There were no Hy's law cases. Overall, there was no severe trend detected for sequential, sustained and frequent highly increased values of ALT or AST. There was a modest signal, however, based on the dose related laboratory event in study C017 and an occurrence of &gt; 3 x ULN in 1.7% of cenobamate treatment in the ISS.
L1625: Due to 3 patient with and adverse entry of “visual impairment” there was exploration of the safety database for a visual dysfunction signal. Most instances were associated with central nervous system event that may result in a non-specific experience of visual disturbance. Overall, examination of terms potentially related to visual acuity dysfunction did not reveal a signal for a cenobamate associated effect on visual acuity.
L1626: Evaluation for seizure worsening related to cenobamate treatment there was no evidence of a pattern consistent with a seizure worsening effect.
L1627: ## Integrated Assessment, Summary:
L1628: The primary safety concern identified with cenobamate treatment is the risk of DRESS syndrome. The potential risk is mitigated with slow dose escalation during titration. The zero frequency of DRESS, sample size, and duration of exposure offer assurance that DRESS risk is in a range similar to the occurrence of serious skin reactions seen with lamotrigine treatment, a prominent AED in the therapeutic armamentarium. Cenobamate does demonstrate a high propensity toward hypersensitive response; this is seen in the safety dataset, where there were cases of rapid onset of features after a few doses. It is uncertain if these and other hypersensitivity reactions that resulted in patient withdrawal from study C021 forecast DRESS syndrome.
L1629: QTc shortening was identified and evaluated by a TQT study. The adverse event profile did not reveal a signal for potentially related events. This will be entered into labeling in Section 5.
L1630: An uncommon adverse event of “appendicitis” was observed. There is no strong temporal relationship for these events but there is a notable increase in frequency over the expected background rate. A signal for adverse neuropsychiatric events may be present, but the totality of evidence for attribution to cenobamate is inconclusive, and the most severe of these adverse reactions, suicide and suicidality is present as a class warning for AEDs. Also identified in the safety review were hyperkalemia and hyponatremia. There was a modest signal for increase ALT seen in a dose related ALT increase in controlled study data, 32 (1.7%) patients with 42 instances of ALT &gt; 3 x ULN in Phase 2-3 study exposure, and a single serious outlier of increased ALT without an alternative explanation. Among common adverse events, there were features of CNS dysfunction including dizziness, somnolence and balance disorder often seen in AED treatment.
L1631: Important negatives include the absence of a Hy's law case. There was a trend of a decline in leukocytes and neutrophils, but when followed over time these tended to plateau and did not reach critical levels. There was no evidence of seizure worsening.
L1632: In section 5 there will be labeling additions of QTc Shortening. The reviewer is recommending an addition of hyperkalemia to a “Laboratory Abnormalities” subsection of Section 6. The trend of OORR high ALT seen in study C017 as well as the single severe outlier value are recommended for addition to the “Laboratory abnormalities” subsection. Addition of a “gastrointestinal disorders” heading in section 6 to contain “appendicitis” is also recommended, see Section 10.1, Prescription Drug Labeling.
L1633: ## 9. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L1634: None planned
L1635: ## 10. Labeling Recommendations
L1636: ## 10.1. Prescription Drug Labeling
L1637: ## 10.1.1. QT Shortening
L1638: ## 4 CONTRAINDICATIONS
L1639: Cenobamate is contraindicated in patients with Familial Short QT syndrome [see Warnings and Precautions (5.x)].
L1640: ## Section 5.x QT Shortening
L1641: Formal cardiac ECG studies demonstrated shortening of the QT interval (mean = 16 msec, for doses &gt;400 mg daily) with cenobamate. In a placebo-controlled study of the QT interval, a higher percentage of TRADENAME-treated subjects (31% at 200 mg and 66% at 500 mg) had a QT shortening of greater than 20 ms compared to placebo (6-17%). Reductions of the QTc interval below 300 ms were not observed. The degree of QT shortening induced by cenobamate is without any known clinical risk. Familial Short QT syndrome is associated with an increased risk of sudden death and ventricular arrhythmias, particularly ventricular fibrillation. Such events in this syndrome are believed to occur primarily when the corrected QT interval falls below 300 msec. Non-clinical data also indicate that QT shortening is associated with ventricular fibrillation Patients with Familial Short QT syndrome should not be treated with cenobamate. Caution should be used when administering cenobamate with other drugs that shorten the QT interval [see Contraindications (4)].
L1642: ## 10.1.2. Laboratory Abnormalities
L1643: ## *Hepatic Transaminases*
L1644: There was 1 (0.9%) patient in the 100mg treatment arm, 2 (1.8%) patients in the 200mg treatment arm, and 3 (2.7%) patients in the 400mg treatment arm and none in placebo, with a post baseline ALT elevation to &gt; 3 x ULN. In the 400mg treatment arm the maximum ALT elevation was 7.6 X ULN.
L1645: ## *Potassium*
L1646: There was an excess of potassium values greater than 5meq/L (upper reference range) observed in pivotal studies. In study1 there were 8 (7%) patients in the placebo arm and 17 (17%) patients in the cenobamate arm with normal baseline values with at least one post baseline maximum value &gt;5meq/L. In study 2 there was a dose related distribution where at
L1647: least one post baseline potassium value was &gt;5meq/L in a dose related distribution, occurring in 5.6%, 8.3%, 9.1% and 10.8% of the placebo, 100mg, 200mg, and 400mg treatment arms respectively. Two patients had a maximum value of 5.9meq/L.

L1650: ## *Gastrointestinal disorders:*
L1651: There was an incidence of appendicitis in the overall clinical trial safety population of 2.9 cases of appendicitis / 1000 patient years of exposure that is in excess of the expected background rate in the general population.

L1665: ## 13.1. Exposure Calculation
L1666: Total exposure is calculated using the ISS 120 day safety update ADEX.xpt dataset. Placebo only exposures are excluded. This leaves 13,567 rows for 1946 Patients. A subset table is created for the 1945 entries for the variable EXDUR. The value for these entries is added to obtain 1138702 days. To exclude placebo exposure days in the double-blind interval of studies C013 and C017 all entries for the PARAMCD entry EXDUR1 where the entry for the variable TRT01A is "placebo" are identified. The value of all entries with these properties are added. This yields a value of 18138 days. This value is subtracted from the sum of all EXDUR values to yield 1120564 days. This value is divided by 365 to yield 3070 years of exposure across all 1945 phase 2 study patients.
L1667: ## 13.2. Study C021 – Narrative Summaries
L1668: ## 13.2.1. SAE Narrative Summaries
L1669: ## Study C021 SAE Key Case Narratives
L1670: ## YKP3089C021- [REDACTED] (b) (6) Encephalitis
L1671: The patient experienced drowsiness, generalized weakness, headache and lumbago. On study day 119, the patient experienced the severe, life threatening, disabling, SAE of meningoencephalitis and was hospitalized. The family of the patient found the patient in bed, drowsy and with poor response to verbal stimuli or mobilization. The patient was taken to the emergency room. Her blood pressure was 128/84 mmHg, pulse 78, respiratory rate 19, somnolence, normal pupilar reflex, weakness in four extremities. Laboratory testing two days later revealed: creatinine 2.19 mg/dL, urea 72 mg/dL, BUN 32, DHL 1073, AST 322 U/L, ALT 167 U/L, GGT 145 U/L, total bilirubin 45 mg/dL, direct bilirubin 2.9 mg/dL, metabolic acidosis, pH 7.38 HCO3 11.2. Computed tomography of head was normal. The patient was treated with hydration and nasogastric tube. On 14 Dec-2017 the patient had stupor and clear data of meningeal irritation with Kernig and Brudzinski sign positive, thus puncture lumbar was performed the same day and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) was reported with glucose level 10 mg/dL (serum glucose was unknown but apparently was normal) and protein level 600 mg/dL.
L1672: Five days after hospitalization, the patient had cardio respiratory arrest and required mechanical ventilation support and aminergic support, and a central line was placed. The patient was transferred to general surgery service. Another lumbar puncture was made on [REDACTED] (b) (6) and CSF protein level was elevated, and glucose level was low. WBC was 2-3. CSF Ziehl-Neelsen stain, BAAR, Chinese ink staining gene expert and bacteriologic culture were negative on [REDACTED] (b) (6). The patient had ventilator associated pneumonia and was treated with Imipenem (doses not provided). Aminergic and ventilator support were withdrawn on [REDACTED] (b) (6) but there was no improvement in neurological state being found with minimally consciousness state. The study drug was withdrawn due to the event and the last dose was taken on [REDACTED] (b) (6). The patient was discharged from the hospital and recovered with sequelae from the event of meningoencephalitis on [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L1673: ## YKP3089C021- [REDACTED] (b) (6) Herpes zoster
L1674: Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) a 56-year-old Caucasian female, was diagnosed with epilepsy in [REDACTED] (b) (6). The subject was screened and signed informed consent on [REDACTED] (b) (6), with current seizure types simple partial without motor and complex partial, for participation in “An Open Label, Multicenter, Safety and Pharmacokinetic Study of YKP3089 as Adjunctive Therapy in Subjects with Partial Onset Seizures.”
L1675: Relevant medical and surgical history included: left frontal corticectomy on [REDACTED] (b) (6) insertion of seizure prediction device on [REDACTED] (b) (6) and removal on [REDACTED] (b) (6) depression ongoing since [REDACTED] (b) (6) insomnia ongoing since [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L1676: Concomitant medications included: escitalopram 40 mg oral once a day for depression, ongoing since [REDACTED] (b) (6). Concomitant AED included: levetiracetam 1000 mg oral twice a day, ongoing since [REDACTED] (b) (6) oxcarbazepine 1200 mg oral once a day, ongoing since [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L1677: The first dose of YKP3089 12.5 mg was initiated on [REDACTED] (b) (6). On [REDACTED] (b) (6), the dose was increased to 25 mg which was further increased to 50 mg on [REDACTED] (b) (6). The dose was up-titrated to 100 mg on [REDACTED] (b) (6) further titrated to 150 mg on [REDACTED] (b) (6). On [REDACTED] (b) (6) the dose was increased to 200 mg. Since [REDACTED] (b) (6) the subject was receiving 250 mg.
L1678: On [REDACTED] (b) (6) (study day 296) the subject experienced the moderate serious adverse event of herpes zoster infection (ie. shingles) and was hospitalized on [REDACTED] (b) (6). On [REDACTED] (b) (6) subject experienced rash to left side of face which was diagnosed as herpes zoster. On [REDACTED] (b) (6) subject was admitted from home to the hospital with VZV encephalitis and deranged liver function studies. Abdominal ultrasound showed cholelithiasis with a stone lodged in the gallbladder neck, no cholecystitis, mild hepatic steatosis. Chest x-ray showed moderately expanded lungs which appeared clear, no effusion, pneumothorax or pneumoperitoneum. CT brain showed no acute abnormality. The subject was treated with intravenous acyclovir 800 mg three times a day, oxycodone 2.5 mg oral as needed, nystatin 1 ml oral four times a day, pregabalin 25 mg oral twice a day, refresh liquid gel eye lubricant as needed, prochlorperazine maleate 5 mg oral three times a day. The study drug was not changed due to the event. On [REDACTED] (b) (6) the subject recovered with sequelae from the event and was discharged home to continue intravenous acyclovir via PICC line.
L1679: YKP3089C021- [REDACTED] (b) (6)
L1680: On an unknown day in [REDACTED] (b) (6), (study day 102, estimated), the subject experienced the moderate SAE of psychosis with suspected delusions. The subject called the investigator on study day 193 and reported that he had been hospitalized since study day 191 due to the psychosis. According to the discharge summary, the family had noticed changes in the subject according to the police. He had frequently voiced the suspicion that he was being poisoned and that the family has evil intentions. He had offered to commit suicide for his family. He stated that he was not suicidal and only wanted to offer to commit suicide in order to ascertain the true intentions of the family. He wanted to live. All of this was caused by suspicious or overdue WhatsApp messages from family members. Subsequently, the family had notified the emergency medical services. The subject reported that he repeatedly felt for some time that he cannot rely on his family. He was afraid of being poisoned and had the feeling that a conspiracy was planned against him.
L1681: The subject had quit his job as an IT specialist in February. He stated that he had no problems with his work but complained about the overall environment being “too policial (sic).”
L1682: The subject reported that he had been taking a study medication since the fall but was uncertain about whether it helped to improve his condition. However, there was a conspicuous temporal correlation between the intake of the study medication, the development of the psychotic syndrome as well as the termination of employment. The efficacy of the study medication was inconclusive, an additional three questionable nocturnal seizures were reported to have occurred. In the right temporal epileptic zone, dialeptic as well as nocturnal generalized tonic-clonic and hypermotor/automotor seizures were reported, which occurred less than 1x per month.
L1683: The subject had a positive attitude toward the offer of inpatient. Acutely dangerous situations did not occur. He went back home to get his belongings and the antiepileptics and then returned to the hospital. The study medication was initially discontinued because of its questionable effect and its temporal correlation to the occurrence of the psychosis. In addition, we administered 2x 0.5 mg lorazepam. The medical history from his family included psychotic symptomatology associated with delusion of injury persisting for months. He believes there was a conspiracy against him and his mother is poisoning him. He had no aggressions, no suicide attempts and no suicidal threats. Psychopathological findings on admission: The subject was alert, fully conscious, fully oriented. He had mild concentration problems. Apprehension mildly impaired. Formal thinking mildly slowed down. Moderately systematized and highly dynamic delusion of reference and delusions of injury, no hallucinations. No depersonalization disorders. Euthymic mood. Drive was normal. Silent psychomotor function. He distances himself clearly from suicidality, no aggressive behavior against others. During the inpatient stay, successive worsening of the psychotic symptoms associated with delusions of persecution was observed, which by now seemed to dominate his actions. This was evidenced, for example, by the fact that members of the nursing staff were now part of his delusional experiences and he believed being poisoned by the medications of the hospital. Clearly disorganized and partially bizarre behavior. The study drug was discontinued due to the event of “psychosis” and the last dose was taken on study day 190.
L1684: On the morning of [REDACTED](b)(6) while no longer taking YKP3089 and still hospitalized, the subject experienced the severe SAE of suicide attempt. The subject was increasingly tense and irritated. At the doctor's visit, he requested to be discharged immediately. After the urgent need to continue treatment was discussed with the subject, the subject was informed that a judicial hearing may otherwise be required to make a decision on this. The subject agreed to remain in the ward. Approximately 30 minutes later, the nursing staff was notified by another patient that the subject was found in his bed in a reclined position with a knife in his hand and bleeding severely. MRI performed on [REDACTED](b)(6) assessment: No indication of cerebral abnormalities. CT polytrauma on [REDACTED](b)(6): multiple stab wounds including right cervical without injury of the cervical vessels, one in the upper middle abdomen extending to the intraperitoneal cavity, two stab wounds in the liver without evidence of active bleeding, stab
L1685: injury in the right upper abdomen with herniation of fat tissue and small mesenteric vessels in the stab canal. Suspected injury of the inferior epigastric artery without indication of active bleeding. 26 mm size, arterial hypervascular hepatic lesion in segment VI, adenoma suspected. Complementary Primovist assisted MRI recommended.
L1686: The subject received emergency care and was transferred via the shock room to the emergency surgery department. The physical examination revealed approx. 10 abdominal stab wounds, 3 stab wounds on the right side of the neck, a 20cm long slash wound on the left forearm and a 5cm long slash wound on the right forearm. The CT procedure revealed liver injury as well as injury of an abdominal vessel, which is why the colleagues in General Surgery performed exploratory laparoscopy with subsequent drainage placement. Following surgical treatment, the intubated patient was transferred to the anesthesia intensive care unit. Psychiatric assessment was again performed after extubation. When the subject was asked about the events in the morning, he stated not remembering anything. He denied injuring himself with suicidal intentions. During the discussion, subject displayed parathymic laughter multiple times, he appeared distrustful and demanded the immediate termination of the discussion and indicated he will now talk only to a lawyer. The patient also did not agree to the continuation of intensive care treatment. Because of the remaining threat of self-aggression and the possibility of aggression against others, protective restraints were discussed with the colleagues from anesthesia. As a result of the restraint, the subject was extremely tense and pulled his CVC. The officers of the municipal standards office issued a warrant to take him into police custody. The subject was subsequently housed as per judicial decision at the PsychKG until [redacted] (b) (6) because the subject was not able to control himself due to the psychotic symptoms associated with the misjudgment of people and situations as well as the most likely delusionally motivated suicide attempt.
L1687: The subject was subsequently transferred to our protected hospital and continued to be distrustful and in transparent when communicating with him. On anxiolytic therapy with diazepam, the subject's psychomotor activity decreased significantly. We initiated an antipsychotic medication therapy consisting of amisulpride and later switched the medication to Haldol 3mg/d. On these drugs, the subject was increasingly more approachable and less distrustful. After the expiration of the judicial decision, [redacted] agreed to continue voluntary inpatient treatment. Close contact to the family of the patient showed as well that the patient had achieved the "previous state." In direct contact, the patient continued to demonstrate prolonged response latency. The subject was discharged from the hospital on study day 259 and recovered/resolved with sequelae from the events of psychosis with suspected delusions and suicide attempt.
L1688: ## *Psychiatric Disorders*
L1689: YKP3089C021- [redacted] (b) (6) (PT-psychotic disorder): a 42 yo Caucasian female developed paranoid
L1690: delusions study day 11 at a cenobamate dose of 12.5mg daily. This continued despite treatment with aripiprazole. On study day 13 the cenobamate dose was increased to 25mg. On study day 18 the patient experienced worsened psychosis and was hospitalized. At admission the patient was physically and verbally aggressive, confrontational and paranoid. Paranoid ideation continued to worsen. Cenobamate was discontinued on study day 21. On study day 24 an EEG revealed bitemporal cortical irritability with rare left temporal sharp waves. No subclinical seizures were captured. The patient was discharged from the hospital on study day 37 after treatment with Zyprexa and the psychosis resolved over the subsequent 3 weeks. Causality assessment of psychotic episode: *There was a temporal relationship however, dechallenge is less certain due to initiation of antipsychotic therapy.*
L1691: YKP3089C021-(b) (6): (2 PT's "affective disorder &amp; anxiety disorder) 57-year-old Caucasian female with a history of depression and postpartum depression. On study day 30 at a cenobamate dose of 50mg the patient complained of nightmares that were vivid terrifying dreams of her common law husband hurting her. This worsened over the next few days. She became depressed and weepy. She thought that her common law husband was stealing her money, raping her during seizures and physically abusing her (physical abuse of her by him had occurred in the past). On study day 50 the subject presented to the hospital and was admitted with the diagnosis of "unspecified bipolar 1 disorder, rule out mood disorder due to medical condition. Cenobamate was discontinued. The patient was discharged after 1 day. Treatment continued in the outpatient setting, On study day 58, 10 days after cenobamate was discontinued the patient was treated with risperidone and clonazepam. At last contact, study day 62, that was 14 days after study medication was discontinued the patient was reported "back to normal". *Causality Assessment: This delusional event had a temporal relationship and dechallenge response. Past history of underlying depression may indicate that cenobamate interacts with a background selective vulnerability.*
L1692: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT-Major depression) 38 yo female, examined with a mental status change initially was evaluated in a psychiatric emergency room for a major depressive syndrome (study day 22) and was hospitalized. The patient denied suicidal ideation. Study drug was interrupted (at a dose of 25mg/day based on the ex.xpt file) for a day then resumed at 50mg/day. After this incremental increase in cenobamate the patient developed drowsiness and was again hospitalized on study day 31. On admission the patient was drowsy and had instability when walking. The neurologic exam revealed diplopia and ataxia. These events were noted to coincide with the addition of escitalopram. Escitalopram was stopped, lacosamide and levetiracetam was continued. The study drug was unchanged due to the event. The patient remained free of seizure and side effects and discharged from the hospital on study day 33. The patient subsequently continued cenobamate treatment in study C021 until day 540. *Causality Assessment: There is a temporal relationship to treatment, but the event was manageable indicated by continued treatment. The event is also confounded by concomitant lacosamide.*

Outpatient lab test results on study day 148, 8 days after cessation of cenobamate and dasatinib, revealed subject's hemoglobin improved to 10.9 g/dL, ESR decreased from 110 to 95 mm/h over one week, and the CRP was down to 84.3 mg/L, normal renal function, ALP improved from 191 on (b)(6) to 142 U/L and GGT improved from 663 U/L to 475 U/L. After the discontinuation of cenobamate and dasatinib, the subject was reported to improved clinically. Causal relationship of this serositis is confounded by treatment with dasatinib. Dasatinib has an entry in W&amp;P for severe dermatologic reactions as well as a signal for serositis in Section 6, Clinical trials experience where pleural effusion and pericardial effusion are seen as adverse reactions. An examination of dasatinib in Empirica signal reveals an EB05 of 2.56 for polyserositis. *Causal Assessment: causal relationship to cenobamate is confounded by concomitant therapy with dasatinib, a drug with established signal for serositis.*
L1740: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT Hypovolemic shock): This patient was fatally injured when struck by a motor vehicle. See section 8.4.1.
L1741: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT uterine leiomyoma, endometriosis): 44-year-old Caucasian female developed metrorrhagia with heavy bleeding and abdominal pain on study day 116. This occurs on a background of a two-year history of heavy, continuous and irregular menstruations. On study day 127 the subject experienced the severe SAEs of myoma and external genital endometriosis worsening and was admitted to the hospital for scheduled surgical treatment. On study day 130 the patient underwent a total laparoscopic hysterectomy with bilateral salpingectomy. The study drug was not changed due to the events. *Causal Assessment: No support for causal relationship to cenobamate. Event due to pre-existing condition.*
L1742: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT Intervertebral disc protrusion): 43 yo Caucasian female had diagnosis of massive disc extrusion with herniation at L4/L5 to the left with compression of nerve root. On study day 112 the patient underwent decompression of roots and dural sac L4/5 on the left, and sequestrectomy. The postoperative course was uneventful, standing up since day 1 after surgery, rehabilitation with active therapeutic exercises, walking. Subjectively no radicular pain, felt well, neurology objectively without accentuation of a mild motor deficit in L5 on the left, no urine disorder. Wound healed. Cenobamate treatment was not changed. *Causal Assessment: There is no support for causal relationship to cenobamate. This event was due to pre-existing condition.*
L1743: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT Arterial haemorrhage): 26-year-old Caucasian male. This patient has a past medical history of Tetralogy of Fallot, an MCA infarct, reconstructive cardiac surgery. Concomitant medications included: acetylsalicylic acid 100 mg oral once a day for prophylaxis of left middle cerebral artery infarction, ongoing since (b) (6). On study day 292 the subject experienced the severe SAE of left thoracic artery bleeding and was hospitalized. bronchoscopy revealed hemorrhage visible in the left S3 segment which lessened with local hemostasis. On study day 294, an acute thoracic CT confirmed an abscedating process in the S3 and the aberrant vessel structure also arose as the origin of the hemorrhage. C-reactive protein was elevated at 34.4 and the patient again became pyretic, and therapy was changed to Tienam and Avelox combination. Abscedating pneumonia and pulmonary hypertension were considered the underlying causes of the massive hemoptysis. The study drug was not changed due to the event. The patient continued treatment with cenobamate until study day 348. *There appears to be no causal relation to cenobamate treatment.*
L1744: YKP3089C021- (b) (6) (PT hyponatremia): 20-year-old Hispanic male developed hyponatremia on study day 379 and was hospitalized. The patient had dizziness and nausea since starting cenobamate and the patient was in the process of down titration. On the day of admission, the subject had abdominal pain with dizziness and one episode of vomiting. On study day 379 (day of admission), abnormal laboratory test results included glucose 121 mg/dL, sodium 127 mmol, chloride 94 mmol, creatinine 0.63 mg/dL, alkaline phosphatase 115 IU/L. ECG revealed normal sinus rhythm. NSS 0.9% was started intravenously. Study drug was
L1745: discontinued. The patient was discharged the following day. *Causal relationship to cenobamate is unlikely due to absence of temporal relationship and the possibility of a temporal relation to vomiting. An SIADH effect cannot be excluded.*
L1746: ## *Reports (of interest) not Captured by Variable "AEACN, "AEREL" and "AEOUT" Criteria.*
L1747: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT Fibrin D dimer increased): 22-year-old African American male had 8 seizures on study day 25 while on 25mg of cenobamate. Laboratory abnormalities obtained included increased creatinine, BUN, and decreased creatinine clearance with values of 3.3mg/dl, 35mg/dl and 35mg/dl respectively. Follow up measurement of these values on study day 29 show improvement. The subject was treated with intravenous fluids, acetaminophen, polyethylene glycol powder, pregabalin, lidocaine viscous mucous membrane liquid, and eslicarbazepine. This patient later had a left fibular fracture/trimalleolar fracture of ankle associated with alcohol intake. On study day 406 on a cenobamate dose of 200mg the patient had an event of carpopedal spasm with a laboratory abnormality of markedly elevated D-dimer (10,229-units and reference range not provided) identified. The subject presented (day 406) with 3 weeks of cramping and tingling and generalized weakness in the setting of stopping medications and alcohol abuse. He reported chronic symptoms of fatigue and pain related to his underlying health problems. The basis for the elevation of D-dimer was not identified. There was no evidence of venous thrombosis or pulmonary embolism. Examination of scheduled laboratory measurements on study days 357 and 532 reveal a low phosphate (1.8 meq/L, low reference range 2.5), mild reduction of potassium (3.4meq/L) and low absolute neutrophils (1.3 \times 10^9/L, low reference range 1.7) on study day 357. These abnormalities are not associated with carpopedal spasm. Serum creatinine, BUN and creatinine are examined at all 13 scheduled study visits to determine if the abnormality on study day 25 represents a chronic kidney injury. The only abnormality of these parameters is observed on study day 27 (visit 4) where creatinine was 3.3mg/dl (normal 0.7-1.4), blood urea nitrogen (BUN) was 35 mg/dl (normal 5-20) and creatinine clearance was 35mL/min (normal 85-125). Values for these parameters were normal in all preceding and the remaining 10 measurements over the subsequent 505 days the patient participated in study C021. *Causal assessment: The event of elevated creatinine appears to be a sporadic event, possibly related to a period of acute dehydration. The elevated D-dimer is without explanation but may have been an acute phase response related to the event of carpopedal spasm. Assessment of this patient's medical status is confounded by evidence of alcohol abuse. There is no support for causal relationship to cenobamate.*
L1748: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT Acute respiratory failure): 21-year-old Caucasian female with Angelman syndrome. This patient had severe medical compromise and is discussed in Section 8.4.1.
L1749: YKP3089C021-(b) (6) (PT Pyrexia): 21-year-old Caucasian male experienced the serious adverse event of fever of unknown origin on study day 449 while taking 250mg cenobamate
L1750: (dose escalated on study day 266). This finding occurred in the context of a seizure cluster. The patient body temperature in the emergency room was 102F. The event resolved and no cause for the fever or seizure cluster was identified. *Causality assessment: Cenobamate cannot be excluded but the episodic characteristic and absence of temporal relationship to initiation or a dose escalation makes a causal relationship very unlikely.*
L1751: YKP3089C021- [REDACTED] (b) (6) (PT visual acuity reduced): 43-year-old Caucasian female (country Poland) with diagnosis of epilepsy at age 12 and a history of left parietal lobe brain tumor at age 18. On study day 350 the patient had “weakness of visual acuity” and was hospitalized in the ophthalmology ward for observation. The patient also reported about one year of periodic headache and “weakness of visual acuity”. Technical details of the ophthalmology are provided but normal values are not provided. It can be inferred there is no glaucoma, visual scotoma. A gonioscopic exam was performed and apparently a fluorescein angiography study but results are not provided. Visual acuity is not reported. The patient was discharged to have follow up with treating physician, cenobamate was continued but the exposure dataset reveals the last day of treatment in study C021 was day 353. *Causal assessment: This cannot be determined at this time because the visual lesion is unclear, and a diagnosis is not provided. This may be due to background migraine headache or a remote effect of the parietal lobe tumor, but this is speculative.*
L1752: YKP3089C021- [REDACTED] (b) (6) (PT breast cancer): 60-year-old Caucasian female had a diagnosis of breast cancer on study day 85 while taking 200mg of cenobamate, identified during a routine screen. On study day 163 the patient had an additional SAE of balance disorder. This was found to be associated with concomitant carbamazepine dose. *Causality assessment: the short latency between initiation of cenobamate and diagnosis of breast cancer of less than 2 months does not plausibly support a causal relationship.*
L1753: YKP3089C021- [REDACTED] (b) (6) (PT Acute myocardial infarction): This 38-years-old Spanish, Caucasian female suffered an acute myocardial infarction study day 170 while taking 300mg of cenobamate. There was a positive troponin I at 1.19 ng/ml, reference range not provided. Further history reveals the patient had first cardiac symptomology at approximately study day 80. This pain is characterized as an episode of oppressive pain in the center of the chest, radiating to the arms, jaw and back. The pain was intense and associated with autonomic symptoms, occurring after moderate effort and lessening on its own with rest. This occurs on a background of light cigarette smoking, positive family history of ischemic cardiopathy (mother had AMI at age 50) and a medical history of dyslipidemia. Cenobamate treatment was continued in study C021 until study day 450. *Causal assessment: A role of cenobamate cannot be excluded however is rendered less likely by a strong family history of early coronary disease, smoking and medical history of dyslipidemia.*

L1755: ## Study C018
L1756: ## Patient 13 (1 of 3 Adjudicated DRESS Cases)
L1757: 50-year-old White male) in Cohort 2 began to experience warmth to the trunk and developed a diffuse erythematous rash to the trunk, arms, and legs (bilaterally) on Day 14 [REDACTED] (b)(6) approximately 5.4 hours after receiving the 4th dose of 400 mg YKP3089. He received therapy of oral diphenhydramine hydrochloride that day. The subject was examined by the PI the following morning and found to have a diffuse erythematous rash to trunk, arms and legs that was very red and pruritic. The subject denied any difficulty swallowing or breathing and dosing was continued. That evening he again received oral diphenhydramine for complaint of pruritus. Later that evening, he also complained of feeling weak. Over the next 6 days the rash showed little improvement and the subject complained of continued itching, for which he was treated with oral diphenhydramine 50 mg.
L1758: On Study Day 22 [REDACTED] (b)(6) the rash was noted to include the palms of the hands and soles of the feet. No stridor was noted, and there was no uvular or tongue swelling; the subject denied difficulty swallowing and was allowed to dose. Later that same day, the subject began to complain of tightness of facial skin and was noted to have soft facial tissue swelling as well as swelling of the digits bilaterally. No swelling of the lips or oral mucosa was noted. At this point the decision was made by the PI to discontinue dosing and keep the subject domiciled in the clinic for safety. The subject received the last dose of 500 mg YKP3089 on Day 22. The subject received a total of 5 doses each of 200, 300, and 400 mg YKP3089 and 7 doses of 500 mg YKP3089 before the study drug was discontinued.
L1759: On Day 23 [REDACTED] (b)(6) in the morning, the subject had increased weakness; stating that it was difficult to get out of bed. The rash remained present and the subject continued to deny any difficulty breathing or swallowing. That afternoon, he began to have swelling of the lips and bilateral eyelids, body aches, and a burning sensation in the areas covered by the rash. He received oral diphenhydramine, oral paracetamol, and topical hydrocortisone. Vital signs were normal with the exception of a pulse rate of 99 bpm at a single time point. That evening, his oral temperature increased to 103.8°F, accompanied by a pulse rate of 106 bpm. He was given oral paracetamol.
L1760: On Day 24 [REDACTED] (b)(6) the subject again had an increased temperature, with a maximum of 103.2°F, and increased heart rate of 106 bpm. He received oral diphenhydramine and paracetamol. The pruritic rash was still present. He had continued weakness with observed difficulty changing positions in bed. He reported feeling achy all over, with moderate pain to entire body. He also was noted to have generalized pustules over his face and increased swelling to the facial soft tissues. The subject was given intramuscular methylprednisolone sodium succinate.
L1761: Laboratory work was performed and abnormal laboratory results of note at this time were as follows: sodium 122 mEq/L (reference range 133 - 145 mEq/L), chloride 88 mEq/L (reference range: 97 - 105 mEq/L), blood urea nitrogen (BUN) 28 mg/dL (reference range: 6 - 20 mg/dL), creatinine 1.70 mg/dL (reference range: 0.8 - 1.3 mg/dL), bicarbonate 21 mmol/L (reference range 24.2 - 33.6 mmol/L), ALT 517 U/L (reference range 11 - 65 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (AST) 173 U/L (reference range: 15 - 46 U/L), GGT 255 U/L (reference range 7 - 62 U/L), white blood cells (WBCs) 13.8 Thou/\muL (reference range: 3.4 - 10.5 Thou/\muL), lymphocytes 8.6% (reference range: 16.8 - 51.7%), neutrophils 84.0% (reference range: 40.2 - 75.0%), and absolute neutrophil count (ANC) 11.6 Thou/\muL
L1762: (reference range: 1.5 - 7.0 Thou/L). Glomerular filtration rate (GFR), while still within normal limits at 66 mL/min (reference range: &gt; 60 mL/min), was significantly lower than at baseline (125 mL/min). The decision was made to transfer the subject via ambulance to the local emergency room.
L1763: At the hospital, initial blood work was consistent with the findings at the site. Additional findings included albumin 2.5 g/dL, calcium, 7.8 mg/dL, and INR 1.41. In addition, 2 blood cultures revealed *Staphylococcus Epidermidis*. Treatment with Vancomycin 1 g QD was initiated along with normal saline, heparin, and Solu-Medrol® 120 mg /day.
L1764: The subject was seen by an infectious disease physician. Vancomycin was discontinued by the infectious disease physician who believed, based on the lack of corroborating symptoms, that the blood cultures were contaminated. The infectious disease physician believed the diffuse maculopapular rash was likely a drug eruption secondary to an antiepileptic drug. Within 24 hours, the patient showed signs of clinical improvement with less fever, feeling stronger and some resolution of the rash. Renal function improved, WBC counts reduced with normalization of granulocytes and calcium increased. Hepatitis panel for A, B and C was negative. However, on 30 January 2013, AST increased to 568 U/L, ALT increased to 809 U/L and alkaline phosphatase increased to 133 U/L.
L1765: On [REDACTED] (b) (6) a dermatologist reported a diffuse dusky discoloration of skin involving hands and feet plus areas of fine scaling and palpable purpura extensive on legs. His first impression was drug hypersensitivity syndrome including systemic symptoms and leukocytoclastic vasculitis. A CBC with differential ordered by the dermatologist revealed eosinophils of 11% with a total WBC of 7.1 Thou/\muL. Based on the elevated eosinophil count, the dermatologist believes the condition of the subject can best be characterized as a DRESS syndrome. By [REDACTED] (b) (6) the rash was resolving, and the liver and kidney functions tests were returning towards normal.
L1766: By discharge from the hospital, the sodium, chloride, BUN, creatinine, bicarbonate, and WBC count were within normal limits. The ALT and AST were still elevated but trending downwards. Hemoglobin level was still low at 11.5 g/dL (reference range 12.6 - 16.8 g/dL) as was albumin at 2.7 g/dL (reference range 4 - 5.2 g/dL). The subject was alert and oriented, in stable condition, and his symptoms had significantly improved. The subject was discharged from the hospital on [REDACTED] (b) (6) and returned to the clinical site. The subject continued to follow up with the clinic until resolution of his symptoms. Safety labs drawn on [REDACTED] (b) (6) showed that the international normalized ratio (INR), which was elevated during hospitalization to 1.41 (reference range: 0.9 - 1.1) and AST were now within normal limits and the GFR had improved back to baseline at 113 mL/min. Safety labs drawn on 21 February 2013 showed that hemoglobin and albumin were within normal limits and that GGT and ALT were only slightly elevated. The symptoms of itching and rash continued to improve per follow-up phone calls by the subject. The SAE was considered resolved on [REDACTED] (b) (6)

L1779: ## CLINICAL REVIEW
L1780: NDA number	212839
Application holder	SK Life Science, Inc.
Drug	Cenobamate
Topic	DRESS
Medical reviewer and Office/Division	Gerard Boehm, MD, MPH
ODE I, DNP
L1781: ## DRESS Background
L1782: Drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS), also referred to as Drug induced hypersensitivity syndrome (DIHS), is a hypersensitivity reaction involving multiple organ systems and characterized by rash, fever, lymphadenopathy, leukocytosis with eosinophilia and atypical lymphocytes, and liver dysfunction. I include several diagnostic criteria proposed for DRESS as appendix A to this review. Onset of DRESS symptoms generally occur 2-8 weeks after initiating a drug. DRESS has been reported with drugs such as carbamazepine, phenytoin, phenobarbital, lamotrigine, dapsone, mexilitene, salazosulpyridine, allopurinol, and minocycline. DRESS is also associated with reactivation of herpes viruses (ex. HHV-6 and CMV). Estimates of DRESS risk with first or second prescription of an aromatic antiepileptic range from 2.3-4.5 in 10,000. Mortality from DRESS has been estimated to be 2-14%.1
L1783: Several antiepileptic drugs cause DRESS and their package inserts describe these events in Warnings or Warnings and Precautions statements. Even those antiepileptic drugs with boxed warnings for other adverse events (Tegretol, Depakote, and Lamictal) do not include DRESS within the box. I include as appendix B to this review examples of the labeling language for antiepileptic drugs that cause DRESS.
L1784: ## Cenobamate DRESS Background
L1785: SK Life Science, Inc. (SKLSI) developed Cenobamate for the treatment of partial onset seizures. During early clinical trials, 3 cenobamate exposed participants developed DRESS and one of the cases was fatal. At the time of the identification of these cases, 833 patients took at least 1 dose of cenobamate, yielding a DRESS risk of 0.36% (3/833).
L1786: 2 of the 3 cenobamate DRESS cases occurred in healthy volunteers participating in clinical pharmacology studies and the third case involved an epilepsy patient enrolled in a phase 2 trial (017). SKLSI provided narratives for the 3 DRESS cases and the clinical details and test results supporting the DRESS diagnoses. I provide brief summaries of the cases below, with detailed narratives included as appendix C to this review.
L1787: 1 Watanabe, H. Recent Advances in Drug-Induced Hypersensitivity Syndrome/Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms. J Immunol Res 2018 Mar 18;2018:5163129.
L1788: In the first case, a healthy 38-year-old Hispanic female who enrolled in a QT study developed a rash on day 32 and study drug was stopped the next day. Cenobamate dosing for this individual in this study was Days 1 to 7: 50 mg QD; Days 8 to 14: 100 mg QD; Days 15 to 21: 150 mg QD; Days 22 to 35: 200 mg QD. During follow up she developed pruritis, diffuse adenopathy, depressed mood, facial swelling, blurred vision, ecchymoses, chest pain, dyspnea, and palpitations. She experienced lab abnormalities including decreased hemoglobin, and elevated transaminases, bilirubin, eosinophils, and lymphocytes. She was evaluated by a dermatologist, hepatologist, internist, and study clinic staff. She had a skin punch biopsy that was consistent with DRESS. She was treated with topical, oral, and intravenous steroids and antihistamines. She was hospitalized twice. On study day 87, 6 days after developing chest pain, palpitations, and shortness of breath, she was found unresponsive by her husband and pronounced dead in an emergency department. A post-mortem examination concluded that she died from a cardiac dysrhythmia due to eosinophilic myocarditis due to DRESS, with findings of pleural effusions, pericardial effusion, pulmonary edema, cardiomyopathy, and necrotizing myocarditis.
L1789: In the second case, a healthy 50-year-old white male who enrolled in a clinical pharmacology study developed an erythematous rash with warmth on day 14. In this study, participants were started on cenobamate 200mg/day and titrated up by 100mg weekly. He was treated with antihistamines and continued in the study until day 22 when he developed facial swelling. He subsequently developed fever (highest reported temperature 103.8°F), swelling of the lips and bilateral eyelids, body aches, and a burning sensation in the areas covered by the rash. He was hospitalized. Lab abnormalities included hyponatremia, elevated creatinine, elevated transaminases and elevated eosinophils. An infectious disease specialist suspected a drug eruption secondary to an antiepileptic drug and a dermatologist felt this was DRESS syndrome. The subject was discharged from the hospital on [REDACTED] (b) (6) and labs and symptoms improved. The investigator reported that SAE was resolved on [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L1790: In the third case, a 36-year-old white female, with complex partial seizure and partial onset seizure with secondary generalization entered a study for cenobamate. Concomitant AED medications included divalproex sodium, levetiracetam, and zonisamide. In this study, subjects began with a cenobamate daily dose of 100 mg, followed by weekly increments of 100 mg in the daily dose, to the target dose (100mg, 200mg, or 400mg). Due to tolerability issues, the dosing was changed in a protocol amendment to initial doses of 50 mg/day and the titration rate was slowed from 100 mg/day/week to 50 mg/day/week until 200 mg/day was reached and then the dose was increased by 100 mg /week in subjects randomized to 400 mg/day. The narrative for this adverse event described confusion about dosing with some sources suggesting missed doses. On study Day 24, she presented to an emergency room with several days of fever and chills; a 2-day history of diffuse macular erythematous and pruritic rash covering the face, trunk, back, arms, and legs; headache; arthralgia; myalgia; and memory problems. Upon admission, she had a temperature of 101.3°F and a morbilliform rash on the abdomen and legs. Laboratory results included elevated transaminases and eosinophils. A dermatology consultant diagnosed DRESS. A chest CT showed mediastinal lymphadenopathy and she was also noted to have bilateral axillary and inguinal lymphadenopathy. Treatment included paracetamol, diphenhydramine hydrochloride, and methylprednisolone which was subsequently switched to prednisone. She was discharged from the hospital and subsequently lost to follow up.
L1791: SKLSI and DNP agreed to a study to evaluate whether a lower starting dose and slower cenobamate titration would reduce the risk for DRESS. DNP consulted the Division of Biometrics regarding the planned sample size for this study. SKLSI proposed enrolling 1,000 patients. SKLSI selected this sample size by assuming 0 cases of DRESS would occur with slower titration, allowing SKLSI to cap the DRESS risk at 0.3% (upper bound of 95% CI, given no cases with 1000 exposures). DNP accepted this 0.3% point risk estimate "based on similarity to SJS risk in lamotrigine."2 The Division of Biometrics memo included tables of sample size and power calculations for a one arm study but provided the following advice:
L1792: The optimal, statistically valid way to compare the two titration schedules would involve a randomized clinical trial in which subjects are randomized to either titration schedule. The comparison of data from a new single arm study to a historic single arm study is limited by the fact that subjects in the new and the historic studies might differ in terms of measured and unmeasured covariates that may be associated with DRESS. Furthermore, even if subjects were comparable at baseline in both studies, the conduct of the two studies and the procedures to collect and record adverse events may differ and may affect the observed rate of DRESS in both studies regardless of treatment effect.
L1793: In the conclusion of their memo, the Division of Biometrics wrote the following:
L1794: If the review team decides that the main scientific and regulatory comparison of interest is between the new modified titration schedule and the historic study group, and that a single arm study with the new schedule is the only feasible or ethical alternative to compare both treatment arms; then we recommend that the sponsor submit a prospective statistical analysis plan (SAP) to compare the two treatments based on the two studies (new and historic) and that the SAP justify the comparability of the two studies based on the study populations and study procedures.
L1795: It does not appear that the Division of Biometrics reviewed a prospective statistical analysis plan for study 021.
L1796: ## 021 Trial design
L1797: Study YKP3089C021 was an open-label, multicenter (37), multi-country (17) safety study in patients with poorly controlled partial seizures that enrolled 1,339 patients. Enrolled patients received cenobamate 12.5mg/day for 2 weeks, followed by 25mg/day for 2 weeks, followed by upward titration every other week at a rate of 50mg/day to a target dose of 200mg/day. The maximum allowed dose in this study was 400mg/day, and this dose was reached by upward titration every other week at a rate of 50mg/day. After 12 months of participation in the open-label treatment period, subjects were re-evaluated. Those who benefited from treatment with cenobamate were given the option to continue use of the drug at the discretion of the investigator.
L1798: Study 021 included elements to protect patients in the event of DRESS and to ensure identification of cases. Below I identify several of these elements. Many of these elements were not present in the
L1799: 2 Memorandum Division of Biometrics 7
L1800: earlier studies and as noted above by the Division of Biometrics should be considered when evaluating the lower DRESS risk observed in 021.
L1801: 021 Study subjects received the following hypersensitivity information on a laminated card to carry with them:
L1802: - Rash may occur and may progress to DRESS.
- Rash or other signs or symptoms of hypersensitivity (e.g., fever, lymphadenopathy, and swelling) may herald a serious medical event and that the subject should report any such occurrence to the investigative site immediately.
- Instructions on whom and where to call in the event a symptom arises and information that can be provided to an emergency room in the event the subject cannot reach the investigator site.
- Reminder to check skin daily for evidence of rash for the first 4 months of therapy.
L1803: If a subject reported a rash by telephone, an unscheduled visit was performed promptly. Any subject with a rash or manifestation of hypersensitivity reaction such as lymphadenopathy, fever, or facial swelling was seen immediately at the study site. If it could definitely be determined that the rash or other symptoms were unrelated to study drug, then the subject could be maintained on YKP3809. If the cause of the rash or other symptoms could not be definitely determined immediately, then YKP3089 would have to be discontinued immediately.
L1804: The initial physical/rash assessment for hypersensitivity reaction at the investigative site included the following:
L1805: - Measurement of body temperature
- Characterization of lymphadenopathy, if present
- Performance of hematology with manual differential and chemistry tests including amylase, lipase, and creatine phosphokinase
- Measurement and description of extent and area of rash
- Evaluation for other causes of multi-organ dysfunction, including antinuclear antibody, blood culture, serology for HIV, hepatitis virus, chlamydia and mycoplasma, Epstein-Barr virus, human herpesvirus 6, and cytomegalovirus, if indicated
L1806: If the skin and physical/rash assessment results indicated a rash with the involvement of 1 other organ system or a suspected case of DRESS, the subject was immediately referred to pre-identified local experts and the sponsor was notified immediately.
L1807: Investigators were instructed to identify local medical specialists who were able to diagnose and treat suspected DRESS cases. The locally identified specialists were informed by the investigator that their services may be needed during the course of the study. These specialists were identified by the PI prior to any new subjects being initiated at an investigative site. The specialists could have included, but were not limited to, a dermatologist, hepatologist, and/or cardiologist.
L1808: During the conduct of the study, a report of subjects with any new hypersensitivity reactions was generated monthly. Hypersensitivity reactions were identified by filtering the existing clinical database for any terms in the standardized MedDRA queries (SMQs) for hypersensitivity and DRESS. All SAEs with similar terms were also identified.
L1809: At Visits 1, 14, and yearly thereafter, a full physical exam was performed. At Visits 3 to 10, a limited physical exam to identify evidence of hypersensitivity signs, such as rash, swelling, lymphadenopathy, and fever, was performed.
L1810: Site calls to subjects at least once in between the clinic visits to remind them to be vigilant regarding signs and symptoms of DRESS.
L1811: In addition to the procedures noted above, compared to trials 013 and 017, study 021 had additional exclusion criteria regarding history of hypersensitivity,
L1812: ## Exclusion criteria related to hypersensitivity 021
L1813: - History of any serious drug-induced hypersensitivity reaction (including but not limited to Stevens Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis, drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms) or any drug-related rash requiring hospitalization.
- History of any drug-induced rash or hypersensitivity reaction with documented nature of the rash or hypersensitivity reaction.
- History of a first-degree relative with a serious cutaneous, drug-induced adverse reaction.
L1814: ## Exclusion criteria related to hypersensitivity 017
L1815: - History of 1 serious drug-induced hypersensitivity reaction (including but not limited to Stevens Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis, DRESS) or any drug-related rash requiring hospitalization.
- History of AED-associated rash that involved conjunctiva or mucosae or more than 1 maculopapular rash that required discontinuation.
L1816: ## Exclusion criteria related to hypersensitivity 013
L1817: - Subject has had greater than 2 allergic reactions to an AED or 1 serious hypersensitivity reaction to an AED.
L1818: ## Study 021 Results
L1819: SKSLI reported that no cases of DRESS were identified from trial 021.
L1820: ## DNP Reviewer search for cases of DRESS in trial 021
L1821: Using the submitted AE dataset for trial 021, I did not identify any cases that were suggestive of DRESS.
L1822: For my first analysis, I reviewed the verbatim AE terms as listed in the dataset and selected any terms suggestive of signs and or symptoms of DRESS, including rashes, allergic reactions, pruritis, fever, lymphadenopathy, hematological abnormalities, liver injury, or kidney injury. I then identified the subjects with at least 2 AE terms from the search (n=35; 27 subjects had at 2 terms, 4 subjects had 3 terms, 3 subjects had 4 terms and 1 subject had 5 terms). From this group, I identified the subjects who had terms suggestive of involvement of more than 1 organ system. For example, one patient had 3 AE
L1823: terms identified from the search, but all 3 AE terms were related to liver laboratory results (elevated ALP, elevated ALT, elevated AST) so this patient was dropped from further consideration. This left 12 subjects with AE terms involving one or more organ system. From this group, I excluded patients where the AE terms occurred more than 30 days apart. For example, a patient had an AE of acute kidney injury and another of allergic reaction, but these events occurred more than 1 year apart from each other. After excluding temporally unrelated events, there were 8 patients. None of these 8 patients appeared to have DRESS after further consideration. None of these events were SAEs, none were deemed severe by investigators, and none described involvement of more than 2 organ systems. 6 of the 8 identified patients continued study drug and 2 patients were withdrawn (one for fever and erythematous rash, and one for eosinophils increased and prurigo). 5 of the 8 patients had all AEs classified as resolved or resolving. 3 patients had AEs that were classified as not recovered/not resolved (leukopenia, elevated GGTP, and increased monocytes).
L1824: For my second analysis, I used the sponsor created potential DRESS symptom flags in their analysis dataset for 021. In the Phase 2/3 AE dataset (ADAE), SKSLI included the following derived variables: DRESS1 (acute skin rash), DRESS2 (internal organ involvement)3, DRESS3 (enlarged lymph nodes), and DRESS5 (fever above 38 degrees C). I identified patients with at least one of these events. The most commonly reported potential DRESS symptom was internal organ involvement (N=135) followed by acute rash (n=96), fever (n=13), and lymphadenopathy (n=8). 8 patients had 2 potential DRESS symptoms, and none had more than 2 potential DRESS symptoms.
L1825: In addition to analyzing the AE data sets, I reviewed the deaths, SAEs and Discontinuations for AEs narratives from study 021 to look for cases of DRESS. None of the 4 deaths appeared to be DRESS cases. I identified 8 rashes that were classified as SAEs and none were suggestive for DRESS cases. Lastly, I reviewed the narratives for potential DRESS symptoms/signs that led to discontinuation and none were suggestive for DRESS.
L1826: ## Comparison of AEs and Discontinuations for AEs
L1827: As noted above, study 021 included instructions to investigators and participants about monitoring for DRESS signs and symptoms along with procedures for evaluation and discontinuation that were not part of other development program trials. Given the possibility that these differences could have contributed to the observed reduced risk of DRESS in study 021 (ex. by preventing cases through earlier identification and intervention), I examined the risk for skin and hypersensitivity related AEs and discontinuations in trial 021 to look for differences compared to trials 017 and 013. One might expect that the differences in study procedures would have led to increases in identification of AEs such as rashes and to increased discontinuation for these events in study 021. In the following table I provide the percentages of subjects with of Skin/Immune system SOC AEs and discontinuation for such events in trial 021 and trials

L1829: 013 and 017. As displayed below, the percentage of patients with Skin and subcutaneous SOC AEs and discontinuation for such events was higher in trial 021 compared to trials 013 and 017.

L1832: SOC AE data from ISS Tables 4.1.2; 4.1.4; D/C for SOC AE data from ISS Tables 4.9.2; 4.9.4
L1833: After considering the differences in duration of exposure when comparing the double-blind trials 013 and 017 to the open label trial 021, there did not appear to be meaningful differences in risk for Skin and subcutaneous SOC AEs or discontinuation for such AEs. Given the differences in duration for study 021 (1110 subjects exposed for 6 months, study ongoing) compared to trials 013 and 017 (8 weeks, each), any observed differences in risk resulting from the above comparisons could be merely due to longer observation time and greater opportunity to observe an event in study 021. To address this issue, I looked at Skin and subcutaneous SOC AEs and discontinuations for Skin and subcutaneous SOC AEs during the first 8 weeks of study 021 compared to trials 017 and 013. For all Skin and subcutaneous SOC AEs, during the first 8 weeks of trial 021, the risk was 8.8% (118/1339) compared to 7% (31/442) for trials 017 and 013. The rate of discontinuation for these events during the first 8 weeks of study 021 (2.2%, 30/1339) was comparable to the rate in trials 017 and 013 (1.6%, 7/442).
L1834: For the 44 subjects who discontinued from 021 for Skin and Subcutaneous tissue AEs, there did not appear to be strong evidence suggesting that these subjects received treatment leading to reduction in DRESS risk (Summaries included as an appendix to this review). Thirty-one individuals had cenobamate discontinued with no other intervention noted and 13 received treatment for their skin AEs (mostly topical corticosteroids and antihistamines). Seven of these treated individuals had diagnoses such as urticaria, angioedema, psoriasis, and eczema and the treatment appeared appropriate. The remaining 6 individuals who received treatment had AEs of rash or pruritis.
L1835: Although it is difficult to determine whether monitoring, early discontinuation and treatment prevented any potential cases of DRESS, I identified 2 patients of interest. I provide summaries for those cases below.
L1836: YKP3089C021-(b)(6) Approximately 6 weeks after receiving the first dose of cenobamate, the subject experienced mild skin erythema which was 4 cm in diameter on the left neck nape area (visit 6). The study drug was withdrawn. The subject did not experience any lymphadenopathy, temperature
L1837: elevation, or facial swelling. The subject's general practitioner made the diagnosis of contact eczema and prescribed a local corticoid and after this the lesions disappeared. The patient had an elevated eosinophil count as noted below.
