L1839: YKP3089C021-(b)(6) On study day 4, the subject experienced an SAE of pneumonia and was hospitalized. Treatment while hospitalized included amoxicillin/clavulanic acid. The study drug was not changed due to the event. The subject recovered from the event of pneumonia. Approximately 3 months after starting cenobamate, the subject experienced mild "prurito with papulas" (papules and pruritus on arms and legs) and abnormal eosinophils lab results. A specialist (not described) diagnosed the event as "unspecified prurigo". Study drug was withdrawn due to the event. Prior to the event, the subject had eosinophil counts ranging from 0.1-0.6 \times 10^9/L (normal range 0.0-0.8 \times 10^9/L). One week before the event, the eosinophils were 1.1 and were 1.2 \times 10^9/L the day after stopping cenobamate. Two weeks after stopping cenobamate, eosinophils were 0.3 \times 10^9/L. ALT, which was normal at baseline increased to 116 U/L the week before the event and was 35 U/L the day after stopping cenobamate. The subject was treated with Fucicort and Clobetasol.
L1840: In addition to the discontinuations for Skin and Subcutaneous AEs, I reviewed the hypersensitivity reports that were identified by MedDRA queries during study 021 and submitted to the IND during the study. The sponsor provided 62 reports, 44 of which were the same subjects who discontinued for Skin and Subcutaneous SOC AEs as noted above. The remaining 18 reports included AEs for rash (n=11), itching (n=3), increased eosinophils (n=2), candidiasis, and erythema (n=1, each). None of these 17 reports appeared to describe severe events and many of the narratives documented resolution.
L1841: ## Baseline Demographics
L1842: When comparing the populations in the double-blind trials (013, 017), and the Open label study 021, the baseline measured demographics were similar, but there were some differences compared to the Phase I pool. The phase I pool included a higher proportion of males (68%) compared to the double-blind trials (49%) or study 021 (50%). In the Phase I pool 51% of patients were classified as white and 39% black compared to 76% white and 3% black in the double-blind trials and 79% white and 3% black in study 021. Mean and median age and BMI were similar for all three populations (based on data included in ISS tables 21, 22, and 23).
L1843: ## Discussion
L1844: Early development program trials demonstrated that cenobamate can cause DRESS at a relatively high rate. A subsequent open label trial that used a lower starting dose and slower titration did not result in any DRESS cases in over 1300 patients. Since the evaluation of DRESS risk with low starting dose/slower
L1845: titration was not tested in a randomized controlled trial, the data do not allow for definitive conclusions about whether the revised dosing regimen is solely responsible for the observed reduction in DRESS risk. Other possibilities include differences in underlying studied populations and differences in study conduct (ex. heightened surveillance and early discontinuation). As noted above, there were differences in exclusion criteria in these trials and the Phase 1 pool, from which 2 of the 3 DRESS cases arose, had notable baseline differences in participant race classification. In addition, study 021 included several requirements intended to identify early and protect patients from DRESS. Although these differences did not appear to result in increased reporting or discontinuation Skin or Immune system AEs, the impact of these design differences on observed DRESS risk may not be completely understood.
L1846: If cenobamate is approved, the sponsor should accurately describe the risk, monitor for DRESS cases in the post marketing period and search for predictive factors for increased DRESS risk. The sponsor should include standard language about DRESS as a Warning statement in labeling with care to avoid falsely implying that DRESS risk has been eliminated with the use of the lower starting dose/slower titration. The Division should consider requiring a Medication Guide that includes information like that provided to patients in study 021 about signs and symptoms of DRESS and instructions to contact health care providers immediately if these occur. The sponsor should closely monitor for and include as 15-day reports to the Agency any spontaneous reports suggestive of DRESS with the intention of assessing real world use risk and identification of predictive factors. In addition, the sponsor should perform genomic studies to look for predictive factors for DRESS. In previous discussions with the agency, the sponsor admitted that they did not preserve the necessary samples from the development program that would allow such studies, so this should be done in the post marketing period. I do not believe a REMS is required.
L1847: ## Appendix A
L1848: ## Proposed DRESS Diagnostic Criteria
L1849: Diagnosis of DRESS is confirmed by the presence of all of the following criteria:
L1850: - Cutaneous drug eruption
- Adenopathies \ge 2 cm in diameter or hepatitis (liver transaminases \ge 2 times upper limit of normal) or interstitial nephritis or interstitial pneumonitis or carditis
- Hematologic abnormalities: eosinophilia \ge 1.5 \times 10^9 L^{-1} or atypical lymphocytes
L1851: Criteria for potential cases of drug reaction with DRESS by RegiSCAR.
L1852: - Hospitalization
- Reaction suspected to be drug-related
- Acute skin rash*
- Fever above 38^{\circ}C*
- Enlarged lymph nodes in at least two sites*
- Involvement of at least one internal organ*
- Blood count abnormalitiesLymphocytes above or below the laboratory limits*
- Eosinophils above the laboratory limits*
- Platelets below the laboratory limits*
L1853: *Three or more criteria required. RegiSCAR: research group investigating severe cutaneous adverse reactions (SCAR).
L1854: Diagnostic criteria for drug-induced hypersensitivity syndrome (DIHS) established by a Japanese consensus group.
L1855: - Maculopapular rash developing 3 weeks after starting with a limited number of drugs
- Prolonged clinical symptoms 2 weeks after discontinuation of the causative drug
- Fever (\ge 38^\circ\text{C})
- Liver abnormalities (alanine aminotransferase \ge 100\text{U}\cdot\text{L}^{-1})a
- Leukocyte abnormalities (at least one present)Leukocytosis (\ge 11 \times 10^9 \text{L}^{-1})
- Atypical lymphocytosis (\ge 5\%)
- Eosinophilia (\ge 1.5 \times 10^9 \text{L}^{-1})
- Lymphadenopathy
- Human herpesvirus 6 reactivation
L1856: The diagnosis is confirmed by the presence of the seven criteria above (typical DIHS) or of the first five (1–5) criteria (atypical DIHS).
L1857: aThis can be replaced by other organ involvement, such as renal involvement.
L1858: ## Appendix B
L1859: ## Examples of DRESS labeling from approved antiepileptic drugs
L1860: ## Tegretol
L1861: Has a boxed warning (TEN/SJS, AA) but doesn't include DRESS
L1862: ## Warning Statement
L1863: ## Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS)/Multiorgan Hypersensitivity
L1864: Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), also known as multiorgan hypersensitivity, has occurred with Tegretol. Some of these events have been fatal or life-threatening. DRESS typically, although not exclusively, presents with fever, rash, lymphadenopathy, and/or facial swelling, in association with other organ system involvement, such as hepatitis, nephritis, hematologic abnormalities, myocarditis, or myositis sometimes resembling an acute viral infection. Eosinophilia is often present. This disorder is variable in its expression, and other organ systems not noted here may be involved. It is important to note that early manifestations of hypersensitivity (e.g., fever, lymphadenopathy) may be present even though rash is not evident. If such signs or symptoms are present, the patient should be evaluated immediately. Tegretol should be discontinued if an alternative etiology for the signs or symptoms cannot be established.
L1865: ## Depakote
L1866: Has a boxed warning (Hepatotoxicity, fetal risk, pancreatitis) but doesn't include DRESS
L1867: ## Warnings and Precautions
L1868: ## 5.12 Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS)/Multiorgan Hypersensitivity Reactions
L1869: Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), also known as Multiorgan Hypersensitivity, has been reported in patients taking valproate. DRESS may be fatal or life-threatening. DRESS typically, although not exclusively, presents with fever, rash, and/or lymphadenopathy, in association with other organ system involvement, such as hepatitis, nephritis, hematological abnormalities, myocarditis, or myositis sometimes resembling an acute viral infection. Eosinophilia is often present. Because this disorder is variable in its expression, other organ systems not noted here may be involved. It is important to note that early manifestations of hypersensitivity, such as fever or lymphadenopathy, may be present even though rash is not evident. If such signs or symptoms are present, the patient should be evaluated immediately. Valproate should be discontinued and not be resumed if an alternative etiology for the signs or symptoms cannot be established.
L1870: ## Lamictal
L1871: Has a boxed warning for serious rashes but doesn't mention DRESS
L1872: ## Warnings and Precautions
L1873: ## 5.3 Multiorgan Hypersensitivity Reactions and Organ Failure
L1874: Multiorgan hypersensitivity reactions, also known as drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS), have occurred with LAMICTAL. Some have been fatal or life threatening. DRESS typically, although not exclusively, presents with fever, rash, and/or lymphadenopathy in association with other organ system involvement, such as hepatitis, nephritis, hematologic abnormalities, myocarditis, or myositis, sometimes resembling an acute viral infection. Eosinophilia is often present. This disorder is variable in its expression, and other organ systems not noted here may be involved.
L1875: Fatalities associated with acute multiorgan failure and various degrees of hepatic failure have been reported in 2 of 3,796 adult patients and 4 of 2,435 pediatric patients who received LAMICTAL in epilepsy clinical trials. Rare fatalities from multiorgan failure have also been reported in postmarketing use.
L1876: Isolated liver failure without rash or involvement of other organs has also been reported with LAMICTAL.
L1877: It is important to note that early manifestations of hypersensitivity (e.g., fever, lymphadenopathy) may be present even though a rash is not evident. If such signs or symptoms are present, the patient should be evaluated immediately. LAMICTAL should be discontinued if an alternative etiology for the signs or symptoms cannot be established.
L1878: Prior to initiation of treatment with LAMICTAL, the patient should be instructed that a rash or other signs or symptoms of hypersensitivity (e.g., fever, lymphadenopathy) may herald a serious medical event and that the patient should report any such occurrence to a healthcare provider immediately.
L1879: ## Phenytoin
L1880: No boxed warning
L1881: ## Warnings and Precautions
L1882: ## 5.4 Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS)/Multiorgan Hypersensitivity
L1883: Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), also known as Multiorgan hypersensitivity, has been reported in patients taking antiepileptic drugs, including DILANTIN. Some of these events have been fatal or life-threatening. DRESS typically, although not exclusively, presents with fever, rash, lymphadenopathy, and/or facial swelling, in association with other organ system involvement, such as hepatitis, nephritis, hematological abnormalities, myocarditis, or myositis
L1884: sometimes resembling an acute viral infection. Eosinophilia is often present. Because this disorder is variable in its expression, other organ systems not noted here may be involved. It is important to note that early manifestations of hypersensitivity, such as fever or lymphadenopathy, may be present even though rash is not evident. If such signs or symptoms are present, the patient should be evaluated immediately. DILANTIN should be discontinued if an alternative etiology for the signs or symptoms cannot be established.

L1889: Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), also known as multiorgan hypersensitivity, has occurred with NEURONTIN. Some of these reactions have been fatal or life-threatening. Eosinophilia is often present. This disorder is variable in its expression, and other organ systems not noted here may be involved.
L1890: It is important to note that early manifestations of hypersensitivity, such as fever or lymphadenopathy, may be present even though rash is not evident. If such signs or symptoms are present, the patient should be evaluated immediately. NEURONTIN should be discontinued if an alternative etiology for the signs or symptoms cannot be established.

L1893: Study Number: YKP3089C017 Subject ID: [REDACTED](b)(6) MedDRA Preferred Term Drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms Relationship to Treatment Probably

L1895: Subject [REDACTED](b)(6), a 36-year-old white female, with complex partial seizure and partial onset seizure with secondary generalization entered study YKP3089 on [REDACTED](b)(6). She was randomly assigned to YKP3089 200 mg on [REDACTED](b)(6). Concomitant AED medications included divalproex sodium, levetiracetam, and zonisamide. Concomitant non-AED medications included docusate, enoxaparin, gentamycin sulfate, omeprazole, ondansetron, and acetaminophen/chlorpheniramine/phenylephrine. The narrative suggested confusion about dosing. The last dose of the study drug noted in the electronic data capture system was 100 mg on [REDACTED](b)(6). According to the drug accountability record, no study drug was taken from [REDACTED](b)(6) to [REDACTED](b)(6). According to the site, the subject should have been taking 200 mg QD from [REDACTED](b)(6) onward. According to the investigator, the last dose of the study drug, which was 200 mg QD, was taken on [REDACTED](b)(6). During a protocol-specified phone call on [REDACTED](b)(6) the subject complained of tiredness. On [REDACTED](b)(6) (Study Day 24), she presented to an emergency room with several days of fever and chills; a 2-day history of diffuse macular erythematous and pruritic rash covering the majority of body including the face, trunk, back, arms, and legs; headache; arthralgia; myalgia; and memory problems. Upon admission, the subject had a temperature of 101.3°F and a morbilliform rash on the abdomen and legs. Laboratory results are provided in the table below. Chest x-ray and urinalysis were reported as negative (specific results not reported). A lumbar puncture was performed and was reported to be negative, including cultures. A dermatology consultant made the diagnosis of drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS) syndrome. A CT scan of the chest and pelvis was performed on [REDACTED](b)(6) to evaluate for lymphoma. Findings included bilateral pleural effusion, right side was greater than the left side, with right lower lobe consolidation. A moderate amount of free fluid in the abdomen and pelvis tracking along the right pericolic gutter was observed, with either a thickened gallbladder wall versus fluid in the gallbladder fossa. Mediastinal lymph nodes were enlarged bilaterally but not consistent with lymphoma along with bilateral axillary and inguinal lymph nodes. Treatment for the event included paracetamol, diphenhydramine hydrochloride, and methylprednisolone which was subsequently switched to prednisone. She also received vancomycin and ceftriaxone; however, antibiotics were stopped as she did not have an acute infectious process. On [REDACTED](b)(6), the subject was discharged from the hospital. According to the investigator, the subject went to an emergency room on [REDACTED](b)(6) and [REDACTED](b)(6) because of rash. Following this, the subject was lost to follow up despite multiple attempts to have her return. The outcome of the event of DRESS syndrome was unknown. The subject did not experience any other AEs at the time of the event of DRESS syndrome. The investigator considered the severe SAE of DRESS syndrome probably related to the study drug. The subject discontinued from the study on [REDACTED](b)(6).

L1915: The next morning (b) (6) [Day 34] she reported by telephone that the rash on her chest was improving, as was the pruritus of her neck. On (b) (6) (Day 39), the subject returned to the clinic and the rash was noted to be worse; a generalized maculopapular rash was now covering her face, neck, and trunk, front and back down to her buttocks. The subject complained of pruritus and stated that the diphenhydramine was not helping. In addition, she reported difficulty breathing, and said she felt the need to cough to breathe better. She also reported flank pain and nausea. Upon examination, lung sounds were clear, but diminished. Vital signs showed blood pressure of 101/65 mmHg, pulse rate of 98 bpm, respirations of 18 rpm, temperature of 99.8°F, and oxygen saturation 96% on room air. The subject's ALT (238 U/L) and AST (187 U/L) were noted to be significantly increased from the early termination visit 6 days prior as shown in Table 1. Due to her worsening symptoms, the subject was referred to a local emergency department. At the emergency room she described of acute non-radiating back pain (right mid and right low back). She was alert and oriented and vital signs included blood pressures ranging from 91/58 to 98/62 mmHg, pulse rates of 93 to 104 bpm, temperature of 100.0°F, and oxygen saturation percentages of 96% to 98% on room air. A computed tomographic (CT) scan of the abdomen and pelvis was performed and showed a non-obstructing stone in the area of the right kidney and a complex ovarian cyst, with recommendation for a pelvic ultrasound. The subject was given intravenous normal saline 1000 mL, Solu-Medrol® 125 mg, and Benadryl® 25 mg. She was discharged with diagnoses of drug rash, ureterolithiasis - renal colic, and ovarian cyst. She was sent with prescriptions for ibuprofen, Norco, Zofran®, and prednisone 20 mg (with taper instructions), and was instructed to follow-up with her private physician in 2 to 3 days.
L1916: She returned to the clinic for follow-up visits on [REDACTED](b)(6) (Day 40) and [REDACTED](b)(6) (Day 41) and reported no improvement to the rash or pruritus, and continued nausea. At both visits, her ALT and AST continued to increase, along with several other serum liver function tests (alkaline phosphatase [ALP], direct bilirubin, gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT), and lactate dehydrogenase [LDH]). Additionally, several WBC differential parameters were out of range as shown in Table 1, Table 2, and Table 3. Vital signs showed blood pressures ranging from 92/62 to 107/69 mmHg, pulse rates of 82 to 123 bpm, respirations of 20 rpm, and temperatures of 97 to 100°F.
L1917: On [REDACTED](b)(6) (Day 42), she returned to the clinic for follow-up laboratory work. The erythematous macular rash was still present on her chest, abdomen, back, and legs and she continued to have nausea. She was then taken to a scheduled dermatology appointment. Her rash was noted to be located throughout her body, and was itchy and red. She stated she felt better after the prednisone, but the improvement did not last all day. A punch biopsy result was consistent with DRESS syndrome. Viral laboratory panel test results showed: Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) antibody panel presence of Immunoglobulin G (IgG) antibodies (1.46; reference range: 0 - 0.9), but no Immunoglobulin M (IgM) antibodies (0.00 reference range: 0 - 0.9); Herpes virus-6 deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) negative (0; reference range: 0 - 500 copies/mL) and herpes virus-6 IgG antibodies detected (1:20; reference range &lt; 1:10); Cytomegalovirus (CMV) antibody panel presence of IgG antibodies (2.29; reference range: 0 - 0.9) but no IgM antibodies (0.00; reference range: 0 - 0.8). Additionally, an aldolase value was elevated at 28.0 U/L (local laboratory reference range: 1.2 - 7.6 U/L). She was prescribed triamcinolone cream for the rash, the prednisone dosage was set at 20 mg, and additionally she was placed on calcium and vitamin D for bone health prophylaxis while taking the prednisone.
L1918: On [REDACTED](b)(6) (Day 43) she reported by telephone no improvement in her AEs and was instructed to return for a laboratory recheck and was advised to call the clinic or go to an emergency room if symptoms worsened. She returned to the clinic on [REDACTED](b)(6) (Day 44) and her rash was noticeably worse; also pronounced diffuse adenopathy was noted. She denied nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea, but generally felt ill. Her ALT (937 I/U) and AST (610 I/U) were continuing to increase. Coagulation parameters were obtained and showed an elevated prothrombin time (PT, 13.5 seconds; reference range: 9.6 - 11.6 seconds) and international normalized ratio (INR, 1.3; reference range: 0.9 - 1.1), and a partial thromboplastin time (PTT) within normal range (26 seconds; reference range: 23 - 32 seconds). Her blood pressure was 106/69 mmHg, pulse rate 96 bpm, respirations 20 rpm, and temperature 99.8°F. L1932: On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 87), she was found unresponsive by her husband and emergency medical services transported her to a local emergency room where she was pronounced dead. On 12 August 2015, a post-mortem examination concluded that she died from a cardiac dysrhythmia due to eosinophilic myocarditis due to DRESS, with findings of pleural effusions, pericardial effusion, pulmonary edema, cardiomyopathy, and necrotizing myocarditis.
L1933: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) a healthy 50-year-old Caucasian male enrolled in a Phase 1, single-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple ascending dose study. He had no relevant medical history or reported medications. On Day 14 [REDACTED](b) (6), approximately 5.4 hours after receiving the 4th dose of 400 mg YKP3089, he experienced warmth to the trunk and developed a diffuse erythematous rash to the trunk, arms, and legs (bilaterally). He was treated with oral diphenhydramine hydrochloride that day. The PI examined him the following morning and found a diffuse erythematous rash to trunk, arms and legs that was very red and pruritic. The subject denied any difficulty swallowing or breathing and dosing was continued. That evening he again received oral diphenhydramine for complaint of pruritus. Later that evening, he also complained of feeling weak. Over the next 6 days the rash showed little improvement and he complained of continued itching, which was treated with oral diphenhydramine 50 mg. On Study Day 22 [REDACTED](b) (6) the rash was noted to include the palms of the hand and soles of the feet. No stridor was noted, and there was no uvular or tongue swelling; the subject denied difficulty swallowing and was allowed to dose. Later that same day, the subject was noted to have soft facial tissue swelling as well as swelling of the digits bilaterally. No swelling of the lips or oral mucosa was noted. The PI discontinued dosing and kept the subject domiciled in the clinic for safety. The subject received a total of 5 doses each of 200, 300, and 400 mg YKP3089 and 7 doses of 500 mg YKP3089 before the study drug was discontinued on Day 22. On Day 23 [REDACTED](b) (6) in the morning, the subject had increased weakness; stating that it was difficult to get out of bed. The rash persisted, and the subject denied difficulty breathing or swallowing. That afternoon, he began to have swelling of the lips and bilateral eyelids, body aches, and a burning sensation in the areas covered by the rash. He received oral diphenhydramine, oral paracetamol, and topical hydrocortisone. Vital signs were normal with the exception of a pulse rate of 99 bpm at a single time point. That evening, his temperature increased to 103.8°F, accompanied by a pulse rate of 106 bpm. He was given paracetamol.
L1934: On Day 24 (b) (6) he had a temperature of 103.2°F, and increased heart rate of 106 bpm. He received oral diphenhydramine and paracetamol. The pruritic rash was still present. He had continued weakness with observed difficulty changing positions in bed. He reported feeling achy all over, with moderate pain to entire body. He also was noted to have generalized pustules over his face and increased swelling to the facial soft tissues. He was given intramuscular methylprednisolone sodium succinate.
L1935: Laboratory results included the following: sodium 122 mEq/L (reference range 133 - 145 mEq/L), chloride 88 mEq/L (reference range: 97 - 105 mEq/L), blood urea nitrogen (BUN) 28 mg/dL (reference range: 6 - 20 mg/dL), creatinine 1.70 mg/dL (reference range: 0.8 - 1.3 mg/dL), bicarbonate 21 mmol/L (reference range 24.2 - 33.6 mmol/L), ALT 517 U/L (reference range 11 - 65 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (AST) 173 U/L (reference range: 15 - 46 U/L), GGT 255 U/L (reference range 7 - 62 U/L), white blood cells (WBCs) 13.8 Thou/\muL (reference range: 3.4 - 10.5 Thou/\muL), lymphocytes 8.6% (reference range: 16.8 - 51.7%), neutrophils 84.0% (reference range: 40.2 - 75.0%), and absolute neutrophil count (ANC) 11.6 Thou/\muL (reference range: 1.5 - 7.0 Thou/L). Glomerular filtration rate (GFR), while still within normal limits at 66 mL/min (reference range:&gt; 60 mL/min), was significantly lower than at baseline (125 mL/min). The decision was made to transfer the subject via ambulance to the local emergency room.
L1936: At the hospital, initial blood work was consistent with the findings at the site listed above. The ER also reported the following: albumin 2.5 g/dL, calcium, 7.8 mg/dL, and INR 1.41. In addition, 2 blood cultures revealed S. Epidermidis. Treatment with Vancomycin 1 g QD was initiated along with normal saline, heparin, and Solu-Medrol® 120 mg /day.
L1937: An infectious disease physician felt the culture results represented contaminant based on the lack of corroborating symptoms and discontinued the Vancomycin. The infectious disease physician believed the diffuse maculopapular rash was likely a drug eruption secondary to an antiepileptic drug. Within 24 hours, the patient showed signs of clinical improvement with less fever, feeling stronger and some resolution of the rash. Renal function improved, WBC counts reduced with normalization of granulocytes and calcium increased. Hepatitis panel for A, B and C was negative. However, on (b) (6) AST increased to 568 U/L, ALT increased to 809 U/L and alkaline phosphatase increased to 133 U/L. On (b) (6) a dermatologist reported a diffuse dusky discoloration of skin involving hands and feet plus areas of fine scaling and palpable purpura extensive on legs. His first impression was drug hypersensitivity syndrome including systemic symptoms and leukocytoclastic vasculitis. A CBC with differential ordered by the dermatologist revealed eosinophils of 11% with a total WBC of 7.1 Thou/\muL. Based on the elevated eosinophil count, the dermatologist believes the condition of the subject can best be characterized as a DRESS syndrome. By (b) (6) the rash was resolving and the liver and kidney functions tests were returning towards normal. By discharge from the hospital, the sodium, chloride, BUN, creatinine, bicarbonate, and WBC count were within normal limits. The ALT and AST were still elevated but trending downwards. Hemoglobin level was still low at 11.5 g/dL (reference range 12.6 - 16.8 g/dL) as was albumin at 2.7 g/dL (reference range 4 - 5.2 g/dL). The subject was alert and oriented, in stable condition, and his symptoms had significantly improved. The subject was discharged from the hospital on (b) (6) and returned to the clinical site. The subject continued to follow
L1938: up with the clinic until resolution of his symptoms. Labs drawn on [REDACTED](b)(6) showed that the international normalized ratio (INR), which was elevated during hospitalization to 1.41 (reference range: 0.9 - 1.1) and AST were now within normal limits and the GFR had improved back to baseline at 113 mL/min. Safety labs drawn on [REDACTED](b)(6) showed that hemoglobin and albumin were within normal limits and that GGT and ALT were only slightly elevated. The symptoms of itching and rash continued to improve per follow-up phone calls by the subject. The SAE was considered resolved on [REDACTED](b)(6)

L1943: Box plot illustrating the Percent Change from Baseline (CFB) Monthly Seizure Frequency by Arm (Placebo vs. 200 mg Cenobamate) and By Time Period (Baseline, Titration, Maintenance) in Phase 2 Study C013.
The Y-axis represents % CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency, ranging from -100 to 150. The X-axis shows four time periods: Baseline (Day -56 to -28), Titration (Day 1 to 29), Titration (Day 29 to 57), and Maintenance (Day 57 to 85).
The legend indicates two arms: Placebo (Blue) and 200 mg Cenobamate (Green).
Baseline (Day -56 to -28): Both Placebo and 200 mg Cenobamate groups show a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency near 0, with interquartile ranges spanning approximately -50 to 50.
Titration (Day 1 to 29): The Placebo group shows a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency around -20. The 200 mg Cenobamate group shows a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency around -30. Both groups show similar interquartile ranges (approximately -50 to 50).
Titration (Day 29 to 57): The Placebo group shows a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency around -20. The 200 mg Cenobamate group shows a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency around -30. Both groups show similar interquartile ranges (approximately -50 to 50).
Maintenance (Day 57 to 85): The Placebo group shows a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency around -20. The 200 mg Cenobamate group shows a median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency around -30. Both groups show similar interquartile ranges (approximately -50 to 50).
Overall, the 200 mg Cenobamate group consistently shows a lower median CFB Monthly Seizure Frequency (indicating greater reduction in seizure frequency) compared to the Placebo group across all time periods, suggesting a dose response effect.

The Y-axis represents the Percent Change from Baseline (ranging from -100 to 150), and the X-axis represents time periods: Baseline (Week -8 to 0), Titration (Week 2 to 6), Maintenance (Week 6 to 10), Maintenance (Week 10 to 14), Maintenance (Week 14 to 18), and Conv to OLE (Week 18 to 22). The legend identifies four treatment arms: Placebo (Blue), 100 mg Cenobamate (Red), 200 mg Cenobamate (Green), and 400 mg Cenobamate (Yellow).
The Baseline period shows a median percent change near 0% for all arms. During the Titration period, the Placebo arm shows a median decrease of approximately -10%. The active treatment arms show median decreases of approximately -15% (100 mg), -20% (200 mg), and -25% (400 mg). During the Maintenance periods (Weeks 6 to 10, 10 to 14, and 14 to 18), the Placebo arm maintains a median decrease of approximately -10%. The active treatment arms show median decreases of approximately -15% (100 mg), -20% (200 mg), and -25% (400 mg). In the Conv to OLE period (Weeks 18 to 22), the Placebo arm shows a median decrease of approximately -10%. The 100 mg Cenobamate arm shows a median decrease of approximately -30%. The 200 mg Cenobamate arm shows a median decrease of approximately -20%. The 400 mg Cenobamate arm shows a median decrease of approximately -25%. Error bars (T) are included for all data points.
L1945: Figure 4 is a line graph showing the Predicted Mean Plasma Concentration (ng/mL) on the Y-axis versus Time after First Dose (Weeks) on the X-axis (0 to 20 weeks). The graph compares two titration regimens: Phase 2 titration (blue line) and Label titration (red line).
The Phase 2 titration regimen starts at 12.5 mg/day and increases weekly to 400 mg/day by week 6. The Label titration regimen starts at 25 mg/day and increases weekly to 400 mg/day by week 18. Both regimens show a steady increase in plasma concentration over time, reaching a plateau around 35 ng/mL.

L1946: Figure 5 is a scatter plot showing the predicted change from baseline 28-day seizure count over time (Weeks) for two titration regimens: Label titration (red squares) and Phase 2 titration (blue diamonds).
The X-axis represents Time after First Dose (Weeks), ranging from 0 to 20. The Y-axis represents % Change From Baseline 28-day Seizure Count, ranging from 0 to -100.
Both regimens show a rapid decrease in seizure count in the first few weeks, stabilizing around -60% to -70% change from baseline after approximately 10 weeks. The Phase 2 titration generally shows a slightly lower percentage change from baseline compared to the Label titration in the initial weeks, but both regimens converge towards similar long-term outcomes.
